The kind's fields follow, then `}`. `at` is the signer's own clock, used only as described in §3.3.

| `kind` | Extra fields, in order | Signed by |
|---|---|---|
| `genesis` | `"ownerKey":<b64_32>,"vkCommit":<hex64>` | the creator (`actor`) |
| `add` | `"member":<uuid>,"memberKey":<b64_32>,"role":<role>,"grantId":<b64_16>` | owner |
| `role` | `"member":<uuid>,"role":<role>` | owner |
| `remove` | `"member":<uuid>` | owner |
| `leave` | — | the member leaving (`actor`) |
| `offer` | `"to":<uuid>,"toKey":<b64_32>,"offerId":<b64_16>,"expiresAt":<ms>` | owner |
| `cancel` | `"offerId":<b64_16>` | owner or the offer's target |
| `accept` | `"offerId":<b64_16>` | the offer's target |
| `delete` | `"deleteId":<b64_16>` | owner |
| `restore` | `"deleteId":<b64_16>` | owner |

Example genesis statement (one line, shown wrapped):

```
{"v":2,"purpose":"vault-log","vaultId":"…","seq":0,"prev":"000…000","kind":"genesis",
 "actor":"…","at":1763164800000,"ownerKey":"…43 chars…","vkCommit":"…64 hex…"}
```

### 3.2 Chain

- **Template rules (VR-1):** a `genesis` has `seq = 0` and `prev` = 64 `0` characters, and every other kind has `seq >= 1`. A violation is `bad_statement`, at the parse. Position is then checked by `bad_seq` and `bad_prev` alone: a valid `genesis` frame placed later in a chain is `bad_seq`, and so is a first entry whose `seq` is not 0.
- Every later entry has `seq = previous.seq + 1` and `prev = H(previous frame)`.
- `vaultId` is constant along the chain.
- The **head** is `(seq, H(frame))` of the last entry.

### 3.3 State and transitions

Replaying entries 0..n yields a **vault state**:

```
S = { vaultId, head, owner, ownerKey, vkCommit,
      members: userId -> { key, role ∈ {owner, writer, reader} },
      pending: { offerId, to, toKey, expiresAt } | none,
      deleted: { deleteId } | none }
```

`genesis` sets `owner = actor`, `ownerKey`, `vkCommit`, `members = { actor: { ownerKey, owner } }`, `pending = none` and `deleted = none`. Each later entry must satisfy **every** condition in its row, checked against S before the entry. The first failure is the verdict.

| kind | Signer key must be | Preconditions | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| `genesis` | its own `ownerKey`: it signs itself (VR-2) | first entry only (the template rules of §3.2) | sets up S, as the paragraph above says |
| `add` | `S.ownerKey`, with `actor = S.owner` | `deleted = none`; `member ∉ members` | `members[member] = {memberKey, role}` |
| `role` | `S.ownerKey`, with `actor = S.owner` | `deleted = none`; `member ∈ members`, `member ≠ owner` | role replaced; if `pending.to = member`, then `pending = none` |
| `remove` | `S.ownerKey`, with `actor = S.owner` | `deleted = none`; `member ∈ members`, `member ≠ owner` | member deleted; if `pending.to = member`, then `pending = none` |
| `leave` | `members[actor].key` | `actor ∈ members`, `actor ≠ owner`. Allowed **while the vault is deleted**, so a member of a vault in its grace period can still leave and delete their account (B2). | actor deleted; if `pending.to = actor`, then `pending = none`. A member who leaves during the grace period stays out if the vault is restored. |
| `offer` | `S.ownerKey`, with `actor = S.owner` | `deleted = none`; `to ∈ members`, `to ≠ owner`; `toKey = members[to].key`; `at < expiresAt <= at + 14 d + 10 min`; `pending = none`, or `pending.expiresAt < at` | `pending = {offerId, to, toKey, expiresAt}` |
| `cancel` | `S.ownerKey` (actor owner), or `pending.toKey` (actor `pending.to`) | `pending ≠ none`; `offerId = pending.offerId` | `pending = none` |
| `accept` | `pending.toKey`, with `actor = pending.to` | `deleted = none`; `pending ≠ none`; `offerId = pending.offerId`; `at <= pending.expiresAt` | the old owner's role becomes `writer`; `owner = pending.to`, `ownerKey = pending.toKey`, the new owner's role is `owner`; `pending = none` |
| `delete` | `S.ownerKey`, with `actor = S.owner` | `deleted = none` | `deleted = {deleteId}`; `pending = none` |
| `restore` | `S.ownerKey`, with `actor = S.owner` | `deleted.deleteId = deleteId` | `deleted = none` |

**Time rules (T-R1).**
- `at` never decreases along a log: every entry needs `at >= previous.at`, or it fails as `precondition`. A signer sets `at = max(own clock, head.at)`, so a device with a slow clock never deadlocks the vault.
- Before applying any entry whose `at > pending.expiresAt`, the verifier **clears the expired offer** (`pending = none`). So once the owner signs anything after the expiry, a late `accept` is impossible.
- `accept` additionally needs `at <= pending.expiresAt`. A target who backdates can only reach back to `head.at`, and the server refuses an accept after expiry by its own clock.
- **Ceiling (T2-R-a).** The server refuses any entry whose `at` is more than **10 minutes ahead of its own clock** (`400 at_ahead`, §3.7).
  - Without it, a departing member could sign `leave` with `at = 2^53 − 1`. Every later entry would have to carry that `at`, so no `offer` could ever satisfy `at < expiresAt` again: ownership would be frozen. Leaves built for an account deletion at the device's own clock would fail too.
  - The check is the server's, **outside** the shared verifier. `VaultLog.verify` stays clock-free, so every device reaches the same verdict on the same bytes, whatever its clock says.
  - A hostile server can still store a far-future `at` and so freeze offers on that vault. That is denial of service, which T1 can do anyway (R20).
  - A refused frame was never stored, so the device discards it. A device whose clock is ahead says "Your device's clock is ahead. Set the correct time and try again." It never adopts a time the server suggests.

All signer keys come from the log itself: `ownerKey` from genesis or the last `accept`, member keys from `add`, and the target key from `offer`. Verifying a log therefore needs no contact pins. Whether a device *adopts* a vault is a separate decision (§3.6).

**Verdicts**, in check order:
1. `bad_frame`, `unknown_format`, `non_canonical`, `bad_statement` (the §2 parse);
2. `wrong_vault`;
3. `bad_seq`;
4. `bad_prev`;
5. `not_authorized` (the signer key named by the table is not the one in the frame, or `actor` is not the required account);
6. `bad_signature`;
7. `precondition` (a row condition failed);
8. `fork` (a different valid entry at a sequence number this device already saw).

**Undefined signer keys and expired offers (FI-B1).**
- Expired-offer clearing (the time rules above) is applied to S **before** step 5.
- At step 5, if the key the row names does not exist in S, the verdict is `precondition` and checking stops. The cases: `accept`, or a `cancel` by the target, with `pending = none`; `leave` by an actor who is not in `members`.
- So `accept` after expiry is always `precondition`: either the clearing removed the offer, or `at <= pending.expiresAt` fails.

### 3.4 Key commitment

```
vkCommit = hexLower(HKDF-SHA-256(ikm = VK, salt = empty, info = "andvari/v2|vk-commit|" + vaultId, L = 32))
```

Every vault key a client obtains, from any source, is checked against `S.vkCommit` before use: the creator's own wrap, a member self-wrap, a new owner's wrap, or a grant. A mismatch means the key is **refused**: the vault is "key doesn't match this vault's history — the server may be misbehaving", nothing is decrypted or written under that key, and the anomaly is shown. This replaces held-key memory, vouches and `vk_mismatch` (all deleted, §15).

### 3.5 Grants (purpose 0x01, binding amended)

The grant template is unchanged:

```
{"v":2,"purpose":"vault-grant","from":…,"to":…,"toKey":…,"vaultId":…,"grantId":…,"issuedAt":…,"vk":…}
```

It is sealed with `crypto_box_seal` to `memberKey` and delivered on the member's grant row. A recipient runs these checks in order; the first failure is the verdict:

1. The seal opens (`unopenable`).
2. The frame checks of §2.4, for purpose 0x01: `unknown_format` for another magic (a bare JSON grant included), `bad_frame` for a plaintext that is too short or a frame of another purpose.
3. Strict parse (`non_canonical`, `bad_statement`).
4. `to` is this account and `toKey` is its seed-derived key (`misaddressed`).
5. `vaultId` is the row's vault (`vault_mismatch`).
6. The vault's log verifies (§3.9), and **this account's latest `add` entry** has `grantId` equal to the statement's, `member` equal to this account, and `memberKey` equal to its key. Call the actor of that entry O, and the owner key at that entry K. A grant bound to an earlier `add` of the account, or to none, is `no_add_entry`. If the log itself does not verify, the grant is not evaluated at all: the log is replayed before any grant is opened, so the result is the log's own verdict even when the grant has a fault of its own.
7. `from = O`, and the signer maps to K (`not_authorized`).
8. Signature (`bad_signature`).
9. `vk` is 32 bytes and `commit(vk) = S.vkCommit` (`vk_commit_mismatch`).
10. No later `remove` or `leave` for this account without a later `add` (`removed`). After a remove and a re-add only the new `add`'s grant opens (step 6). Otherwise a server could put an old grant back on the row and the accept prompt would name whoever owned the vault at the earlier `add`, a person who may no longer be its owner, instead of the person who made the latest one.

This closes R15(f): a pinned non-owner can no longer grant. It also closes R15(g): no fresh device can be pushed onto a different key, because the key is committed at genesis.

### 3.6 Adopting a vault on a device

A device starts using a shared vault's key when **all** of these hold:

- (a) the log verifies;
- (b) this account is in `S.members`;
- (c) the key passes §3.4;
- (d) one of the following:
  - this account is the genesis owner, the log has **no `add` of this account**, and the key comes from its own owner wrap (FN-2). A genesis owner who left and was added again is asked like anyone else (next bullet);
  - the key comes from this account's own **self-wrap** or **owner wrap**, which **opens with the AD that names this log's genesis hash** (`andvari/v2|vk-shared|{vaultId}|{userId}|{genesisHash}`, §2.3, T-B3), **and** an `accepted` record with `decision = accept` answers this account's latest `add` (its `addSeq`, §5.4) (FC-7). So a server that replays an old self-wrap cannot make a device adopt a vault the person was re-added to without asking;
  - the key comes from a §3.5 grant, **and** the `add` entry's actor is a contact that is **current on this device** (§6.9) with the same key, **and** the person accepted the vault (below).
- (e) the account's **vault record** in account state agrees (T-B3).
  - The record is `vaults[vaultId] = {genesisHash, seq, hash}` in UVK-sealed account state, written by a device when it adopts the vault or records the person's answer to it, never merely because the server listed a log, and updated as the verified head advances (§5.4). Otherwise a server alone, or a contact creating and deleting vaults, could fill the document.
  - A served log whose genesis hash differs is refused. So is a served head below the record's `seq`, or a different hash at that `seq`.
  - **No record yet** (T2-R-d): (e) holds, and the device writes the record once the log verifies. A record never makes a log pass; it can only refuse one. The merge rules are §5.4.
  - Any holder of the account's UVK can write a record, including a wrong one that makes every device of the account refuse or freeze that vault. That is residual R35.

**Why (d) and (e) bind the genesis.** The commitment `vkCommit` is public, and a wrap used to bind only the key, the vault and the user. Without the genesis hash, the server alone could serve a fresh device a fabricated log: its own genesis with the copied commitment, then `add` for the victim. The wrap AD now names the genesis the wrapping device verified, and the account's own devices record it, so a different genesis fails both.

**Accepting an incoming vault (T-R4).** A vault reaching this account through a grant is **never adopted unasked**, even from a current contact. The account sees "*Contact label* wants to share “*vault name*” with you" [Accept] [Decline].
- The vault name is decrypted only after (a)–(c) pass, and is shown as plain text.
- Accept is recorded once per account in account state, as `accepted[vaultId] = {owner, addSeq, at, decision}` (§5.4), so the account's other devices do not ask again. `addSeq` is the `seq` of the `add` entry the person answered.
- **A record answers only that `add` (FS-R6).** After a `leave` or `remove`, any new `add` of this account has a higher `seq`, so the old record no longer applies and the person is asked again. A former owner's re-add is never adopted unasked (T-R4).
- Decline hides the vault on every device of the account **and signs a `leave`** (the account is a member once added), so the owner sees the decline and the server revokes the grant.
- This replaces Q5's automatic adoption.
- **The server also refuses an `add`** unless the owner and the new member are **connected** (`409 not_connected`; X1). The server keeps a durable `connections(a, b)` row, `a < b`, written when a connect link is answered **or** an account registers through a register link. The janitor never touches it; it goes only when either account is deleted. It is hygiene against strangers pushing vaults on an honest server; the accept prompt is the control against a hostile one.

**Otherwise** the vault is listed as "*Name as the server reports it* wants to share a vault with you. Connect with them first." The vault's name is not shown, and nothing is decrypted.

**Vaults left out of the AutoFill snapshot and the extension.** A vault not yet accepted, frozen or failed is never written into the iOS AutoFill snapshot, which the Safari extension also reads. A vault that later freezes is removed from the snapshot at the next refresh. The Chrome and Firefox extension opens wraps itself, so it applies §12.2 rule 1's acceptance check instead; it cannot see a freeze or a stale `addSeq` (R24, FN-1).

**A frozen or failed vault** (§3.9) is read-only on every path that writes under its key: item saves and edits, restore from trash, moving items in or out, copying items in, draining the iOS save queue into it, renames, and every log entry except `leave`.
- **Copying items out stays allowed** (T2-R-k). It writes only under the destination vault's key, and it is the way out of a frozen vault (§3.13).
- The copy dialog says: "Items in this vault may have been changed by someone the server let in. Check what you copy."

**After adoption.** A device that adopted a key from a grant posts the self-wrap (`PUT /vaults/{id}/grant/self-wrap`, kept from today, now with the genesis-bound AD). The account's other devices then adopt through the wrap without needing current contacts. Once a device has adopted a vault, later log entries are judged by §3.3 alone, and the device keeps the account's vault record current.

### 3.7 Append (server)

**Route:**

```
POST /api/v1/vaults/{vaultId}/log
{ "entry": <b64 frame>, "grant"?: <b64 sealed 0x01>, "wrappedVk"?: <b64 envelope>, "metaBlob"?: <b64 envelope> }
```

This route replaces `POST /vaults`, member add, member remove, role change, leave, transfer offer, cancel, accept, delete and restore. Required side data:
- `genesis`: `metaBlob` (≤ 4 KiB) and `wrappedVk` (the creator's owner wrap under the genesis-bound AD, §2.3; ≤ 1 KiB);
- `add`: `grant` (≤ 2100 chars);
- `accept`: `wrappedVk` (the new owner's owner wrap under the genesis-bound AD; the client round-trip-verifies it first).

Every other kind takes nothing else.

**The server checks, in order** (FI-B2, FI-R11):
1. The session.
2. The body caps.
3. The strict parse of the frame and purpose 0x03, and the frame's `vaultId` against the path.
4. `entry.actor` is the caller, and the signer maps to the caller's stored `identityPub`; the Ed25519 signature verifies. This is hygiene; clients never rely on the server having checked.
5. **Retry, then access.**
   - A frame byte-identical to one already stored, with `actor` = the caller (step 4), answers `200` and changes nothing, **before** any grant check (FC-1). So the retry of a stored `leave`, a decline included, still gets its `200` although that entry's own effect revoked the caller's grant.
   - `genesis`: no vault and no grant exist yet, so there is nothing to resolve; the vault id must be unused (`409 vault_id_taken`).
   - Every other kind: the caller's grant is resolved first (`403 no_grant`, also for an unknown vault, so a non-member learns nothing). A grant revoked with reason `vault_delete` counts as the caller's grant for `restore` and `delete` by the owner, and for `leave` (N13, FC-1), so the owner can restore a vault in its grace period and a member can leave it. After the purge every non-genesis append answers `403 no_grant`, the owner's `restore` included; a byte-identical retry of a stored frame still answers `200`. Then the caller's server-side role must allow the kind (`403 not_vault_owner`).
6. **Position.** `seq` and `prev` must equal the stored head plus one, or `seq = 0` for `genesis`; if not, `409 log_head_moved {seq, hash, at}`. This comes after step 5, so the head is never shown to a non-member.
7. **The shared core verifier** runs (T-R2): the same `:core` code every client uses (`VaultLog.verify`) checks the stored chain plus the new entry. Its verdicts reach the wire as the table below says. The server never keeps a second implementation of §3.3 that could accept an entry every client rejects.
8. Kind-specific checks:
   - every kind: `at` is at most 10 minutes ahead of the server's clock (`400 at_ahead`, §3.3, T2-R-a);
   - `add`: `member` must be an active account with fewer than 500 shared-vault memberships (`409 member_limit`), and a `connections` row must exist for the pair (`409 not_connected`, §3.6, X1);
   - `genesis`: the account must be under its owned-shared-vault and storage quotas (`413 quota_exceeded`);
   - `offer`: by the server's clock, `now < expiresAt <= now + 14 d + 10 min` (`400 bad_expiry`, FI-R11);
   - `accept`: the server refuses after `expiresAt` by its own clock (`409 offer_expired`). The new owner must be under its quotas once the vault's bytes count against it.

Then, in **one transaction**, it applies the effect to its tables (grants, roles, pending offer, deletion state with the existing grace and purge rules), stores the entry, bumps the vault rev and rings the bell for every affected member, the removed included.

**Idempotency and retries (T-R2).**
- A byte-identical frame already stored, signed by the caller, answers `200` and changes nothing, before the grant check (step 5, FC-1).
- A client keeps every frame it signed in a durable **pending append** record until the server acknowledges it.
- After a timeout it re-posts **the same bytes**, and never re-signs with a new `at`. Re-signing would hand the server two valid entries for one position.
- Only after `409 log_head_moved` **and** a fetch that shows its frame was not stored does it discard the pending frame and sign a fresh entry at the new head.
- **While a frame is pending for a vault, the device signs nothing else for that vault.** A new action first re-posts the pending frame. If that is acknowledged, the action goes on from the new head; if it is still unanswered, the action is refused (`append_pending`) and the app says the last change to this vault has not reached the server yet. Nothing is queued behind a pending frame, and the pending record is never overwritten: a device that signed a second entry for the same position would itself hand the server the two entries of a fork (found by the hostile-server harness).
- **One exception: the old vault's delete in "Move everything" (§3.13, decided 2026-11-16).** Only the move re-posts it. The re-posting of held changes after an unlock or a reconnect leaves it alone, and a new action on that vault re-posts nothing: it is refused (`append_pending`), and the app says that a move is unfinished and offers to finish it. While that delete waits the old vault is read-only on the device, and the move re-posts the delete through a call that signs nothing (§3.13). Both clients keep this (§3.13, "What the code keeps").
- **What the person is told after a change of a vault's log, and what waits** (rules of 2026-11-18; spec 03 §10.3 has each point). Stored only where the server's own answer names the frame that was sent, or where a later read holds it; an answer of success whose head lies at or beyond the change's position is the server's word that it holds the change, so "not made" is never said after one, and a different entry at that place is a fork that freezes the vault (rule of 2026-11-19 for every client; an honest server answers success only for a frame it then holds at its position; it replaces the web client's earlier "not made" after a read that reached the named head with the same hash); an answer below the position is no answer about it; otherwise "cannot tell", held and sent again. After "cannot tell" on a change of members the member list is read from the server before the person is sent to it. A held change is shown as waiting where the vault's members are shown, can be withdrawn (with words that differ by what is known), and is given up after a day; a vault's deletion that waits is sent again only at the person's own word. A change sent again that does not happen is said with its vault and its member in a notice that is kept until it is dismissed, and a change given up, withdrawn or called not made is remembered by its hash and position while that position is free, so that a later append of it is said with the way to undo it. A deletion that waits is first looked for in the vault's log, with nothing sent, before the person is asked about it, and a change the server's own answer called stored is never offered for withdrawal: it is made, and the way to undo it is the act that undoes it (rules of 2026-11-19). The apps keep the same rules as the web client. **Kept (2026-11-18):** the web client keeps the outcomes, the fork, the read of the members, the waiting additions and removals with their day, and the kept notice; **kept (2026-11-19):** the shared code keeps a change the server asked it to slow down for, where no earlier send could have been, as known not stored (said as kept, sent again, withdrawable until then), and never again as "not stored" after a send whose outcome is not known; **kept (2026-11-19), in the web client:** every rule of this bullet, the withdrawal whether or not the answer was lost, the waiting change of role, hand-over and deletion, the deletion looked for in the log and then sent only at the person's word, the remembering by hash and position (bounded at eight for a vault), a give-up judged once its read has ended, a fork found where an answer had named an entry, a change that brings back an item deleted elsewhere said once with no copy made, and no day of a restore unless the server served it; the shared code's engine keeps its part of these rules since 2026-11-19, and the vault content makes no conflict copy for a change that brings back an item deleted elsewhere and says it once per vault (kept in memory only); the shared code's layer marks a move's own entries and names the day of a restore only where the server served one since 2026-11-19; **not kept yet (2026-11-19):** the account's deletion by the log engine's own call in the shared code's layer, and these rules on the apps' screens (spec 03 §10.3 lists them). **The fork freezes the vault at the read that shows it** (the rule, kept since 2026-11-19 in the shared code and in the web client): where the server's own answer called a held change stored at its place (any success at or beyond that place) and a complete verified read of the vault's log then holds another entry there, the vault freezes at that read, with nothing else called; the web client also freezes when the answer arrives against a log it has already verified. At the freeze the held change is dropped and nothing of it is sent, shown, asked about, given up or counted, and a finding that its place was taken is taken back; a look, a giving-up and an append whose own read dropped the change answer that the vault is frozen; an answer below the change's place stays no answer. The web client keeps the server's word through a refused re-send, a dropped deletion of a half-made vault and a withdrawal made while the answer comes in, and an answer at the change's own place with another hash is the server's word there. A move whose old vault froze while the move's own deletion of it waited stops at the move's own first read (and again before it signs the deletion), says that the old vault is frozen with the line for that, sends nothing, keeps the move's mark and offers no fresh start; every later run says the same; the old vault stays read-only, and on the web the new vault is never treated as half-made. A move out of a vault that was frozen before its deletion was sent is the ordinary move whose source stays (§3.13) and is not stopped. **The person can close such a move by their own word** (rule of 2026-11-19): the close removes this device's record of the unfinished move and its mark of a waiting deletion (the freeze already dropped that deletion), and nothing more: it sends nothing, changes nothing in either vault, does not touch the freeze or the log's records, and the old vault stays frozen and read-only here; it says that the copied items are in the new vault (named, with their number), that the old vault still exists at its server with what it held, and that on this device it stays frozen; a later "Move everything" out of it is the ordinary move whose source stays. **Not kept yet (2026-11-19):** no such close exists in the shared code, so every run of such a move stops on the frozen line for good. A refusal that comes from a freeze says a line of its own that claims nothing about a change that was sent. A read from which the server withholds entries can verify the other entry's hash without the check; the vault is read-only while withheld, and the freeze comes at the next complete read (a limit, spec 05 R45). **A restore of the server's database from a backup** loses entries the server had acknowledged; where another member then writes at such a place, each device that holds the acknowledged entry freezes that vault by this same rule, and it stays read-only there until the device's records of it are forgotten; the operator's guide says so before a restore (§9.6).

**Storage:** `vault_log(vaultId, seq, hash, frame, actor, kind, at, storedAt)`, primary key `(vaultId, seq)`. Log rows outlive the vault's purge: they are deleted with its tombstone, one year later (§3.8), or at the purge itself for a vault no other account could still fetch (§3.11) (FX-1).

**Errors (FI-B2).** Every client verdict and every server check maps to exactly one wire answer. `not_authorized` and `fork` are client verdicts and never appear on the wire.

| Client verdict (§3.3), or server check | Wire |
|---|---|
| `bad_frame`, `unknown_format` | `400 bad_frame` |
| `non_canonical` | `400 non_canonical` |
| `bad_statement` | `400 bad_statement` |
| `wrong_vault` (frame against path) | `400 wrong_vault` |
| step 4: the actor or the signer is not the caller | `400 bad_signer` |
| `bad_signature` | `400 bad_signature` |
| step 5: no grant (or no such vault); a role that does not allow the kind | `403 no_grant`; `403 not_vault_owner` |
| step 5: `genesis` with a vault id in use | `409 vault_id_taken` |
| `bad_seq`, `bad_prev` (not the stored head plus one) | `409 log_head_moved {seq, hash, at}` |
| `not_authorized` from the verifier (the log names another signer for this kind) | `403 not_vault_owner` |
| `precondition` | `400 precondition` |
| `fork` | never sent: the server stores one chain, so another entry at a stored position is `409 log_head_moved` |
| `at` too far ahead | `400 at_ahead` |
| an offer's `expiresAt` outside the server's window | `400 bad_expiry` |
| `add` limits | `409 member_limit`, `409 not_connected` |
| accept after expiry | `409 offer_expired` |
| quotas | `413 quota_exceeded` |
| rate buckets | `429 rate_limited` |

**Rate buckets** (per device, with a ceiling per account of four times the device's limit, as every bucket of a route that needs a session, §7.8):

| Bucket | Kinds | Limit |
|---|---|---|
| `vault_create` | `genesis` | 10 per hour. `andvari-cli restore` paces itself on `Retry-After` (§14.1). |
| `vault_destructive` | `offer`, `delete`, `remove` | 10 per hour, as today |
| `vault_membership` | `add`, `role` | 60 per hour |
| `vault_recovery` | `restore`, `cancel`, `accept`, `leave` | 30 per hour |

Leaves appended by an account deletion are exempt from every bucket (§3.11).

### 3.8 Delivery and fetch

- Every vault row in `/sync` carries `logHead: { seq, hash }`.
- `GET /api/v1/vaults/{vaultId}/log?after=<seq>` returns `{ entries: [<b64 frame>…], head: {seq, hash, at}, purged?: {purgedAt} }`, at most 500 entries per page. `head.at` lets a device that builds an entry without replaying the log still keep `at` monotonic (T2-R-a).
  - **Paging (FI-B3).** Entries come in ascending order, each with `seq > after`. Without `after`, the page starts at `genesis` (seq 0), which is how a fresh device begins. A client repeats with `after` set to the last `seq` it received until it has reached `head.seq`.
- An active member gets everything.
- **Delete and restore (B3).** A `delete` revokes every grant (reason `vault_delete`) but removes nobody from the log's members. While the vault is deleted, every member of `S.members` keeps fetch access to the whole log, so each can verify the delete, a later `leave`, and the `restore`. A `restore` re-grants every grant **still** revoked with reason `vault_delete`, so members return with full access and need no new `add`.
  - **A leave during the grace period (FI-R7).** Appending it rewrites that member's revocation reason from `vault_delete` to `left`, in the same transaction, so the restore skips it and the member stays out, as §3.3 says. This amends today's rule, which re-grants every `vault_delete` revocation and therefore has to refuse a leave on a deleted vault.
- **Remove and leave.** An account cut off by `remove` or `leave` gets entries up to and including that entry, and nothing after. A later `add` for it restores full access.
- Anyone else gets `403 no_grant`.
- **Purge versus withholding (T2-R-h).** When a deleted vault's grace ends, the server deletes its items and attachments and blanks its grant rows, which stay so that the former members can still fetch the log. It keeps the vault row as a **tombstone** `{finalHead, purgedAt}` **together with the vault's log rows**, for one year after the purge. Every account in the log's `members` at the `delete` keeps fetch access to that log, as during the grace (B3).
  - A device shows "deleted for good" only after it has fetched and verified the log through `finalHead` (§3.9), and the verified state is **deleted**: the owner's signed `delete` with no later `restore`. The server alone cannot fake the owner's `delete`. It can withhold a later `restore` and purge anyway, which is why the device's copy goes to the holding area rather than being dropped (R20, FS-C1).
  - Everything else is **withholding** (§3.9): a tombstone whose log does not verify, does not end deleted, or ends below this device's verified head; and a vault that vanishes without a tombstone, including one whose tombstone year has passed.
  - **The device's own copy** (rule of 2026-11-16). When a device has verified that a vault is deleted for good, its own copy moves to the holding area (§3.12) and stays there for 30 days from that moment, or until the person removes it, and items can still be copied out of it; it never goes straight to deletion. The purge is the moment the copy goes into holding, never the moment it goes. This has no exception for the vault's owner (rule of 2026-11-17): a vault the owner deleted goes into the holding area like any other verified deletion, without the notice a deletion by someone else gets. The shared code does this, and so does the web client: it keeps a deleted vault's copy for 30 days from the verification, the owner's own deletion included (its line in "Recently removed" says the person deleted it, and the owner's restore raises no notice either), names the day the copy leaves, and offers "Remove from this device". On withholding the copy stays, read-only, with copy-out and "Remove from this device" (a local action that tells the server nothing). So the server can never make a device drop its offline copy by claiming a purge.
  - The year of kept log rows is membership metadata the server already held (R25).
- **Account deletion** purges the vaults the account owned with no other members (§3.11). A vault no other account can still fetch keeps no tombstone log: a former member was cut off at its removal, and the deleted account's devices are signed out. A vault in its grace period whose log still names other members keeps its tombstone and log for the year, exactly like the janitor's purge, so those members can verify the owner's `delete` (S3-C5).

### 3.9 Client verification

A device keeps, per shared vault, durably and outliving the offline cache:
- the verified head;
- the derived state S;
- the hash of every sequence number it has seen (for fork detection).

On each sync where `logHead` differs from the verified head, it fetches `after = verified.seq` and verifies each entry in order (§3.3).

- **A failure** leaves the verified state at the last good entry. The device raises "This shared vault's history doesn't check out — the server may be misbehaving." It keeps reading data already decrypted under a committed key, and refuses writes to the vault until the log verifies again: the state ends at the first later fetch that verifies from the last good entry, which includes a server that simply serves the verified head again with nothing beyond it.
- **A served head below the verified head**, or a log missing entries this device verified, is **withholding**. The device keeps its own state and shows "The server is showing an older version of this vault's history." Withholding ends as soon as the served log reaches the verified head again (the same hash at the same position); a stale cache is the benign cause, and one stale answer must not leave the vault read-only until someone else appends. The device therefore re-checks at every sync while a vault is withheld or failed, also when the served head equals its own.
- **A different entry at a sequence number already seen** is a **fork** (equivocation). The device freezes the vault (read-only) and shows the anomaly. Unlike the two states above, a frozen vault never thaws.
- **Checkpoints** carried by a device link (§8.3), and the account's vault records (§3.6 (e)), are verified heads too.
- **Gossip through items (recommended by the threat review).** A writer SHOULD put the vault's verified head into every item it writes, as `logHead: {seq, hash}` inside the encrypted item document (spec 02 §3 gains the optional field). Only key holders can read it, and the server cannot change it.
  - **An item's head is a hint to fetch, never a verdict (FS-R4).** Any writer, or a removed member working with the server, can put any value there, so an item head alone never freezes a vault and never marks it withheld.
  - A reader that decrypts an item whose `logHead.seq` is above its own verified head, or that names a different hash at a position the reader has verified, fetches the log.
  - A **fork** needs two validly signed entries at one position. If the fetch brings nothing new, the vault's details note "An item mentions history the server hasn't shown", and the vault's state does not change. While such a hint is unresolved, the member list is marked "may be incomplete" (FC-10).
  - This still gives members a cross-check against R20 and R21 at no extra round trip: an item head tells a device where to look.
- **Member lists.** A member's label is shown only where the log's member key equals that person's pinned key. Otherwise the list says "unverified member (as the server names them)" (T-R5). The list is the audience **as far as this device has verified the log**; a withheld later `add` is not shown (R20).

### 3.10 Personal vaults

A personal vault never has members, so it has no log. The server refuses members on it (unchanged), and clients never build an `add` for it. See §5.3.

### 3.11 Account deletion and the log

**Self-deletion.** `POST /account/delete` (spec 03 §13) gains a required body field `leaves: [<b64 0x03 leave frame> …]`.

- **Which vaults.** There is exactly one leave for every shared vault where the account holds a **non-owner grant row** that is active or revoked by `vault_delete` (a vault in its grace period). It does not matter whether the deleting device ever adopted the vault.
- **Where each leave is built.** At the **served** head (`GET /vaults/{id}/log` head, or the preview's list), with `at = max(device clock, head.at)` (T2-R-a). The deleting device need not have verified that vault's log: the leave is the account's own statement, and the server's head check (§3.7 step 6) places it.
  - **From an unverified head (FS-R7)**, the device refuses a `head.at` more than one day ahead of its own clock. It signs nothing for that vault, and the deletion stops with "One of your shared vaults reports a time far in the future. Try again later." So a server cannot obtain an honest far-future `leave` that would freeze the vault's offers.
- **The preview.** `GET /account/delete/preview` gains `memberships: [{vaultId, head: {seq, hash, at}}]` for exactly those vaults.
- **Appending.** The deletion transaction appends the leaves under §3.7's rules, except that they are **exempt from every rate bucket**, before it removes the grants. A missing, stale or invalid leave refuses the deletion with `409 account_delete_log_stale {vaultIds}`; the client refetches the heads and retries. A leave whose `at` is too far ahead of the server's clock refuses it with `400 at_ahead` instead, so a device with a fast clock is told to fix it rather than retrying (§3.3).
- **A deletion that is tried again** (rule of 2026-11-17) asks again when what would be deleted has changed: the device compares each fresh preview with the one the person confirmed, and when a vault would be deleted that the confirmation did not show, or the vaults that are left to others changed, it stops and asks again, showing what changed. It costs the person nothing for the server's own refusal: the server gives back what a deletion drew from its limits when it refuses because a vault's log moved on, since nothing about the person was decided. When a limit is reached, the screen says how long to wait. The account's ceiling for deletions is drawn before the password is judged and is never given back, so that a refusal a caller can produce at will (by leaving a leaf out) cannot be sent without end, each try running the password's hash (spec 03 §13). **Not kept yet (2026-11-17):** the browser reads the heads again and tries up to three times in all with a fresh preview, without comparing it with the confirmed one, so a vault made alone on another device meanwhile can be deleted unseen. (Closed on 2026-11-18: the server gives back the per-device deletion limit's and the two-step code's draws of a try refused for a moved log, once per draw, while the account's ceiling of twenty an hour keeps every try.)
- **Caps.** At most 500 leaves (the membership limit, §3.7), and the route's body cap is 1 MiB.
- **Result.** Members' logs always show the account leaving, signed by itself.
- **The device afterwards** (rule of 2026-11-17; spec 03 §13). A deletion the server confirmed leaves the device that made it as a fresh install for that server, as the released line does: everything kept under the server's origin goes, as "Forget this server" removes it (the copy, the keys, what waited to be sent, the identity and address records, the names kept for links, the quick-unlock key, the policy mirrors, the server's own pin and address), and the next start is the first launch, or the first screen where another server is still known. After a deletion whose outcome is not known, the notice offers "forget the account on this device" behind a confirmation, which is the same removal, for exactly that account at that server, and the device then ends that session at its server; it is not the forgetting of an earlier account under a known address (§4.4). The owner confirmed on 2026-11-18 that nothing stays: the notice that the account was deleted is built before the pin goes, says nothing that presumes the server stays known, and the landing is the first launch, or the first screen where another server is known. **What each removal takes** (rules of 2026-11-19; spec 03 §13): forgetting after an unknown outcome removes exactly that account and keeps what the device knows of the server, since the account may still exist and other accounts of that server on this device stay; what the device knows of that account's vaults stays, as at a sign-out, while changes to shared vaults signed and not sent go, counted in the confirmation (or "cannot tell"); the request to end the session is made once, and where it fails that session stays listed at the server until it expires and is ended from another device. A confirmed deletion never takes another account's copy unsaid: where another account's copy is held at that server, only the deleted account's records go and the server stays known, and the notice does not say that it was forgotten. A standing notice that a kit is gone for that address goes with a confirmed deletion and with "Forget this server". **Rules of 2026-11-19 that narrow and extend these** (spec 03 §13): a confirmed deletion forgets the server only where the device keeps nothing of ANOTHER account there, of any kind (a copy, a device row, trust records, an address's record), and otherwise removes the deleted account's records only and says that the server stays (decided by the owner on 2026-11-19, §0.6, which narrows his answer of 2026-11-18 for that one case); "Forget this server" removes every record of that server, of every account, the keeper's records of an address's account and of each identity key included; forgetting after an unknown outcome keeps the keeper's records of the address's account and of its identity key, as a sign-out does, because a server can produce the unknown outcome by ending the session during the request. **Kept (2026-11-19):** the shared code keeps the server after a confirmed deletion where something of another account is kept there, and says so (what counts: below); the kit-gone notice and the kept session's mark go with their server; after an unknown outcome the forgetting behind a confirmation that counts the changes never sent, the signed changes to shared vaults among them, with a new confirmation when the count changes; the web client keeps the keeper's records after an unknown outcome; the Safari extension forgets the server it took from the app once the app answers that it has none, the remembered address with it; the iPhone app's older controller forgets the deleted account's identity records and wipes the account whatever ended the session meanwhile, under the session's own origin. **Kept since 2026-11-19:** a confirmed deletion forgets the server only where the device keeps nothing of another account there (a held copy, a device row or an address's record of another address, a record kept for that server under another account's id; a store that cannot list its records counts as keeping something), and otherwise removes the deleted account's records only and says that the server stays known; the deleted account's copy goes once; the forgetting after an unknown outcome counts exactly the signed changes it removes and removes them after the copy is closed; the kept session's mark reads the same after a server's move; both apps draw that forgetting in two steps; the web client, after a confirmed deletion, keeps nothing that names the account or its address, keeps the server's pin (accepted as built, and put to the owner as a proposal against "nothing stays") and keeps a forgotten earlier account's key record (spec 03 §13). "Forget this server" takes the keeper's records of which account each address is at that server since 2026-11-19. **Not kept yet (2026-11-19 unless dated):** the shared code's forgetting after an unknown outcome removes both of the keeper's records, where the rule keeps them; an engine for vault logs that could not read the store when it began can still write after a forgetting (a send in flight at the confirmation writes nothing since 2026-11-19); the older controller keeps the server's address (2026-11-17). **One account id known at two server addresses on a device** (a server that moved and whose move was not followed, or one that serves a copy of another's accounts; rules of 2026-11-19 to 2026-11-21, kept in the shared code since 2026-11-21). An end at one server is that server's word alone, and it leaves what the other holds:
  - **What each end takes.** A sign-out, a deletion the server confirmed (on both of its roads: where the device keeps something of another account at that server, and where it keeps nothing else there) and the forgetting of an earlier account take what is held for the account at that server and at its former addresses (an address a followed move left, which has no pin of its own); "Forget this server" takes what is held at that server. Nothing held for the same account id at another server the device still knows goes on the first server's word: not its entry among the held accounts, not its copy with the changes in it not yet sent, not its records kept per server, not its name; its marks against an older state stay intact, and the account still signs in and unlocks there. The records kept by the account's id alone (its identity record, the stored account state with its checkpoint and high-water marks, the vault logs' hashes and checkpoints, the item engine's records, the recovery mirror, the escrow mark) go only where nothing at another server ties the account; where nothing ties it, nothing of it is left. "Forget this server" also takes every account held at that server, every record of that server and its pin.
  - **"Tied at another server" is one function**, read for all of these ends alike, and read once, before anything of the end is removed (a tie can run through the first server's record of the address, which the end removes); that one answer decides the identity record and the records kept by the id. At another server, a tie is: an address record that names the account; any record kept per server under the account's id (the record a forgetting keeps of an earlier account's key among them); an address record there that cannot be read, or that names no account the device can tell (it names every account); and a record there for the same address as one whose record at the first server names the account (a device's row, or an address record), unless that server's own address record for that address names ANOTHER account (the same email address used for another account at an unrelated server is someone else's there). A device's row there for that address ties only where it holds a device id (a session was once opened for it there); a row that is read and holds none ties nothing (only a sign-in attempt was made there: a mistyped password or a sign-in never finished writes one before anything is sent); a row whose record cannot be read as a device's record, whose secret is not one, or whose device id is not an id, ties (the device cannot tell, and keeping protects the account). A store that cannot list its records counts as a tie. A session kept for the account at another server is not read by this function: such a session normally leaves an address record there, which is the base tie.
  - **The session keeper's record of an end of the person's own** (a sign-out, a confirmed deletion, "Forget this server") names the server and the account and why it ended, and is written before the session's record goes; a start after a stop settles it by taking only what that server's end takes, comparing the server and the account, so an end at the first server is not settled away while the same id is kept at the second. A record an earlier build wrote without a reason is settled as a sign-out, as those builds settled every end.
  - **The forgetting's mark** (of an earlier account, kept until the forgetting is complete) keeps the tie as it was read before anything was removed and the addresses the account was held at; a forgetting cut part-way and finished at the next start or tap uses that reading, and reads the tie afresh only for a mark an earlier build wrote.
  - **The limit, as found before the sweep** (2026-11-21): records kept by an account's id stayed in a few states, none of them everyday: an account forgotten as an earlier account at one server, whose kept key record there then ties it at its other server's later end, until the first server is forgotten; a true tie whose tying server is later forgotten while it holds only a device's row with a device id; any address record at another server that cannot be read (it ties every account); a store that cannot list its records, also at a forgetting; a deletion stopped after the session keeper's part (the server stays pinned and the records kept by the id stay; nothing finishes it at a start); a former address with a partial carry of a move; a device's row that cannot be read or holds a malformed id (a damaged store); and the mark of a kept session at a server after a forgetting where the platform lost the session, until that server is forgotten. Most of what stays is readable without the account's keys (all but the sealed copy and the logs' contents).
  - **The sweep of accounts the device no longer knows** (rule of 2026-11-21; kept since 2026-11-21 in the shared code's app engine, which the phone and the desktop app run, `AccountSweep.kt`; not in `andvari-cli`, which has no sweep; spec 01 §7, spec 05 R48). Every record kept by the id of an account the device knows nowhere goes, whatever the tie said when something ended: the records of `PinStore.ACCOUNT_KEYED` (the identity record, the stored account state with its checkpoint and high-water marks, the vault logs' records, the item engine's records, the recovery mirror, the escrow mark), the identity record after the others. It only ever removes. Each leftover state in the list above ends with nothing kept by the account's id (found on 2026-11-21), except where the sweep removes nothing (an address record that cannot be read, a store that cannot list its records: below).
    - **A deletion stopped part-way** (built on 2026-11-21; spec 05 R48 (f)): a deletion its server confirmed writes a lasting mark of a deletion begun before its first removal (`EngineAccountSettings.DELETION_KEY`: the server, the account, the address it was known by there as its address record is keyed, the tie and whether another account was known there, both as read before anything was removed, and the take whose session it ends), and removes it after its last; a start, after settling own ends and forgettings and before the sweep, finishes what a mark names by the deletion's own steps, its server's pin and records included, so that the server is a first sight when it is met again. The sweep reads a mark as the account's step in flight. A mark is finished only while nothing has been taken at its server that uses what it would remove: a take at that server of the deleted account, or of any account under that address, removes the mark before the take writes anything of its own (`SessionKeeper.establish`, the one place every take passes, tells the engine), and nothing is ever removed for a mark that ended. A take there of another account under another address leaves the mark: the deletion finishes, and that account's records and the server's pin stay. A take of the same account at another server leaves it too: the deletion finishes at its own server, and the account's records at the other server and its identity record stay. A session kept at the next start decides nothing by itself; only the deletion's own session, kept at its server with a take other than the one the mark names (its at-rest mark lost), keeps everything, and the mark goes. A stop exactly at the mark's own write leaves the device as before the deletion, while the server has deleted the account: the device keeps a session its server no longer knows, and the server refuses its next use. A record of deletions begun with an entry that does not read, or that does not read at all, is written again at the start from the entries that read (none: no record), those are finished, and a diagnostic line says how many were dropped; nothing is removed for a dropped entry, which only ever keeps more. While the secure store says it holds a session record it cannot read now, nothing is finished and the marks stay; a record whose read fails holds the finish and the sweep back.
    - **Known** means at least one of: a session kept for the account, by this process's keeper or in the secure store now; a copy held for it at any address (`EngineAppApi.HELD_KEY`); an address record at any server naming it; something of it in flight: an end of the person's own not yet settled (`SessionKeeper.OWN_ENDS_KEY`; at a start the settling comes before the sweep, so an own end left by a stop is settled by then), a forgetting not finished (`EngineAppApi.FORGETTING_KEY`), a deletion whose outcome is not known. The record a forgetting keeps of an earlier account's key at its server is that server's, not kept by the id alone: it stays and does not make the account known. A device's row names an address, not an account: once no address record ties it to the account, nothing on the device says whose row it is, and it makes no account known (decided on 2026-11-21).
    - **When it runs:** at every start, after the settling of own ends and of forgettings (`EngineAppApi`, the start); and at the end of a deletion the server confirmed where the kept session is that account at that server (`EngineAccountSettings`; otherwise that end does nothing), of both forms of "Forget this server" (`EngineInstance`) and of a completed forgetting of an earlier account (`EngineAppApi.forgetEarlier`), after that end's own removals. It does not run at a sign-out, nor at "forget the account on this device" after a deletion whose outcome is not known; a signed-out account stays known by its address record.
    - **Where it does nothing:** where the store cannot list its keys, or where the session record, the list of held copies, an address record, the record of own ends, the forgetting marks or a mark of a deletion of unknown outcome cannot be read or does not parse as its form, it removes nothing and writes one line to the engine's diagnostic log. A removal the store refuses stops it there, with a line of its own; the next sweep goes on from what is left, so a sweep cut at any of its writes and followed by a start ends as the uncut one, and run again over what is left it removes nothing more.
    - **A take writes the address's record first** (`SessionKeeper.establish`), then the identity record, and every other record kept by the account's id is written while a session is kept, so no moment of a take leaves such a record that nothing names, apart from a registration the session keeper refused (below). A stop between the two writes leaves the address tied to that account at that server under the same key: a later sign-in of that account is taken and writes the identity record; another account served later under that address is refused as a fault, with the forgetting offered (the strict side).
    - **A registration the session keeper refused** leaves the records of the person's recovery choice (the recovery mirror and the escrow mark) with nothing that names the account: they go at the next start, and the person is asked the choice again, which is the safe side.
    - **One engine per device state:** no shipping app runs two engines over one store, and the store's writes are safe within one process only (spec 05 R48 (d)).
    - **A server's new address:** an account that lives at a server's new address while its address record lies under the old one, forgotten at the old address, keeps everything where a session or a held copy is kept for it at the new address; where neither is, its records go and the next sign-in there is a first sight of the account at a server whose own identity is still pinned (decided on 2026-11-21).
    - **The tie is unchanged:** where it keeps more than the sweep, the sweep removes the rest at the end of the same act; where the sweep cannot decide, the tie's keeping stands.
    - **Not kept yet (2026-11-21; decided that day, the first three before the first public release):** while the secure store cannot read its session record at a start, the offer to forget an earlier account is not made and `forgetEarlierAccount` refuses, exactly as where that record names the account (today a server that serves another account under the address in that state can lead the person, by their own two confirmations, into removing the kept account's address record, identity record and copy); each of the engine's own readers drops exactly what the sweep refuses, so that its next rewrite clears it (a held-list line whose id is no account id; forgetting marks that are not the marks' form, or an entry too short; a mark of a deletion of unknown outcome with no id), while an address record or a session record that cannot be read still holds the sweep back; where something ends, "tied" is the tie as it is OR the account known by the sweep's rule, read before anything is removed. Decided on 2026-11-21 as well, with no date set: each app passes the engine's diagnostic log on to its own (no app does yet, so a sweep held back says so nowhere).
  - A record that cannot be read never stops "Forget this server": an address record whose read fails, at any origin, counts as naming every account, so every identity record stays, everything else of that server goes with its pin, and the answer is "partly", with a reason of its own (kept since 2026-11-19).
  - A move whose target is known only by records of a server once known at that address is to be refused with a line of its own that says what forgetting at that address removes (never "already known at the new address" alone). **Not kept yet (2026-11-19):** the shared code's refusal of such a move does not tell the two cases apart, and the moved card has only the line "already known at the new address".
- **Blockers.** The existing blocker stays: the account may not own a shared vault that has other members. Owned vaults with no other members are purged with their logs; a tombstone and log are kept only where another account can still fetch them, as for a vault in its grace period (§3.8, S3-C5).
- **Connections.** The account's `connections` rows are deleted in the same transaction (X1), for self-deletion and operator deletion alike.

**Operator deletion (§9.2) has no leaves.** The person is not present to sign. The deletion transaction removes the account's grants server-side without log entries.

- On each affected vault the member stays in the log's `members` with no grant. Members' apps show "*name*'s account was deleted by the server's operator."
- The owner's app offers a one-tap "Remove from the list", which signs an ordinary `remove`.
- Until then nothing breaks: the server serves the deleted account nothing, and the next `add` of anyone else is unaffected.
- Operator deletion is refused while the account owns a shared vault with other members, as for self-deletion.

### 3.12 What this replaces

Deleted, §15:
- the lifecycle MACs and `lifecycleKey`;
- removal proofs v2 with their nonces, consumed sets and `liveSince`;
- the transfer proof columns;
- the proof-less cancel;
- `ownedVaults`, `issuedGrants`, `memberReceived`;
- "You own X?";
- the grant-authority sender rules that leaned on server labels;
- pending invitations from unpinned senders, which are replaced by §3.6's "connect with them first";
- automatic adoption of grants from pinned senders (Q5), which is replaced by §3.6's one-time accept.

The holding area for removed vaults stays, with its 30 days. A removal is attributed when the removed device verifies the `remove` entry; otherwise it is shown as unverified.

### 3.13 What removal does not do (T-R6)

No step here rotates a vault's key (P6 stays out of scope, R7). A removed member who kept the key, working with the server, can still read every later item. They can also **write**: items carry no author signature, and a hostile server accepts writes it should refuse. The remove dialog says so:

> Removing someone stops the server from giving them this vault. It doesn't change the vault's key: if they kept a copy and the server helps them, they could still read and change what's in it. To be sure, move everything to a new vault.

**Move everything to a new vault** is offered right there. It creates a new vault, copies the items through the existing move path (fresh item ids and attachment keys), re-adds the remaining members, and deletes the old vault. It is the app-level rotation until P6. While both vaults exist it needs free storage equal to the old vault's size; the operator can raise the quota.

The move's rules (decided 2026-11-16), in the order a person meets them:
- **Who is offered it.** The vault's owner only, and not on a withheld vault (§3.9): only on one whose history is verified, frozen or failed, that is not deleted, and whose key this device holds.
- **What the panel says before the person starts.**
  - Only live items move. The trash and earlier versions stay with the old vault and go when it is purged.
  - Items this device cannot read are counted. They are not copied, and the old vault is kept.
  - A remaining member this device cannot add (not a contact current here, or one whose code was never compared) is named. The move does not refuse to start: it adds the others, keeps the old vault, and its result says why.
  - No ticks on a vault in good standing: every remaining member is added to the new vault with the role they hold now, each asked to accept it (§3.6). Ticks, none ticked, appear only on a frozen or failed vault (below).
  - When an earlier move of this vault stopped and cannot be carried on ("Running it again", below), the panel says that this run makes a fresh vault and deletes the half-made one.
- **The new vault's name is typed by the person.**
- **Space, before anything is signed.** On a fresh sync the move checks the account's own use and limits (spec 03 §14.4, "The check before a large copy"); the remove dialog's `remove` is not signed before it either. When the account is short nothing is made, and the panel says which limit, by how much, and that the operator can raise it. When the server serves no use and limits the move starts, and a refusal for space part-way stops it like any other stop.
- **From the remove dialog, the member's `remove` is signed on the old log first,** before the new vault exists, so that a later restore of the old vault does not bring them back. On a frozen or failed vault nothing is signed on the old log (below), so there is no `remove` either.
- **Then the new vault, the copies and the adds.** Items that arrive while the copies run are copied too. What was copied is recorded by item and by the revision copied.
- **Only a stored change counts.** The first `remove`, each add and the old vault's delete count as done only when the server stored them. A `remove` or an add that was sent and has no answer yet (held for re-posting, §3.7) stops the move at that step, and the next run re-posts it first. After the adds the device reads the new vault's log again: an add counted but not stored there stops the move at the adds. The old vault's delete is never re-posted first: it has rules of its own ("A delete signed and not yet stored", below).
- **The old vault is deleted last** (into its grace, §3.8), and only when all of these hold: the vault is in good standing; every remaining member was added; every item was copied; nobody outside the people this move adds holds the new vault's key; and the old vault, read once more after the adds, holds nothing new or changed since its copy. Until then everything stays in the old vault as well: nothing is ever in neither vault. "Deleted" means that the server stored the old vault's delete, and the move says the old vault is deleted only then.
- **A delete signed and not yet stored** (decided 2026-11-16). A signed log change is never withdrawn (spec 03 §10.3), so once the old vault's delete is signed and this device does not know that the server stored it, the move says exactly that: the old vault's deletion is signed and waiting for the server, and it cannot be taken back. It never says "kept" and never "deleted". The old vault's own panel says the same, and that the move must be finished.
  - **Nothing but the move re-posts it.** The re-posting of held changes after an unlock or a reconnect (§3.7) leaves this delete alone. The first screen after an unlock says that a move is unfinished and offers to finish it.
  - **The re-send signs nothing** (rule of 2026-11-17). The move re-posts its held delete only through a call of the log's code that posts the held frame and builds no statement. And any change to the old vault's log, the delete itself included, is signed only over the head its caller checked: when the log moved on between the check and the signature, nothing is signed or sent. A user ID of all zeros is never an account: the log's code refuses any action that names it before it builds an entry, and a client refuses it wherever a server hands it one.
  - **While the delete waits, this device signs nothing else on the old vault's log and puts nothing into the old vault** (rule of 2026-11-17). Every other action on that log (a member added, removed or given another role, an ownership offer, a rename, a share released from a link) is refused before anything is signed, with one line that points to finishing the move; a link's attached share of that vault stays waiting. From the moment the delete is signed the old vault is read-only on this device, and the device says why, so that nothing new is put where it may vanish. Items can still be copied out of it. **Changes that were already queued for the vault when it closed for writing are held back, not sent and not dropped** (rule of 2026-11-17): what becomes of them is the person's choice in the notice the device shows when the vault's deletion was stored (the move's own, or the one for a deletion stored outside a move), which tells how many there are and offers to bring them into another vault or to let them go.
  - **A delete stored outside a run of the move is said** (rule of 2026-11-17). The server holds the signed delete and can store it whenever it likes; this device cannot order that. When a move's delete is seen stored outside a run of the move, it is never a quiet departure: the device compares the copy it holds with the move's record, says how many items were added or changed after they were copied, keeps the copy in the holding area as §3.8 has it, and offers to copy those items into the new vault. What another member added and this device never received cannot be saved by this device, and the sentence says so.
  - **The move's next run, in this order.** First it looks at the old vault's log and posts nothing:
    1. The delete is stored: the move is finished, and only now does it say "deleted".
    2. The log has moved on, and another stored change stands where the delete would have gone: the held delete can never be stored, and it is dropped by the log's own rule for a held change whose place was taken (spec 03 §10.3). The run then goes on as an ordinary run from the adds, with the old vault's members read again, and ends by the ordinary rules: it signs a new delete when every condition of "The old vault is deleted last" holds, or keeps the old vault and says why.
    3. Otherwise the delete can still be stored. The run reads the old vault again and first copies into the new vault whatever is new in the old one or changed since its copy. A changed item replaces its earlier copy, by the move's own record of what was copied and at which revision: never a second copy beside the first. If a copy cannot be made, the run stops there and the delete stays waiting. Then it re-posts the delete, and says "deleted" only when the server stored it.
  - **Why not simply re-post it at the next unlock:** an item that another member put into the old vault after the copies would then go into the deleted vault's grace without anyone being told, and be purged with it. Nothing is ever in neither vault, and nothing is lost quietly.
- **When the old vault is kept, the result says why:** it is frozen or failed; a member could not be added; items could not be read; someone outside this move can open the new vault; or something in the old vault changed meanwhile ("changed meanwhile": an item new in it, or changed since its copy, exists only there).
- **A stop says where.** A refusal or a lost connection at any step (the `remove`, the new vault, a copy, an add, the delete) stops the move and says at which step, how many items were copied, and into which new vault. Nothing is lost: the old vault is untouched.
- **Running it again.** In the same session, a stopped move carries on from where it stopped (the same new vault, the same copies, members already added skipped), but only when every member of the half-made new vault is among the people the present plan adds. Removing someone from the half-made vault would not take back the new key they were given, so otherwise the move starts a fresh vault with a fresh key, and deals with the half-made one, saying so. A move that stopped and is followed by a reload starts again with another new vault, except a move whose old-vault delete is signed and waiting: it is kept, with its record of what was copied, until it is finished (above).
  - **A half-made vault is deleted only if it holds nothing but what the move made** (rules of 2026-11-18). A member whom a stopped move had already added can save items of their own into the half-made vault, so before any road discards it (the run's end, the move's restart, the list worked off at a sync, the unlock that sends a deletion signed earlier and held, another tab) the device reads it, its trash included: where every item, live or in the trash, is a copy the move made at the revision the move made it, it is deleted as before; where anything else is in it (a member's own item, a copy changed, deleted and restored since, a copy of unknown revision, an item this account cannot open), or the read fails, it is NOT deleted: it stays a vault of its own, a held deletion of it is dropped, and the person is told its name and that it holds items the move did not make. The read that decides is never older than what the device was already told: a newer change announced meanwhile is read as well before anything is decided. No line says "held only copies" of a vault that was not read, and no line says "wasn't changed" after a removal the server stored. To judge "at the revision the move made it", the move records each copy's own revision at the server and keeps that record beside the half-made vault's id.
  - **A stopped run is remembered at rest from the moment it makes its new vault** (its id and the copies made so far, beside the move's record), so that after a lock, a reload or a closed tab the next move of the same vault finds the half-made one, reads it, and deletes it or names it. After a lock a stopped run is never carried on: the next move treats what it left as a half-made vault and starts afresh (accepted as the safer reading). A new vault that a move makes says so in its own sealed description (the vault it was moved from, and that it is a move's target), as an optional entry that a reader which does not know it leaves alone, so that another device, or this one after its record was lost, can recognise an unfinished target and name it; both implementations of the move write it.
  - **Before a run copies anything, every half-made vault of an earlier run of the same vault is dealt with** (every run that is not carried on): deleted where it holds only the move's copies, otherwise named, and where the move removes a member, with the plain statement that the member being removed still holds that vault and what is in it. The move is the product's way to cut a member off for good, so it never leaves a removed member holding copies without saying so.
  - **A deletion of a half-made vault that was dropped unsent, or whose answer was lost, is remembered** like every other change the device let go of (§3.7): if the vault's log later holds it, the person is told that the vault was deleted after all, by a deletion signed at such a time, and that it can be restored while its grace lasts.
  - **Between the read and the deletion a member's save can still be lost** (a window of a second or less: a deletion names only the log's head, never the items). The deletion of a vault is to carry, unsigned, the point up to which its items were read, and the server to refuse it in the same transaction when any item of the vault, live or in the trash, is newer; the client then reads again (spec 03 §11.1; spec 05 R43). The server keeps this since 2026-11-18 (the field `itemsAtMost`, the refusal `vault_items_moved`); until a client sends the field, the window stays for it.
  - **How a move's new vault says what it is** (rules of 2026-11-19): its sealed description carries one optional entry, `moveOf`, whose value is the id of the vault the move takes everything out of, as the log writes ids; it is written once, with the new vault's first entry, a rename keeps it, a reader that does not know it reads the name as before, and a vault made by hand carries nothing of the kind. Only the next "Move everything" out of the named vault reads it, on a device that holds no record of a run of its own for that vault, and only among vaults this account created: a vault found so is a half-made vault whose copies are not known, read from the server first (items, trash, what cannot be read), deleted only where that read shows nothing at all in it, otherwise kept and named with who can still open it and how many items the read counted (in lines that do not call them copies; no number where the read failed); more than one: the others are read at the next sync under the same rule. The room check before a move counts the new vault's description with the entry. Spec 05 R43 says what a member can do with the entry.
  - **The half-made step runs first in every run of the shared code, and an unread half-made vault stops the run** (rule of 2026-11-19): a run that cannot read what an earlier run left copies nothing; the failure is of the moment, and the person tries again. (The web client goes on and reads again at the end of the run; for a run that leaves someone out it stops too.) **The note that a half-made vault was kept, and that the member being removed still holds it, is kept at rest until the person dismisses it**, written before the vault leaves the move's record or together with that. **A held deletion of a vault half-made by a stopped move can be dropped by a call of the shared code** that remembers it as a kind of its own, so that the line above can be said, also where the move marked the deletion as its own; and the deletion of a half-made vault names the point up to which its items were read. **A frozen half-made vault of an earlier run does not stop the move for good** (kept in the shared code since 2026-11-19): it is kept as a vault of its own, read-only, with a note until dismissed that names the member who still holds it, nothing of it is sent, and the run goes on with a new vault.
  - **Kept (2026-11-19).** **The web client** keeps every bullet above: it reads a half-made vault, its trash included, before any road deletes it, and deletes it only where it holds nothing but the move's copies at the revisions the move made them; it remembers a stopped run at rest, writes `moveOf` and finds a target by it, deals with an earlier half-made vault before copying, and remembers a dropped deletion of it and says it if it was made after all; its deletion names the sync position of the read that decided it, at every sending, and where the server refuses it because an item changed after that read, nothing is stored or held and the vault is read and judged again, at most three times in one dealing, after which it stays due as a vault not read. **The shared code** reads a half-made vault (its log, a fresh sync and its trash) before it deletes it, deletes it only where every live item is a copy the move made at the revision it made it and every row in its trash is one the move put there, and otherwise keeps it, names it, and says where a member being removed still has it; a read that fails deletes nothing; the half-made vaults are dealt with before the run makes or copies anything, and what each run made is kept beside the deletion it is due; the note that a half-made vault was kept is kept at rest until dismissed, as ids, the names of its lines' keys and numbers only, the member as the account's id and public key, never a name (the names are filled in when the note is read with the account open), and a note read from the device shows only the move's own lines, each in its own shape. A note about a vault the account has since left or deleted is never listed and stays at rest, as ids, until the account's sign-out or forgetting (rule of 2026-11-19). Also kept in the shared code since 2026-11-19: each deletion names the sync position of the read that decided it, and where the server refuses it because an item moved since, nothing is stored or held and the move says the vault changed meanwhile; a held deletion of a half-made vault found to hold other items is dropped unsent through the log engine's own call, which remembers it, so that a later read that finds it stored says so with when it was signed; a copy that an earlier run made and that was deleted in the target since is not made again, and counted neither among the copies made nor in the line about files left out; both apps draw the move's notes. Right after a finished move the old vault's row reads deleted, and a shared vault whose deletion stands is never writable on its row (a restore makes it writable at once), since 2026-11-19. **Not kept yet (2026-11-19):** the shared code does not write or read `moveOf`; a lock during the read that follows a finished move can leave a stale record of an unfinished move (found by reading, not shown by a test).

**What the code keeps (2026-11-17).** Both clients count the old vault's delete as done only when the server stored it, both check space before anything is signed or sent, and both log stores refuse to sign anything else on a log while one of its changes waits (`append_pending`).
