- **The web client** keeps "A delete signed and not yet stored" and its points, "The re-send signs nothing" among them: its log store re-posts the held frame byte for byte and signs nothing for it; it signs the old vault's delete only over the head its checks were made on, and signs nothing when the verified head moved on (the move then keeps the old vault as changed meanwhile, and the next run reads it again); the vault log builds no action that names the all-zero user ID, and a session or a kit recovery's answer that names it is refused. The move and the old vault's panel say that the deletion is signed and waiting and cannot be taken back; the unlock's re-posting of held changes skips the move's delete (it is marked as the move's); the first screen names each unfinished move and offers to finish it. Its next run looks at the log first and posts nothing, drops the held delete when another verified entry stands at its place, and otherwise copies what is new or changed (a changed item replaces its own earlier copy) before it re-posts the delete. The move's record (the new vault, each copied item with the revision copied and its copy, who was added, the half-made vault) is kept beside the held delete, holds no names and no content, and survives a reload. While the delete waits the old vault is read-only in the browser: every other action on its log (a member added, removed or given another role, an ownership offer or its cancel, a delete, a rename) is refused before anything is signed or sent, with one sentence that points to finishing the move; nothing is sealed under its key (no new item, no edit, no item deleted or restored, no import, nothing moved into or out of it), while copying an item out still works; it is no target in the pickers, and one line above its items says why. A move's delete stored outside a run of the move is said: the browser compares what it holds with the move's record, says how many items were added or changed after their copy, keeps the copy in the holding area, and offers to copy those items into the new vault (a changed item over its earlier copy); the notice says that what another member added and this browser never received cannot be saved here.
- **The shared code** says on the move's screen, on the old vault's panel and on the first screen after an unlock that the deletion is signed and waiting and cannot be taken back, and the first screen offers to finish the move. Nothing re-posts held changes after a sync or at an unlock. The move's record survives a lock and a restart, and an item changed while the delete waits replaces its earlier copy. Its next run reads the old vault's log first and posts nothing; a stored delete finishes the move; a log that moved past the delete's place makes the run go on as an ordinary run with the members read again; otherwise it copies what is new or changed, reads the old vault's head again, and stops ("changed meanwhile") when it is not the head its members were checked at, before it signs the delete. The log's code has a call that re-posts a held frame and signs nothing, and an append that signs only over a head its caller names, and it refuses any action that names the all-zero user ID; a link's contacts refuse that ID too. The move uses both calls: it re-posts its held delete through the call that signs nothing, and signs the delete only over the head it checked. While the delete waits, every other road to the old vault's log (a member added, removed or given another role, an ownership offer, its cancel or its acceptance, a rename, a delete or a leave from outside the move, a restore, a share released from a link by hand or by itself) is refused before anything is signed or sent, with one line that points to finishing the move (a record of the move that cannot be read counts as waiting); a link's attached share of that vault stays waiting. From the moment the delete is signed the old vault is read-only on this device and its row says why: nothing is saved, edited, deleted, restored, imported or moved into or out of it, and copying out works. A move's delete stored outside a run of the move is said, with how many items were added and changed after their copy, the copy kept in the holding area, and an action that copies them into the new vault (a changed item replaces its earlier copy; a second run copies nothing). The place where a served session becomes this device's session (a sign-in, a registration, the recovery commits, a device link's pickup), the two-step sign-in's answer, the kit recovery's answer and the device link's identity check refuse the all-zero user ID. The content's code has a call that brings held items, with their files, into a chosen vault (each file re-encrypted under a fresh key; a file the server no longer hands over left out of its copy and counted; an earlier copy deleted only after the new one is confirmed, and kept when someone changed it since; safe to run twice). It has the vault closed for writing, refused on every way in before anything is sealed, with its own line, kept across a lock and a restart; and changes already sealed and queued for a vault closed for writing are held back from it: they are sent when the mark is cleared, parked with the held copy when the vault goes, and carried by that call as the item's newest content. The app interface closes the old vault for writing through the content's own mark just before the move's delete is signed, so every way into it is refused by the content itself and changes still queued for it are held back; it opens the vault again when nothing waits any more (the delete refused before or at signing, refused for good, or dropped because its place was taken, and once the delete is stored), and every opening of the vault marks again a kept record whose deletion waits; a lock that overtakes the signed delete leaves the old vault closed. **Only a deletion closes a vault for writing** (rule of 2026-11-17): a change that waits for a vault's log closes it only when that change is the vault's deletion, which the device signed itself and can read; a rename, or a change of a member or a role, that waits on the old vault of an unfinished move closes nothing. **Kept (2026-11-19):** the app interface closes the old vault of an unfinished move for a held change only when that change is a deletion; a held rename or change of a member or a role closes nothing (until then it counted any held change as the move's waiting deletion). The action that copies items into the new vault goes through the content's call, so an item with files is copied with them, and an item whose change never reached the server counts among those changed after their copy. The content's list of vaults gives a vault closed for writing a state and a line of its own, and a new item, a move or a copy into it is refused with that line. **A person's own deletion that waited when its vault closed** (rules of 2026-11-17, as set aside on 2026-11-18). The rule of 2026-11-17 was that such a deletion is carried without a question, since it was the person's: the earlier copy of the item in the other vault goes to that vault's trash, where it can be restored for thirty days. A copy may be deleted that way only at the revision the move itself made: what is deleted is decided against the copy as it stands when the deletion is sent, so that a copy changed elsewhere since, or one with a change still waiting on this device, is left alone and counted as kept, never deleted and counted as carried. **Until the content can delete a copy only at a revision that is named, a move carries none of the person's waiting deletions:** each is settled as kept, once and without a question, counted, and said ("N items you had deleted are still in that vault; delete them yourself"), and nothing is deleted. The app interface keeps this since 2026-11-18, in the finishing run and in "copy what is late"; carrying comes back once the finishing run and the loop over stale copies name the revision the run recorded for every deletion they ask for (the content's side is built since 2026-11-18, below). A deletion that a move's carry kept says why, with a sentence for each case: the copy was changed in the new vault since, the server refused the deletion, or no revision of the copy was known (the content tells these apart since 2026-11-18; **not kept yet (2026-11-18):** the app interface does not split the kept deletions by reason, so no screen can say which case it was). **"Copy what is late" has the same fault** (rule of 2026-11-18): a changed item of the old vault replaces its earlier copy, and the earlier copy is deleted after a sync without a look at it, so a copy another member changed in the new vault goes to the trash. A pick is to name the revision it replaces, and the earlier copy is to be deleted only at that revision and kept at any other; then the move carries deletions again and names the revision at both places. **What the content keeps (2026-11-18):** an earlier copy is deleted only at the revision the pick names, and the deletion is sent at that revision, not at the one the device holds: where the pick names no revision, the copy is kept (no revision known); where the device holds it at another revision, or a change of it waits on this device, or the server answers that it changed since, it is kept (changed there since), and a refusal keeps it too; nothing is deleted on a guess. A kept earlier copy stays live in the new vault and the new copy stands beside it. "Copy what is late" names the revision the move recorded for each copy. **Kept (2026-11-19), in the shared code:** an earlier copy that a newer copy replaced is deleted only at the revision the move made it at, and one changed since, or of no known revision, stands beside the newer copy and is counted and said; the person's own deletions that waited when the old vault closed are carried again, each at the revision the move recorded for its copy, and kept and counted where that revision no longer stands. Both apps draw the move's notes since 2026-11-19, the member after the line that names them, until the person dismisses them; both apps name the member also after a frozen half-made vault's line (the desktop and the phone since 2026-11-19). **Running the copy again** (rules of 2026-11-18), wherever this device can know (its own record of the run, and its own trash): a copy changed in the target since it was made is never overwritten and never deleted, and it is said; a copy deleted in the target since is not made again, and it is said ("were deleted there since and were not copied again", never "you deleted", since another member may have); a source changed since replaces a copy that is untouched at the revision the run recorded, and stands beside one that was changed; a confirmed copy's change is never sent again. A count never says more than is so: a copy not made again because it was deleted there is not counted as copied. The content keeps all four since 2026-11-18 (a copy deleted there is not made again, and an earlier copy its pick names is then kept). **Not kept yet (2026-11-18):** nothing says the first two cases to the person, except that since 2026-11-19 the shared code hands a line for each with the notice for a deletion stored outside a move, and neither app draws those lines yet (the shared code no longer counts a copy found deleted in the new vault as copied, since 2026-11-19); a device that never saw the deletion (another device, or past the device's kept trash list) brings such a copy back, and the server then holds it as restored with a conflict copy beside it, so the item stands there twice. **A put that brings a deleted copy back makes no conflict copy of the deleted version.** The server keeps this for both clients since 2026-11-18: a put over a deletion is answered with the deletion's mark every time (a replay and an answer at its size limit included) and the item it brings back is stored without the conflict mark, so neither client makes a "(conflict)" copy of it; the answer still says "conflict" with the deletion's mark, so a client can tell the person that its change brought back an item deleted elsewhere (spec 03 §5). **The line after a lock** that cut a copy short says that copying again copies only what is not there yet, and that where an item changed since its copy, the newer version may stand beside the older one (rule of 2026-11-18). **Not kept yet (2026-11-18):** the line still says that copying again makes no duplicates, which is more than is so where a lock fell inside an earlier copy's deletion, where another member changed an earlier copy, or where a second device ran the move. **Decided for later: no synced record of the copies.** A record every client would keep of which items of a vault were copied into which (so that a move finished from another device, or past the server's journal window, could be judged) is not built before the release: what it would settle is answered on the safe side today, since the device keeps what it cannot judge and makes at most one more copy, so the worst case is an item that stands in the target twice, never one that is in neither. Against it stand a new kind of synced record that every client must hide, never count, never export and never fill from, and that would tell every member of the target vault which items of the other vault were copied. It is built when use shows that the second device matters, or when the owner says so. **A change waiting on this device that it cannot read** closes the vault for writing with a line of its own (a deletion may be waiting), not with the line for a deletion this device signed (rule of 2026-11-18): the content has the reason and its line since 2026-11-18, kept across a restart, and the app interface marks such a vault with that reason since 2026-11-19; **not kept yet (2026-11-19):** where the engine itself refuses as the backstop, the line is still the one for a signed deletion. **The notice the device shows when the old vault's deletion was stored** (the move's own, or the one for a deletion stored outside a move) counts the changes that were held back together with what was added since, and offers to bring them into another vault; letting them go is the person's answer to that notice, given after the number is said; and it says how many deletions were kept. The notice for a deletion stored outside a move keeps this in part (2026-11-17): it counts the changes held back together with what was added and changed since, offers to bring them into another vault, and its dismissal is letting them go, after the number is said. Both apps draw, in the notice for a deletion stored outside a move, the lines that say how many of the person's deletions were carried and how many were kept (2026-11-18). **Not kept yet (2026-11-18):** the move's own run, when it stores the deletion, shows no notice, so its held-back changes are not said.

**On a frozen or failed vault** (§3.9) "Move everything" still works, as the way out (T2-R-k). It copies the items out (§3.6) and leaves the old vault where it is, still frozen. **It re-adds only the members the owner ticks, and none is ticked by default** (FS-R3): the last verified state may predate a `remove` this device never saw, so its member list could bring back someone the owner removed. It signs nothing on that log, because the device cannot tell which branch is real. The person can then hide the old vault with "Remove from this device".

---

## 4. The instance card

### 4.1 Template (purpose 0x04)

```
{"v":2,"purpose":"instance-card","instanceId":<uuid>,"seq":<int>,"prev":<hex64>,"origin":<origin>,
 "name":<name>,"kind":"public"|"private","operator":<uuid>,"operatorSignKey":<b64_32>,
 "recoveryFp":<hex64n>,"issuedAt":<ms>}
```

- `origin` is the instance's canonical origin.
- `name` is the display name.
- `kind` is `ANDVARI_INSTANCE_KIND` (§7.1). It selects the copy and defaults that differ between a public instance and a private (family) instance. It grants nothing.
- `operator` and `operatorSignKey` name the operator account and its **Ed25519 signing key**: the joint key, derived from the account's identity seed (spec 01 §5). The card names the Ed25519 form so that a verifier without a Montgomery map (the extension, §12.2) can check the chain byte for byte (B5). The server requires `operatorSignKey` to be **byte-equal to the Ed25519 key that signed the operator account's genesis** (the genesis frame's `signerPub`, which the server stores, §4.3). Among clients only the operator's own devices check it, against the Ed25519 key they derive from their own identity seed (`operator_key_mismatch`, VR-11). The comparison is on the Ed25519 key itself, never through its X25519 image: a key and its negation have the same image, the key's holder can sign under either, and a card naming the negation would pass an image check and then lock the real key out of every later card. Other clients hold no trustworthy copy of that key, so they do not check it, and nothing rests on it: every card is still signed by the previous card's key.
- `recoveryFp` is the SHA-256 of the instance recovery public key, or `none` when the instance offers no operator recovery.
- The operator's short fingerprint shown anywhere is the usual identity fingerprint over that X25519 key (spec 01 §5).

### 4.2 Chain

- Card 0 has `seq = 0` and `prev` = 64 zeros. Its `signerPub` equals its own `operatorSignKey`.
- Card n has `prev = H(card n−1)`. Its `signerPub` equals **card n−1's `operatorSignKey`**.
- `instanceId` is constant.
- Any field except `instanceId` may change at n: a move (`origin`), a rename, an operator handover (`operator`, `operatorSignKey`), or a recovery-key rotation (`recoveryFp`).
- Verdicts and their order are in §2.4 (VR-10): those of §3.3 without `precondition`, with `instance_mismatch` in place of `wrong_vault`.

### 4.3 Serving

- **`GET /api/v1/instance`** returns `{ "cards": [<b64 frame> …], "rotations": [<b64 0x08 frame> …] }`, card 0 first and rotations oldest first (N3). Unauthenticated, cacheable with an ETag, rate-limited per IP.
  - While **no card exists yet**, it returns `{ "cards": [], "setup": {instanceId, origin, name, kind, recoveryFp} }`: the values `andvari setup` wrote.
  - These are used only by a signed-in operator resuming an interrupted setup (§6.7, R10). That is the operator trusting their own host at setup time; it never serves as a pin.
- **`PUT /api/v1/instance/card { card, currentAuthKey }`** is for the operator session, with a fresh `currentAuthKey`. The server checks the chain and stores the card.
  - On a handover it switches the `operator` role to the named account atomically. That account must be active. On a public instance it must also hold no shared-vault ownership.
  - **Refusals (FI-R3):** `403 not_operator` (the caller is not the operator account); `409 card_head_moved {seq, hash}` (the card is not the next one); `400 bad_card` (parse, chain, signature or field checks, including card 0's checks below and, under variant R, a public card with a `recoveryFp` other than `none`); `409 handover_target_invalid` (the named account is not active, or on a public instance owns a shared vault).
- **`PUT /api/v1/instance/rotation { statement, currentAuthKey }`** is for the operator session (†). The server stores a 0x08 statement only if it verifies against the current recovery key (`fromFp` equals the newest card's `recoveryFp`, or the newest stored rotation's `toFp`); otherwise `400 bad_rotation`. The server cannot tell a signed rotation from an unsigned change, so it accepts a card that changes `recoveryFp` either way. The operator's tools upload the rotation before the card, and clients treat the change as signed only when the matching rotation is served (§4.5, S3-C4).
- **`GET /api/v1/recovery-pubkey`** (existing, FI-R3) serves the instance recovery public key, which §4.5 condition 3 checks against the card's `recoveryFp` at full length. It answers `404` when `recoveryFp = none`, and on a public instance under variant R.
- **Card 0** is refused unless all of these hold (R10):
  - `instanceId`, `origin`, `kind` and `recoveryFp` equal what `andvari setup` wrote (`ANDVARI_CANONICAL_ORIGIN`; `ANDVARI_RECOVERY_FINGERPRINT` or `none`);
  - `operator` is the caller, who is the operator account;
  - `operatorSignKey` is byte-equal to the Ed25519 key that signed the caller's genesis (the stored genesis frame's `signerPub`), never compared through its X25519 image (§4.1).
- **Card n** passes the same `operator` and key check for the account it names.
- **`kind` is not changed from a client in this version.** It follows the host's setting at setup, and neither the apps nor the command-line tool offer to sign a card with another `kind`. A card that does change it is still handled by every client as §4.4 says.
- **Moves.** When a card moves the origin, the old server keeps serving the chain, so devices that still dial it learn of the move.

### 4.4 Pinning and trust roots

A client keeps, per origin namespace, a pinned card record that outlives sign-out and cache wipes:

```
{ instanceId, card0Hash, seq, hash, origin, name, kind, operator, operatorSignKey, recoveryFp }
```

`card0Hash` is the hash of the chain's card 0, which every root knows when it makes the first pin (each one anchors at card 0 or verifies the chain from it). A served chain whose card 0 does not hash to it is a **fork** at once, however short that chain is: a replaced chain is then never mistaken for one that is merely withheld, and §4.6's reset shows as a fork as soon as the new card 0 is served.

**Rules for the pin store.** They hold for every client that keeps pins (the review of the first engine code found each one broken somewhere):
- **A root never replaces an existing pin.** When the origin already has a pin, a root that turns up later (baked, kit, setup, link offer, Trust Gate) first compares the served chain with that pin and stops on anything but `ok`. It may then add its anchor or raise the root; it never swaps the chain. Otherwise a kit scanned on an already-pinned device would hide a fork or roll the pin back.
- **A card that changes both `origin` and `kind` is a `kind` change**, with its Trust Gate, never a move alone. When the person accepts it, the pin records the accepted `kind` and stays at its card; the same card's changed origin is then offered as a move in its own right, with its own tap, and only following that move advances the pin. A client never stores a pin whose `origin` is not the origin it is stored under: such a record is `damaged`.
- **"Store builds never follow a move or a `kind` change of the baked instance" is keyed on the instance**, that is, on a pin whose `card0Hash` is the baked one or whose origin is the baked origin, however that pin was first made.
- **A move never replaces a pin that already exists for the new origin.** It is refused with `pin_conflict`.
- **A fork is durable.** It is stored with the pin and survives restarts. While it is set, the client refreshes nothing from that origin as trusted, the password rule refuses, and every flow with the server stops. The only way on is "Forget this server".
- **The stop is enforced where the client dials.** The one place that opens a connection for an origin refuses every request to an origin whose pin is forked or damaged, a token refresh and a WebSocket included, whatever flow asks; no flow is trusted to remember to check. (The hostile-server harness found sync, push, account-state and log requests still going out with the session's token under a forked pin, because only the password rule's callers looked at the pin.) Each sync also judges the card chain first (below), so a fork is noticed by ordinary use and not only when a flow happens to refresh the card.
- **A fork stops traffic, not reading.** What this device already holds stays readable: a vault it has a copy of can still be unlocked from that copy, read, copied from, filled from and exported, with the warning in view. Nothing is sent to the origin, nothing from it is accepted, and nothing is written. A server must not be able to take a person's saved passwords away from their own device by misbehaving, and after an operator reset (§4.6) people need a way to save what had not synced before they forget the server. "Forget this server" says plainly that it deletes this copy. A `damaged` pin is different: the device can no longer check what it holds against anything, so it stays blocked.
- **A session the server ends does not delete the device's copy.** A refused refresh, a revoked session, or any other answer by which the server ends a session, ends the session: its tokens and the quick-unlock key are dropped, and the account stands on the device as signed out by the server. What the device holds stays. It can still be unlocked from that copy with the master password and read, copied from, filled from and exported, with a notice in view; nothing is sent and nothing is written while it stands so. Signing in again to the same account at the same origin, with its pin in force, restores the session, and the changes not yet sent are sent then. The copy is deleted only by the person: "Sign out", "Forget this server", deleting the account, or a policy that forbids an offline copy (§7.1 `offlineCacheAllowed`; it tries once to send what waits first, below). A server can refuse a refresh at will, by fault or on purpose; if that deleted the copy, one answer would take away everything not yet synced, which the rule above forbids. The cost is stated on the Devices screen: revoking a lost device stops it syncing and does not wipe it; its copy stays encrypted under the master password.
- **A device's copy is never deleted over changes that were not sent without the person having been told** (rule of 2026-11-17). Signing out deletes this device's copy of the account, and so does the switch to another server's invitation, which is a sign-out: one account is open on a device at a time, this server's pin and trust records stay, the account stays on its server, and this device's copy goes as at any sign-out. Before either act the app interface gives the screen the number of changes on this device that were not sent (it can count them while the vault is open); with any, the confirmation says how many will be lost, offers to try sending them first, and the act happens only on the person's own second confirmation. With the vault locked, where they cannot be counted, the confirmation says that it cannot tell. When the account has "New devices must be linked" on, the line beside the switch says, before the switch, that signing in again on this device will not bring the account back: it needs another of the account's devices to link this one, or a recovery. **Over changes not sent, a sign-out is asked twice, in both apps alike** (rule of 2026-11-17): the first confirmation shows the line with the number (or that this device cannot tell) and the offer to send them first; its main button opens a second confirmation, which shows the line again and alone signs out (or switches). With nothing waiting there is one confirmation. The second confirmation's title and button have the same words in both apps. The browser and `andvari-cli` are held to the same rule.
  - **What the code keeps (2026-11-17).** The shared code gives the screen a check before either act (`signOutCheck`): the number of changes made on this device and not sent (queued changes, changes the server refused that are still kept, and the changes parked with vaults held on the device), or none when the vault is locked; the line that says how many will be lost, or that this device cannot tell; and the offer to send them first, made only while the vault is open, the session is live and something waits. A sign-out or a switch that the check says needs a confirmation is refused, with nothing deleted, until the screen confirms that same check; a check that no longer holds (a further change, a lock) confirms nothing. While the vault is locked the act needs the confirmation too. Sending first runs a sync and counts again, and says when not everything could be sent. The switch asks the other server nothing before it is confirmed. Its line says that signing in again will not bring this device back by itself when this device set "New devices must be linked" on during this session, the plain line when it set it off, and otherwise, the usual case because the server serves no read of that setting, that this holds if the account has it on. The Android and desktop apps both ask twice over changes not sent, wherever an account is signed out (Settings, the lock screen, a kept account the server signed out, and the switch from another server's invitation, where the invitation's own window is the first step): the first confirmation shows the line and the offer to send first, its main button opens the second confirmation, and only that one signs out or switches; locked, the line says this device cannot tell, and where the session is not live (locked, or an account its server signed out) no send-first is offered.
  - **What a device keeps to send, and so what the number counts** (rule of 2026-11-17, which writes down what the shared code and the current app already do; spec 03 §5). A copy kept at rest (on Android by default, on the desktop with "keep me signed in", in the browser with the offline copy on) keeps a change that got no answer, to send it later, and counts it; a save the server refused for now (the session has ended, too many requests, a fault of the server) is kept and sent later the same way, and is said as that, never as "failed". A copy kept only in memory keeps nothing it cannot send now: the save is refused, nothing stays queued, the editor still holds what was typed, and an earlier waiting change of the same item that the refused one had folded is put back. A queue in memory never claims to keep something. So the number of changes not sent can be above zero wherever the copy is at rest, which is the ground of the rule above; a desktop that is not kept signed in refuses an offline save instead of counting it. A save with files that is kept to finish counts as a change not sent. **Such a save stays until its item has landed** (rules of 2026-11-17; spec 03 §5): its record and its set-aside files are kept until the server holds the item itself, on every road, also where the session ended meanwhile, and the next sign-in finishes it; before a waiting change is sent whose files the server may have removed, they are sent again. Only an answer that says the change can never be taken gives it up, and says what was lost. The shared content keeps this since 2026-11-18, the giving up included: a kept save whose queued change the server refused for good is let go once, with its files, and said, and never sent again (spec 03 §5 has the cases and what is accepted). **A copy into the personal vault that a lock cut short** says what is so: copying again copies every item again, the ones already copied too. A copy that can be repeated without duplicates is designed first (what a repeat does to a copy the person edited or deleted since, and to one whose source changed) and built as a unit of its own; until then each copy gets a fresh id (the shared content, since 2026-11-18). The copy of held items into a vault (the move's own road) still names its copies by what is copied; a repeat of it follows the rules of 2026-11-18 for running a copy again (§3.13: a copy changed or deleted in the target since is left as it is, and a confirmed copy is never sent again), and its line after a lock is to stop saying that a repeat makes no duplicates (§3.13 says where that is more than is so).
  - **The count is a count of changes, in every client** (rule of 2026-11-17): a vault in the holding area adds to it only the changes that are parked with it and were not sent; a held vault with none adds nothing and is never called a change made on this device. Where what waits cannot be sent by anything the person can do, the line does not offer to send it first.
  - **Not kept yet (2026-11-17):** changes that an extension captured into the save queue are not counted. The browser counts each held vault as one change, whatever is parked with it (the shared code counts each parked change since 2026-11-18). `andvari-cli` keeps no copy and no queue on disk between runs, so its sign-out has nothing to count. (Closed on 2026-11-17: a sign-out counts saves with files that are kept to finish; an import cut short on a copy in memory keeps nothing queued behind a line that says it stopped, with how many items were saved and how many not, and on a copy at rest what could not be sent is kept, counted and sent later; a save kept on the device is never said as failed; on a copy in memory a refused save, for any reason, leaves nothing queued and puts back an earlier waiting change of the same item; the browser's sign-out counts as the shared code counts, queued and refused changes and held vaults, also while locked, says "cannot tell" when it cannot read them, says how to send them first, and counts again at the second click; the two apps' second confirmations have the same shape, a title of its own that asks the question and a button that names the act, one pair for a sign-out and one for a switch, under the same four entries of the wording page.)
  - **The extension** (rules of 2026-11-17). It keeps no queue of changes of its own; what it holds that was not sent is the logins and cards it captured on pages and has not saved. A sign-out says how many will be lost and, while the vault is open, how to save them first. A lock by the person's own hand asks first when there are any (a page's password is never kept past a lock). A lock that nobody is there for, the idle lock, deletes nothing a person has not been told about: a captured login or card that was waiting when the extension locked by itself is said the next time the popup opens ("N logins this extension had captured were not saved before it locked"), once, with no way to bring them back claimed; the same line follows the full wipe after a session the server ended for good. "Forget this server" on the extension's options page, with captures waiting or when it cannot tell, says how many on the first click and forgets on the second.
  - **A sign-in that ends in a sign-out by the client itself** (rule of 2026-11-17: in the browser, the recovery kit's step that timed out, or an account that turned out to be the server's operator, which the browser does not take) says the number before anything is deleted, not after: the same two steps as every other way out.
  - **What the browser and the extension keep (2026-11-17).** The browser's "Forget this server", with changes not sent or when it cannot tell, says how many on the first click and forgets on the second. Turning the offline copy off counts queued changes and held vaults, says "cannot tell" when it cannot read them, offers to try sending them first (one sync, then a new count), and counts again at the second click. A sign-in that ends in the browser's own sign-out (the kit's step timed out, the account is the operator's, or an open vault's card names the account as the operator) keeps the copy, its queue and the session record without tokens when anything waits, and lands on the unlock card with the reason and the number; nothing is deleted until the person's own Sign out there, which counts and asks again. The extension's sign-out says how many captured logins and cards will be lost and, while the vault is open, how to save them first; a lock by hand with captures waiting asks first; the idle lock and the full wipe after a session the server ended for good keep only the number and say it once the next time the popup opens; "Forget this server" on the options page says how many, or that it cannot tell, on the first click and forgets on the second.
  - **Not kept yet (2026-11-17), in the extension:** the popup's Lock reads a count it cannot get as none and so locks on one click, where "cannot tell" must ask; the popup's own "Forget this server" buttons on its stop card forget on one click; in a worker that starts cold and cannot open its seal within the lock's time, captures from an earlier life of the worker are not counted, and the line after the idle lock then says nothing for them where it should say that it cannot tell; what following a move does to captures not saved is not yet said.
  - **A server's policy that forbids the offline copy** (decided on 2026-11-17; spec 03 §1.1, spec 05 R11). Such a policy (`offlineCacheAllowed=false`) is a prohibition with a prompt, unconditional wipe of that server's own copy on the device. When changes wait that were not sent, the client first tries ONCE to send what waits, then wipes as specified, whatever the attempt brought. Once means one run through what waits, each request with its ordinary limit, no second try, no waiting for a connection to come back, no question to the person; the run ends at the first request that gets no answer; a session that has ended sends nothing, and the wipe then follows at once. Afterwards it says how many changes could not be sent, once, at the first moment the person can read it; the number is kept apart from the copy so that it survives the wipe (a number and the server's name, nothing of the content). Where every change was sent, the notice says that the copy was removed because the server's policy asks for it, and that nothing was lost. This holds for every copy the policy wipes (the browser's offline copy and its unlock copy; any other client's own). **The extension** (rules of 2026-11-17): whatever of a vault's content, of changes waiting to be sent or of an account's keys a client keeps beyond the browser's session is a copy at rest in the policy's sense, the extension's included; the extension keeps none. Its record of the personal vault's wrap (`personalWrap:<userId>`, the vault's key sealed under the account's key as the server served it) opens nothing the extension keeps and gives nobody a way to test a guessed password (the account key's own wrap is not kept), so it is not such a copy and stays. A capture that waits for the person's answer is not yet a change, and the policy does not wipe it: it may be a password the person has nowhere else. How the attempt runs (rules of 2026-11-17; spec 03 §1.1 has each point): the wipe is of the copy of the account whose session read the policy, and another account's copy waits until that account is opened and reads the policy itself; every request goes with the copy's own account's session, checked at that request, and the attempt ends where it is gone; the whole attempt has one limit of sixty seconds, each request thirty; one run per copy across tabs, under the lock every other wipe takes, and from the reading of the policy that copy alone takes no new change and says why; three outcomes, sent (the server's answer says it took it), may or may not have arrived (no answer in time, or none), and not sent, where a conflict is judged by what the server did with it (rule of 2026-11-18: a put over a newer version is stored and counts as sent, an edit that restored a deleted item counts as sent, only a deletion over a newer edit counts as not made, and no conflict copy is made during the attempt); the number is read by the run that wipes, and a copy that cannot be opened is "cannot tell", never zero; a wipe that could not be made is never said as made, is tried again at each start only while the policy still forbids the copy, and ends with its notice when the policy is lifted; a run that a tab's closing cut off was not the one attempt, and the next start makes it; a wipe that is owed (the policy's, or a sign-out's own wipe that waited for the policy's run) is finished at the next start in that browser, without a session; a start that cannot read the policy neither retries a held wipe nor opens the copy; and a wipe that another tab holds up says to close andvari's other tabs in this browser. **Kept (2026-11-18):** the web client does all of this, and judges a conflict by what the server did with it since 2026-11-19; the records of each shared vault's log are not removed by the policy (accepted on 2026-11-18: they are trust records and the few signed entries that wait, nothing of a vault's content).
    - **On a device** (rules of 2026-11-18; the owner's decision of 2026-11-17 holds for every client that keeps a copy). The two apps, through the shared code, try once to send what waits, then wipe, then say what could not be sent; `andvari-cli` keeps no copy at rest and so has nothing to wipe. **Nothing is removed before it is counted:** from the moment a device records a forbidding policy for an account's server, a wipe is owed for that copy (a mark at rest outside the copy, which holds nothing but which copy), and only this run removes anything: not a composition of the vault, not the next opening, not the content's own stores. What waits, what the device holds as refused, what is parked with a held vault, saves kept to be finished with their set-aside files, and unfinished moves of everything are all part of the copy and of the count. **A kept policy record that cannot be read is not a forbidding policy:** nothing is deleted on its account, and the copy stays closed (neither opened nor deleted) until the policy has been read from the server, and the person is told why. **The attempt is the browser's:** each request with the kept session of the copy's own account, checked at that request; sixty seconds in all and thirty for each request; one pass, no second try, no pull; whether the server was stopped is asked before each request; what the device holds as refused, and what is parked with a held vault, is not sent and counts as not sent; each kept save is finished as one more change of the pass (its files first, then its item), without the full drain and without conflict copies, and one not finished when the attempt ends, or when a lock takes the keys it needs, counts as not sent; a request that left the device and got no answer in time is "may or may not have arrived", one that never left it (a connection never made) is not sent, and a save in flight when the copy closed for the wipe is among those that may or may not have arrived. **The count** is taken from the copy before anything is removed; a number that is known is never hidden behind "cannot tell" (where some changes are known lost and kept records cannot be read, the notice says at least so many were not sent and that more cannot be told; whether such records exist is found out without opening them); moves of everything that were not finished get a number of their own, because the person must learn that a move stands half-done at the server. **From the moment the wipe is owed until it is made, nothing new is taken into that copy:** a change is refused with a line that says why, and what the person typed stays on the screen; the screens can tell "the copy is being removed" from "this server is stopped", and a change to a shared vault refused meanwhile carries the same line. **One lock for every wipe:** a sign-out, forgetting the server, an account deletion, the switch that keeps data on the device turned off, the settling at a start and the policy's run take the same lock for that account at that server (device-wide, accepted: one account is open at a time); a sign-out, a forgetting or a deletion that waits for the run is recorded durably first, so it is finished at the next start if the app stops meanwhile. **A lock during the attempt** does not end it where what remains is already sealed: it goes on with the session alone and touches no key; a step that would need the account's keys after the lock is not made and counts as not sent; no key outlives a lock for the attempt's sake. **Stopped, held up or lifted:** the mark makes the next opening run the attempt again from the start; a wipe that could not be made keeps the mark, is tried again at each start on the policy the device keeps (accepted for a device, where a start often has no network: it only removes a copy already counted and never opened again), and is never said as made; a policy that allows the copy again ends the mark and its notice. A forbidding policy recorded on a live session that the server then ends while the device is locked is wiped at the next opening (accepted); an owed wipe follows a followed move to the server's new address. **The notice** is in what the screens are handed, like the notice that a recovery kit is gone: the server's name and numbers only (falling back to its address where the server was forgotten), shown when the copy is gone, readable on the lock screen too, taken away only by the person's dismissal; it belongs to its account: its record goes when the account is deleted on the device and when the server is forgotten, it survives a sign-out, and it is shown and dismissed only where no other account is signed in on the device, or inside its own account. **What goes besides the copy:** the account's wrapped keys at rest and the quick-unlock key; the session's tokens and the public record of the account's identity stay, so the account stays signed in and works from the server; an unlock proves the password against the keys the server serves, as a sign-in does, and without the server there is no unlock, which the lock screen says. **A lock never drops changes that wait:** on a device that keeps nothing at rest (a desktop without "Keep me signed in", or any device under this policy after its wipe), changes that wait stay in memory, sealed, through a lock, until they are sent or the app ends (they need no key to be sent), and ending the app over them asks first, with their number, as a sign-out does; the number the app can ask for before it ends counts everything that waits in memory, kept saves and move records among it, and answers "cannot be told" while locked where such records may exist. Turning off the switch that keeps data on the device carries what was at rest into memory, and the opening that follows reads it, so that a save a lock cut short is finished and counted. **With the server out of reach there is no unlock under such a policy** (rules of 2026-11-19, which replace the one try at the first unlock with the server out of reach): the account's wrapped keys and the quick-unlock key leave the device at the moment the policy is recorded, on every road, and every unlock asks the server for them (nothing derived from the password is sent; the keys are opened on the device), so nothing is tried, nothing is wiped and nothing is lost until an unlock the server answers, and the one try and the wipe come then. A device kept offline keeps a copy that nothing on the device can open. The owner decided so on 2026-11-19 (§0.6; §19, question 9). With it (rules of 2026-11-19): a record whose keys were at the server gets no wrapped key back at rest until a readable policy that allows it is kept (so an unreadable kept policy record and an unlock that got the keys but no answer to the policy's read write nothing back, and a caught login never opens offline with the master password alone); the mark that a wipe is owed is written before the keys are removed, or survives a failure of that removal; a quick-unlock enrolment whose prompt was on show when the policy was recorded looks again before it writes a key; a server's "too many requests" or "forbidden" at the keys' fetch removes nothing and is said as what it is. The same holds on every road that takes a session (a sign-in, a recovery with the kit, the operator's help, a device link's pickup take it through one place; a registration over another account's record is refused; a refresh writes no keys), and a record marked as having its keys at the server is stripped of any wrap when it is written at rest, so a password change answered after a lock leaves no key material at rest (the race in which a pending wrap is written into a stripped record is covered by the same strip by reading only). Kept since 2026-11-19 (spec 03 §1.1). The account's state document stays: it is sealed under the account's key, which is no longer on the device, and its counters protect the next unlock against an older state. **The mark that a wipe is owed is written at the moment the policy is recorded**, not after the hop to the main thread, so a process that ends in between still owes the wipe. **What is said, besides the above** (rules of 2026-11-19): "never left" is never said of a request that may have left (on Android a failure that carries an earlier attempt that was sent is "may have left"; spec 05 R11 says the other direction); a save the server stored is never said as not made (where the removal begins after the answer to the batch that carried it, it is made), and a save stopped before it was ever queued is not made; while the copy is being removed nothing reads as "this server is stopped" (a read refused during the removal does not turn the state to stopped), every act on a shared vault refuses with the removal's line, and the apps say that line instead of hiding "New"; after the run, a wipe of the person's own that waited is followed by a redraw. The shared code answers whether a wipe is owed for an account at a server, so that the desktop app no longer deletes the copy by itself. On an iPhone a request never reports that it did not leave, so there an unreachable server's changes read as "may or may not have arrived".
    - **Logins the Android app has caught and not yet answered** (decided on 2026-11-18). A capture that waits for the person's answer is not yet a change, on the phone as in the extension: it stays sealed on the phone until the person answers it (saving it, which sends it, or discarding it), also under a policy that forbids keeping data, and also when it is caught after such a policy came into force, while andvari is locked. The policy's wipe does not remove it, the one attempt does nothing special with it, and the notice counts nothing for it; the catch says what every catch says ("kept for andvari"), never "your server doesn't allow…". Nothing removes a caught login but the person's answer, a sign-out with its usual count and confirmation, or the removal of the account from the phone. At an unlock the list is read as without a policy (what needs no answer is saved and sent, what needs one is asked), and a caught login that can no longer be opened is said and counted, never dropped without a word. The list is bounded as it is without a policy, and the settings screen says under such a policy that logins andvari catches are kept sealed on the phone until they are answered. What stays at rest for it, and nothing more: the sealed entries, the public half of the key they are sealed to (so that a catch can be sealed while andvari is locked), and what lets the secret half be had again at an unlock the server answers. **The condition comes first:** this holds only where a caught login on a phone under such a policy cannot be opened without an unlock that the server answers (the phone keeps no wrapped keys of the account after the wipe); if that does not hold, none of it is built.
    - **Kept (2026-11-19), in the shared code:** the mark written at once on the thread that recorded the policy, under a lock of its own; nothing removed before it is counted (the queue, what is held as refused, what is parked with a held vault and the kept saves; a part that cannot be read is "cannot tell", and a known number is never hidden behind it); an opening over an owed copy opens it halted and refuses a change with its line; the one attempt with the copy's own session, its limits and its three outcomes; "never left" never said of a request that may have left; a save the server stored is made, one stopped before it was queued is not; no stopped-server state during the removal, and the removal's line on every act on a shared vault; one lock for every wipe, a wipe of the person's own that waits behind the run kept durably and followed by a redraw; the notice per account with its numbers, until dismissed. An edit of an item that already had an earlier change waiting, stopped before it was queued (behind a running sync or its files' uploads), is said as not made: the item engine says whether it queued the change (a limit of 2026-11-19, closed that day). **Kept since 2026-11-19:** the wrapped keys and the quick-unlock key leave the device and every unlock asks the server, so with the server out of reach nothing is tried and nothing is wiped; caught logins stay sealed, no longer go with the copy, and after a removal are sent once, never twice in the vault, and never said as gone; kept saves with files are finished by the attempt; unfinished moves have a number of their own, the next opening reads what the switch-off carried into memory, and an owed wipe follows a followed move; during the removal the vault has a line of its own and a change then is refused with one; both apps draw the notice and the removal's line, and the desktop leaves the removal of the copy to the shared code. **Kept since 2026-11-19 besides:** the shared code answers the apps whether a removal is still owed for the session's own account, and the desktop reads that answer, never its disk; when the desktop's switch "Keep me signed in" goes off, by the policy once the removal is done or by the person's untick, the desktop removes every file in its cache folder, which holds one account's files at a time, since it refuses a second sign-in until the kept account is signed out; both apps draw the lock screen's line that the keys are kept at the server; neither app says at the server's end of the session that the copy opens read-only (the desktop says in its own line that nothing here opens the vault until the person signs in again and that nothing was removed); an unlock the server did not answer is said on both apps, in their own words, as the server not answering; at an opening that records a newly forbidding policy the attempt does not send the changes of the opening's own request still out beside it: it counts them by that request's answer and gives them its one try only where that request ended without an answer (spec 03 §1.1). **Kept since 2026-11-19:** the shared code hands the apps a line for each of three states under such a policy, and both apps draw it as handed, with no line of their own (rules of 2026-11-19, spec 03 §1.1): an unlock the server did not answer (nothing opened or removed; the password could not be checked, because only the server can; try again when it can be reached; a right and a wrong password read the same), the server's end of the session (this device keeps no key and no copy that opens the account, so nothing opens until the person signs in again; nothing removed; the caught logins stay sealed until then; never that a copy opens; a line of its own beside a way out that signs the kept account out first), and the first unlock that meets that end (answered as that end at once: the keeper asks for the end first). **Not kept yet (2026-11-19):** a refusal or a "too many requests" at the read of the keys is said as a server that did not answer, because the session keeper hands no refusal there (spec 03 §1.1). A login caught before the removal and saved at its opening is said as saved (kept in the shared code since 2026-11-19: the removal carries the list of what that opening saved, in memory only). `andvari-cli` reads the policy only for its hashing cost. The condition of the bullet on caught logins holds by the construction of 2026-11-19 (no wrapped key and no sealed seed of the account stay on the phone, the master password opens nothing there, and a restart without the server answers that it cannot be reached), built since 2026-11-19 (shown at the phone's own level by a walk over the real engine since 2026-11-19) for every state but one, which held only since 2026-11-20: **a session that ends while the account is open.** Every end of the session (the server's, by a refused renewal or the live channel's word that the device was revoked, and this device's own, where it could not confirm the session and no try is left) leaves in the session keeper's memory exactly what it keeps at rest, as a lock does, so no wrapped key of the account stays and the master password opens nothing, and no caught login opens or is said as saved, until an unlock the server answers after the person signs in again; also with the policy recorded while the account was open, and after a restart. Without such a policy the copy at rest still opens read-only with the password after the server's end. Before 2026-11-20 such an end left the served keys in memory until the app's next start, so with the server silent the master password opened the account and a login caught after the end (spec 05 R11). **Not kept yet (2026-11-20):** an account held as its server's operator and the record a sign-in call that was left puts back still keep the served keys in memory; every unlock refuses the first before it touches a key, and no sequence is known in which the second opens anything; each is to be made what is kept at rest.
- **No credential goes to an origin without a pin in force.** A bearer token, a refresh token, a logout, and anything derived from the master password are sent only to an origin whose pin is in force on this device. With no pin record at an origin, only the requests of a first pin go out (the card chain and what the Trust Gate needs), and they carry no credential. A kept session whose origin holds no pin record (a move that stopped half-way, a record that was lost) is shown as a blocked server that can be forgotten: it does not unlock, it dials nothing, and signing it out ends it on the device without telling the server. This is enforced in the password rule and where the client dials, as the stop for a fork is.
- **What a device remembers of an account, and an account made again under the same address** (decided on 2026-11-17; spec 01 §5 and §7, spec 05 R19(c)). A device remembers, for an address at a server, which account it is (its id and its identity key), and that memory outlives a sign-out on purpose: it is what stops a server from handing the device another account under a known address. It also means that when a person deletes their account and makes a new one under the same address, every device that knew the earlier account refuses the new one. The way out:
  - **Where a device refuses because an address is known on this device under another account or key** (the identity fault, at a sign-in, a registration, a recovery with the kit or with the operator's help, or a device link, wherever a flow's own comparison or the session keeper says it), **the refusal says both possibilities in plain words:** the account under this address was deleted and made again, and this device still remembers the earlier one; or the server is not giving this device the account it knows, and then something is wrong with the server and nothing should be forgotten. And it says how to tell: the person knows whether they, or the operator with their knowledge, deleted the account and made it again.
  - **It offers "Forget the earlier account on this device", behind a second confirmation.** Never done by the device by itself, never preselected, never something a server or an operator can set off: only the person, on that device. The second confirmation says what will happen: this device forgets the earlier account under this address; if it still keeps a copy of that account, the copy is deleted, and the confirmation first says how many of its changes were not sent, or that it cannot tell (through the same count a sign-out uses, above); it cannot be undone; and if the account was NOT deleted and made again, forgetting means trusting whatever account the server serves under this address from now on.
  - **The offer is for another account only** (rules of 2026-11-19, which narrow "another account or key" above to its meaning): it is made only where the account the server serves now is another account, by its id, than the one this device ties the address to. Where the server serves the SAME account under another identity key, nothing is offered and nothing can be forgotten: that is the substitution the record exists to stop, the refusal stays the identity fault, and an account deleted and made again never comes back under its old id. Every place that raises the offer knows the served account's id or raises none; a refusal whose cause the device cannot tell apart raises none. Where the device holds a record of the served account's own identity key and the served key differs from it, nothing is offered whatever the address is tied to; that comparison comes first. The offer and its confirmation are refused once the address is tied to the account the server serves. The old account served again under its own key while the address is tied to the new one gets the offer to forget the new one (someone who forgot by mistake finds the way back). No plate that says not to enter the password again stands above an offer.
  - **The forgetting keeps the record of the earlier account's identity key** (rule of 2026-11-19): its id and public key, nothing secret, kept with the server's records, so that it goes when the server is forgotten and with a deletion of that account its server confirmed. The earlier account then never comes back on this device under another key, also after a forgetting: a server that first shows another account under the address, to obtain the forgetting, and then serves the old account with a key of its own is refused at that second step. The confirmation's last sentence stays as it is. Changes to shared vaults that the earlier account signed and did not send go with the forgetting and are counted in what the confirmation says, or it says that it cannot tell; a failed write of the device's own record is not answered "forgotten"; a tab or a process that has the account open holds a lock for it, and a forgetting is refused while that lock is held (the web client keeps this since 2026-11-19 with a lock of the browser's own; a browser without one forgets nothing; a tab that opens the account during another tab's forgetting is not held back, spec 01 §7).
  - **Forgetting removes the tie and the copy:** the device's record that ties the address at that server to the earlier account, and the earlier account's copy on this device if one is kept, with everything else the device keeps of that account (below), and not the record of its identity key (above). It leaves everything else: other accounts on the device, and above all what the device knows of the server (its card, its pin), so the server is still checked as before. Nothing is sent to the server by the forgetting itself.
  - **The guards** (rules of 2026-11-17). **A flow under way for that address blocks it from its first step to its end:** a sign-in through its two-step code; a registration from its ticket; both recoveries from their start to their commit (a kit that was checked and not yet committed included); a device link from its start to its pickup; a request for the operator's help for as long as the device keeps it. The forgetting then answers that something is under way and removes nothing. **It is judged by the account, not by the address alone:** where what it would remove belongs to an account the device still keeps or has open, under this address or another one of the same server (after a followed move), it removes nothing and answers that the account is open here; it never takes the identity record of a kept account. Where no record ties the address to an account but a session for it is kept, the answer is "nothing to forget", and nothing is removed. **It never touches an account the device holds as its server's operator:** that account has its own way out (the person removes it from the device), and the offer leaves it in place and says so. **Afterwards nothing of the forgotten account is left on the device,** every store of it included (the account's own store and its mirror among them, deleted without condition), so that a server which later serves the same account id finds nothing to reuse.
  - **After it, the address is new to the device:** the next sign-in, registration, recovery or link under it is taken as on a device that never knew the address, and the person is told once that this device now remembers the new account.
  - Every client that keeps this memory offers it: the Android, desktop and iPhone apps, the browser and the extension (each in its own store), and `andvari-cli` as a command.
  - **A hold has no time limit** (rule of 2026-11-18): a flow that is dropped without its cancel holds the forgetting back until the app is started again, which is the safer reading; the app interface is to call the flows' cancels (and the call that leaves a sign-up) wherever it drops a flow, so that the hold ends with the flow. **Accepted as they are** (2026-11-18): a server can keep a request for the operator's help, and with it the hold, until the person cancels it; a forgetting at an old address of an account can land while a flow under its new address runs, and it then removes what a forgetting just before the flow would have removed.
  - **What else goes, and how the screens walk it** (rules of 2026-11-18; spec 01 §7 has each point). Everything the device keeps for that server under the earlier account's user id goes with the forgetting (contacts it confirmed, code and link records, checkpoints, the quick-unlock offer, recovery requests, the record that a kit was shown, a session record that cannot be read), decided kind by kind so that a kind added later is never left behind; a sign-out keeps them, since there the identity record stays and guards them. A forgetting that was begun is finished, by a lasting mark written before it and removed only after it; a second tap answers "forgotten". The confirmation names every copy that will go, with "cannot be told" for what it cannot count; a lock between the two steps takes the confirmation back; after "forgotten" the sign-in stands at its password step; the line for a flow under way where a flow is on show says to leave that flow first, not to try again in a moment. Where a store fails part-way, the second step stays on show for the person's next tap and the app never tries again by itself. No offer stands beside a kit: after a commit refused for the account's identity, the kit's screen comes first, and the offer comes at the next sign-in that meets the same refusal (accepted). A person whose device still keeps the earlier account, signed out by its server, meets two refusals and two confirmations, first the sign-out of that copy, then the forgetting; each is for one thing that is lost (accepted). **Kept (2026-11-19):** the app interface does all of this but the drawing: it raises the offer only on the session keeper's own refusal for an address tied to another account, with both possibilities, the second step that names every copy, the lasting mark, the deletion kind by kind, and the notice at the next sign-in under that address (its mark lives in memory, so a restart before that sign-in loses the one-line notice); the shared code removes a session record it cannot read where it names the earlier account or does not read at all, and keeps one that names another account.
  - **Kept (2026-11-18):** the shared code has the forgetting with its guards (spec 01 §7 says what it removes and what it leaves): each flow holds it from its first step to its end, it is judged by the account's id, it answers "open for that account" and removes nothing for an account held as its server's operator, and it sends nothing; setting aside a request for the operator's help at the step that confirms its target ends its hold (2026-11-18); the app interface hands the offer to the screens and ends a flow's hold wherever it drops the flow (2026-11-19); both apps draw the offer, its two steps and the notice (2026-11-19); at a device link the offer is raised only where the shared code's stop names the address, which it names only for another account id (2026-11-19). **The web client keeps all of this bullet list since 2026-11-19** (its own implementation, spec 01 §7): the offer only for another account and never against a kept key record of the served account, the record of the earlier account's key kept, the held changes counted, no plate above the offer, a failed write not "forgotten", a forgetting cut short finished at the next start, and the notice afterwards kept until dismissed and drawn in every open tab. **The extension's records** (rules of 2026-11-19, spec 01 §7; kept since 2026-11-19, before which the extension kept no record that ties an address to an account): per server, which account each address is and each account's identity key, the key kept in one fixed form and compared as decoded bytes; the password sign-in, its code step and the quick unlock refuse the same account under another key, and the sign-in another account under a known address; a read that fails refuses and takes and writes nothing; a record is written only where none exists, and any value under a record's name counts as one; the device's own record is written after the checks and before the first request after the login; the records outlive every sign-out, lock and "Remove data for this server", and go only with "Forget this server"; the first sign-in after the update is trust on first use. **Its way out** (rules of 2026-11-19; kept since 2026-11-19): "Forget the earlier account on this device" in the popup, at a password sign-in and at its code step only, with the second step taken only from a press, or a click with no press, a full second after the confirmation stood (so a double click or a held key passes nothing) and the worker kept awake while the confirmation stands, the key's record kept, the mark of the notice kept at rest, every sign-in and quick unlock under way ended, and the confirmation saying that a login saved while locked goes to the account that signs in next; its costs are those of every client (spec 01 §7). The same guard stands on the second step of the popup's "Forget this server" where logins or cards were caught and not saved (kept since 2026-11-19). **Kept since 2026-11-19 in the shared code:** the keeper tells the two causes apart; the offer only for another account on every road, at a device link once and only at its end where the stop names the address and another account is served; the forgetting held to the account the confirmation named; a cut forgetting finished; the key's record kept, and counted as something of another account at a deletion (spec 01 §7); the mark for the notice afterwards kept at rest with the server's records, carried when the server's address moves, gone with the server's forgetting and with a deletion of the account under that address, and the notice raised again after a restart until dismissed; the confirmation counts the earlier account's signed changes to shared vaults that were never sent (which the forgetting also removes) also where no copy of that account is held, says how many or that it cannot tell, and a change in that count while it is on show makes a new confirmation. Closed on 2026-11-19: both apps draw the offer at a device link's end; the web client holds a lock while a tab has the account open and refuses a forgetting meanwhile; `andvari-cli` has its command, `forget-earlier-account` (spec 01 §7).
- **A namespace's trust records move with its pin.** When a move is followed, everything the device keeps per origin for deciding what to trust (the account-state high-water marks, vault records and checkpoints, the device record) is carried to the new origin with the pin, and the old namespace is removed. Following a move never resets a mark. The target of a move is checked (its origin grammar, and that this device can keep a pin for it) before either pin is touched.
- **A trust record that cannot be read is not an empty one.** A failed read of a pin, a high-water mark or a vault record is "unknown": the client keeps what the record guards closed, and does not write the record back from what the server serves.
- **A device link's `pin_mismatch` removes only the pin that link made** in its step 0, never a pin the device already had.
- **A pin record that fails to decode is `damaged`** and blocks the origin. It is never read as "no pin".

The first pin comes from exactly one of five roots. The sixth row, the device link, only confirms a pin made by another root (T2-B1):

| Root | When | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| **Baked** | the store apps' "Create an account on `andvari`", and any connection to `PUBLIC_INSTANCE.origin` | card 0 of the served chain must hash to `PUBLIC_INSTANCE.card0Hash`, and `instanceId` must match |
| **Link offer** | opening a connect or register link (signed by an account, §6.3) | the served chain must contain a card with `H = offer.cardHash` and `instanceId = offer.instanceId`; the newest verified card is pinned. **An enrol link is not a root.** Its offer is minted by the host, so it passes the typed-address Trust Gate, and the pin it leaves records root `enrol`, which is not a password pin (FI-B8). At the baked origin it takes the baked root instead, and the pin's root is `baked` (below). |
| **Device link** | a device added through §8 | **confirms** a pin, never makes the first one (T2-B1). The new device pins the link's origin before it claims (§8.2 step 0); the bundle's card checkpoint, authenticated under the account's UVK (§8.3, T-R9), must then agree with that pin, or the device signs out. |
| **Setup** | the first operator, through a setup link | the app creates card 0 itself (§6.7) and pins it |
| **Kit** | recovering on a fresh device by scanning a recovery kit, **only** on Sign in → Forgot your master password? → Scan your recovery kit (§10, FS-B1) | card 0 of the served chain must hash to the kit's `card0Hash`. A kit made before an operator reset (§4.6) does not match. It falls back to the Trust Gate with the warning "This server's identity changed since your kit was made. Continue only if you know why" (N2). |
| **Typed address** | "Use your own server" with a hand-typed origin (an enrol link passes the same gate but records root `enrol`, above) | trust on first use. The Trust Gate shows the origin, "It calls itself “*name*” — nobody you know has vouched for it", and the operator key's 32-hex short form for anyone who wants to check. Connect pins the newest card. |

**At the baked origin the baked root always comes first.** On a build that bakes a public instance, a first pin at `PUBLIC_INSTANCE.origin` is made through the baked root whatever started the connection: a typed sign-in, the first screen's "andvari" choice, or a link of any kind. The baked checks run first, in §2.4's order (`origin_mismatch`, `card0_mismatch`, `instance_mismatch`; an empty chain is `withheld`), and the pin records root `baked`, which is a password pin: it is the build, not the link, that vouches for the instance. A link then adds its own check on top:
- a connect or register link: the chain must also contain the offer's `cardHash` under its `instanceId` (`card_not_in_chain`, `instance_mismatch`);
- an enrol link: nothing more. No Trust Gate is shown, and the pin's root is `baked`, not `enrol`;
- a setup link: refused. An empty chain is `withheld` under the baked root, so nothing is signed, and a served chain that passes the baked checks is `already_set_up` (one that fails them is `card0_mismatch` or `instance_mismatch`, as for any link). A build that bakes an instance never signs that instance's card 0: the instance was set up before any build could carry its card's hash.

A link to any other origin is unaffected by what the build bakes.

**Under an existing pin at the baked origin** the pin is compared first, as always (a root never replaces a pin). The baked checks then run on the same chain: when they pass, the pin's root becomes `baked`; a pinned chain whose card 0 is not the baked one is `card0_mismatch`, and the device does not use that instance until the person forgets the server.

**Password rule (T2-B1).** A client sends a value derived from an account's secrets (prelogin for its email, `authKey` or `currentAuthKey`, `recoveryAuthKey`, a TOTP code) only to:
- the origin of a session this device already holds **for that account** (re-authentication, password change, recovery settings); or
- for signing in, linking a device or recovering, an origin with a **password pin** for that purpose:
  - a pin this device made through the **baked** root or the **Trust Gate** counts for any account;
  - a **kit** pin counts only for recovering the kit's own account (the email in the kit), and a **setup** pin (this device signed card 0) only for the operator account it enrolled (FS-B1). Signing in to any other account there, or linking a device there, shows the Trust Gate first. A kit pin is for the recovery alone: a later sign-in through it, even to the kit's own account, also shows the Trust Gate. The kit vouches for one thing, that this is the instance the kit was made on, and for one act; entering the master password at a server is a separate decision that the person makes at the gate. A setup pin does allow signing in to the operator account it enrolled, and recovering that account with its kit. **Creating** an account (`register`) proceeds with any pin for the origin, a kit or setup pin included, since nothing derived from an existing account's secrets is sent. Assisted recovery through a kit or a setup pin shows the Trust Gate. A re-authentication with no session for that account at that origin is refused at the entry: there is nothing to re-authenticate. A device keeps at most one pin record per origin.

A pin that came only from someone else's **link offer**, or from an **enrol link**, is enough to *create* an account on that instance, never to sign in to an existing one. Signing in through such a pin shows the Trust Gate first, after which it is a password pin.

Every screen that asks for an existing account's master password names the account and the instance: "Master password for *email* on *name* (*origin*)".

The rule is one pure core function (`PasswordGate`, in the core engine), used by every native client and the CLI. `passwordgate.json` (§17) pins its decisions. In the CLI the Trust Gate is the printed origin, name and operator key with a confirmation, or `--trust-card <card0Hash>` for scripts. It closes the relay in which an attacker's server mints a device link, receives the password-derived `authKey` and replays it at the real instance (§8.2).

**On every policy probe or sync** the client fetches the chain when its ETag changed, and verifies forward from the pinned card.

- **Fork.** A chain that does not contain the pinned hash at the pinned `seq` makes the client **stop using the instance**: no sign-in, sync or writes. It says: "This server's identity changed in a way andvari can't verify. Don't continue. If you trust the operator, ask them what happened." The only way on is "Forget this server" (§12.3).
- **Withheld.** A chain that ends below the pinned `seq` (a stale cache, or a server hiding an update) does not stop anything. The client keeps its newer pinned card, shows "This server is showing an older version of its identity card", and retries on the next probe.

**A move:** when the newest verified card's `origin` differs from the namespace's, the app shows "*name* (*old origin*) moved to *new origin*, signed by its operator". One tap moves the namespace, with no Trust Gate.
- **After following a move the app asks the person to make a new recovery kit**, because the kit names the address it was made for.
