- **An address the chain has ever named must stay the operator's.** The chain is public, so whoever later controls an old address (a lapsed domain, a reused dynamic-DNS or tunnel name) can serve the genuine chain cut off before the move. A device that never saw the move still trusts that address: a sign-in there can be relayed, a session token sent there can be used, and a recovery kit made before the move would be accepted there and its proof replayed at the real instance. No client check can tell a cut-off chain from a current one. The self-hosting guide says this in its section on moving, and it is recorded as residual R36.
- **Store builds never follow a card-driven move of the public instance** (T-R7). Its origin is baked, and a move of the public instance ships as an app update. They show "This server says it moved. Update andvari."
- **Wherever a card's name is shown, its origin is shown beside it** (T-R8). A look-alike instance that calls itself "andvari" is still visibly another address.

**A `kind` change** (T2-Rec). `kind` decides which recovery copy and rules apply (§7.7), so a verified card that changes it is never followed silently.
- Store builds never follow a `kind` change of the baked public instance (`PUBLIC_INSTANCE.kind`, §0.3). They show "This server says it changed. Update andvari."
- For any other instance the client shows the Trust Gate again, naming the change (§12.3).
- Until the person confirms, the client keeps using the old `kind`.

### 4.5 Uses

**Recovery consent lives with the account, not the server (T-B4).** The person's recovery choice is recorded in UVK-sealed account state, mirrored in device state:

```
recovery = { posture: "private" | "operator", fp: <hex64 the person consented to> | none, at }
```

- It is written only by an explicit choice: at sign-up (§7.7), in Settings, or on re-consent (below).
- The server's `users.recovery` is a mirror the server uses for its own route checks. **Clients never decide anything from a served posture.**
- A client seals an escrow blob (0x02) only when **all** of these hold:
  1. its account-state record says `operator`;
  2. the pinned card's `recoveryFp` equals the consented `fp`, or follows from it by a chain of valid rotation statements (next bullet);
  3. the served recovery public key hashes to that `recoveryFp` at full length.

  No human types a fingerprint, and no served value can turn a private account into an escrowed one.
- **First consent (FS-R2, FC-2).** When a person turns operator recovery on, at sign-up or later in Settings, the client first walks the served card chain **from card 0** to the newest card, **whatever its pin root**. Every change of `recoveryFp` along it must be matched by a stored 0x08 rotation from the old value to the new one, or a chain of them that leads from the old value to the new one (a card may jump from A to C while the served rotations go A to B and B to C). If any change is not, the choice shows the unsigned-change warning below and preselects "Only me".
  - **What anchors card 0, per pin root:**
    - baked: `PUBLIC_INSTANCE.card0Hash`;
    - kit: the kit's `card0Hash`;
    - setup: this device signed card 0;
    - link offer: the offer's `cardHash`, which the creator's device verified and which fixes every earlier card through `prev`, so card 0 is as well anchored as the creator's own pin;
    - enrol and Trust Gate: nothing beyond the chain the server served at first use.
  - **What the walk cannot detect:** an enrol or Trust Gate pin made on a server that was **already** compromised. Such a server can serve a chain fabricated from card 0 that names its own recovery key from the start, and no walk finds an unsigned change in it (R22). Everyone whose card 0 is anchored before the swap is warned: baked, kit, setup and link-offer pins, and enrol or Trust Gate pins made before it.
  - A server whose operator really lost the old seed shows this warning to every later opt-in, for good; the copy says to ask the operator why.
- **Own mirror (T2-R-e).** A device seals an escrow, or re-seals one after a rotation, **without asking** only when its **own device-state mirror** of the recovery record agrees with the account-state record.
  - The mirror is set on this device only by the person's own choice made there (sign-up, Settings, re-consent), or by a verified re-seal made there.
  - **A device link does not carry it (FS-R1).** A linked device asks once, like a password-only one. The bundle's `accountState` checkpoint only refuses rollbacks (§8.3). It is not a mirror, because the linking device may itself hold a replayed state it never confirmed.
  - A device without such a mirror, such as one signed in with only the password, seals nothing on its own. When a seal is due it asks: "Your account lets the operator of *name* (*origin*) restore it. Keep that?" with [Not now] (the default: nothing is sealed, and it asks again at the next unlock), [Keep operator recovery] and [Turn it off]. Both choices need the master password (`PUT /account/recovery`, §7.7), and then the mirror is set.
  - Without this rule, a server could serve a password-only device an old account state whose record still says `operator`, from before the person turned it off. A rotation signed by whoever holds the recovery seed would then make that device re-seal the UVK unasked.
- **Under variant R** (§7.7) a client never seals an escrow for an instance whose pinned card has `kind = public`, whatever `recoveryFp` and the account state say.

**Rotation of the recovery key.** A card that changes `recoveryFp` does **not** by itself cause any re-seal.
- **Signed rotation (normal case).** The operator runs `recovery-cli rotate` offline with the **outgoing** recovery seed. It produces a 0x08 statement, `{"v":2,"purpose":"recovery-rotation","instanceId":<uuid>,"fromFp":<hex64>,"toFp":<hex64>,"issuedAt":<ms>}`, signed by the outgoing seed's Ed25519 key (§2.3). The server serves it beside the card chain.
  - A client whose consented `fp` is `fromFp` verifies the signer against the key `fromFp` names, updates its consent to `toFp`, and re-seals at its next unlock (only with its own mirror; otherwise it asks, as above).
  - The re-seal uses **`PUT /api/v1/escrow/self {sealed, fingerprint, rotations: [<b64 0x08 frame> …]}`**, which needs a session but no password. `rotations` is the chain from the fingerprint of the account's current escrow to `fingerprint`, oldest first, because consent may follow more than one rotation (FI-R1). The server stores the new escrow only when every link verifies in order and the last `toFp` equals `fingerprint` (otherwise `409 escrow_not_due`).
  - A server compromise cannot forge a rotation: the seed is offline.
- **Unsigned change** (the outgoing seed is lost, or someone else signed the card). No silent re-seal ever happens. The app shows (T2-R-f):

  > **The recovery key for *name* (*origin*) changed without the old key's signature.**
  >
  > This can mean the operator lost the old key. It can also mean someone has taken over this server's identity. Keep operator recovery only if the operator has told you why it changed.
  >
  > [ Turn off operator recovery ] *(default)* [ Keep it with the new key ] [ Later ]

  - Both changes need the **master password** (`PUT /account/recovery`, §7.7). [Later] changes nothing and seals nothing; the prompt returns at the next unlock.
  - A thief of the operator key can publish such a card. Every opted-in person then sees this prompt, which defaults to off and names the possibility of a takeover (R30).

`escrowStale` and `escrowNeedsV2` are deleted.

**The display name** comes from the card, always shown with the origin (§4.4). It is marked "(as the server names itself)" for typed-address pins.

### 4.6 Losing the operator (R10)

The card advances only with the operator's signing key. If the operator account is lost (master password **and** kit, and no signed-in device left), the instance can never sign another card. That means no move, no rename, no recovery-key rotation, and no handover. The only way on:

1. On the host, `andvari admin reset-instance-identity` starts a **new card chain with the same instance id**. **In the same transaction** it demotes the old operator account to member, **deletes the stored cards and rotations**, and mints a new setup link (FC-4). Until the new card 0 exists, `GET /instance` returns the `setup` block (§4.3), so the setup link's "no cards" check passes (§6.7), and every other registration gets `409 setup_incomplete` (§7.3). Setup needs only that no operator account exists (§7.3, N2). A new operator account enrols through the link and signs the new card 0. Accounts and vaults are untouched. Every account's genesis still names the same instance id (§5.2), so unlocking keeps working.
2. Every device that pinned the old chain now sees a **fork** at its pinned position (§4.4) and stops. (While no card is served, it treats the empty chain as withheld and keeps working; the new card 0 is the fork, FC-4.) Each person uses "Forget this server" and reconnects: through a link from someone they trust, or as a typed address (trust on first use). Recovery kits name the old `card0Hash`, so each person makes a new kit afterwards (§10).
3. **For the public instance**, store builds bake the old `card0Hash`. They cannot reach the instance until an app update bakes the new one, so the store builds must be rebuilt and resubmitted.

**Prevention.**
- The operator account's kit is printed twice and stored with the instance recovery sheet.
- A second signed-in device of the operator account (for example `andvari-cli` on an offline machine, §18.2) can sign card updates if the first is lost.
- Operator recovery is never offered to the operator account itself, because it would be circular. Posture `operator` on it is refused with `400 operator_recovery_unavailable`, at setup registration too (S3-C8).

---

## 5. The account genesis record and the personal vault

### 5.1 Template (purpose 0x05)

```
{"v":2,"purpose":"account-genesis","userId":<uuid>,"identityKey":<b64_32>,"instanceId":<uuid>,
 "personalVaultId":<uuid>,"createdAt":<ms>}
```

### 5.2 Rules

- The registering client signs exactly one genesis, at registration. No client API can mint a second one.
- The register body carries the genesis (§7.3).
- The server checks that the signer maps to `identityPub`, that `userId` and `personalVaultId` equal the body's, and that `instanceId` is its own. It stores the genesis in `users.genesis` and serves it in `AccountKeys.genesis`.
- At **every** unlock, after deriving the identity key from the seed, a client requires all of:
  - the genesis parses;
  - the signer maps to the seed-derived key, and `identityKey` equals that key;
  - `userId` equals the session's;
  - `instanceId` equals the pinned card's;
  - the served `personalWrap.vaultId` equals `personalVaultId` (if a wrap is served).

  Each check has its own verdict string, in the order of §2.4 (VR-13), and any failure is the identity security fault (`IDENTITY_MISMATCH` copy), the same as the existing seed-derived key mismatch.
- **Offline unlock (R14)** runs the same checks against the genesis cached with the AccountKeys (spec 02 §8). A cached AccountKeys without a genesis is refused as damaged key material.

### 5.3 The personal vault

- `personalVaultId` names the account's one personal vault forever. Clients never add members to it; the server refuses members on it.
- `PUT /account/personal-wrap` is **deleted** (S3-C2): register carries the account's only personal wrap (§7.3), so the route could only ever refuse. Replacement, re-designation, the designation question, the proposal, CHANGED and UNCHOSEN states, and the `personal` designation in account state are all deleted.
- A served wrap that opens and names `personalVaultId` is in force on **any** device, including a fresh one, with no question.
- A withheld or unopenable wrap leaves "no personal vault" with the anomaly, as today: no default save destination. A cached wrap still stands on a device that has one.

This is safe on fresh devices because exactly one personal wrap ever exists. No superseded wrap can be replayed, and the genesis vault's key has never been given to anyone.

### 5.4 Account-state records and their merge rules (N6)

The UVK-sealed account state (spec 03 §3) carries the records below after the cut-over. Every device merges a served copy with its own on each read and on `409 stale_state`. **The server alone** can only serve an older copy, never a forged one. Any holder of the UVK can write anything, and the rules below limit, but do not prevent, what a wrong record does (R35, T2-R-d).

The document's own `seq`, inside the sealed blob, is the authenticated counter that "older" and "newer" refer to. The `seq` the server serves beside the blob is only its compare-and-set token (FI-B14). `accountstate.json` (§17) pins every merge below for core, the web twin and the extension (FI-R8).

| Record | Shape | Merge rule |
|---|---|---|
| `contacts` | §6.9 | Per `userId`: the newest `verifiedAt` wins, and a tombstone not older than a pin removes it. A different key for the same `userId` is never merged silently (§6.8 step 6): the record keeping the key this device already uses stays, and the other goes to the key-change prompt. `compared` merges as OR between records with the same key: once a comparison matched, it stays matched. A record with a different key starts at `false` (FI-R8). |
| `vaults` | `vaultId → {genesisHash, seq, hash}` (§3.6 (e)) | `genesisHash` is immutable: a copy with another genesis for the same `vaultId` is an anomaly, and the local record stays. Otherwise the higher `seq` wins. The same `seq` with a different `hash` is a **fork** (§3.9). No record for a vault passes (§3.6 (e)). A removed vault keeps its record, so a re-add of the same vault is checked against the same genesis. |
| `accepted` | `vaultId → {owner, addSeq, at, decision: accept \| decline}` (§3.6) | The higher `addSeq` wins per vault. For the same `addSeq`, `decline` wins whatever `at` says (a leave is final for that `add`; a fast clock must not let an `accept` outlive it); between two records with the same decision the newest `at` wins. A record whose `addSeq` is not the `seq` of this account's latest `add` in the verified log answers nothing, so the person is asked (FS-R6, FI-R8).  A device that **creates** a vault writes `{owner: its own account, addSeq: 0, decision: accept}`; `0` stands for the genesis, since no `add` has that position. A device that signs, or verifies, a `leave` or `remove` of this account writes `decline` for the current `addSeq`. Both exist for the extension, which reads `decision` only (§12.2 rule 1). |
| `recovery` | `{posture, fp, at}` (§4.5) | The newest `at` wins. Ties go to `private`. A device acts on an `operator` record only with its own mirror (§4.5, T2-R-e). |

**Document layout (the sealed plaintext).** One JSON object:

```
{"v":2,"userId":<uuid>,"seq":<int>,
 "contacts":{<userId>:<contact>},
 "contactTombstones":{<userId>:<removedAt ms>},
 "vaults":{<vaultId>:{"genesisHash":<hex64>,"seq":<int>,"hash":<hex64>}},
 "accepted":{<vaultId>:{"owner":<uuid>,"addSeq":<int>,"at":<ms>,"decision":"accept"|"decline"}},
 "recovery":{"posture":"private"|"operator","fp":<hex64n>,"at":<ms>}}
```

- `<contact>` is §6.9's record; its `userId` equals its map key. `recovery` is absent until a choice has been recorded.
- A document with another `v`, or whose `userId` is not the account's, is refused whole. A single record that fails its own shape (a contact whose `method` is not `"link"`, a malformed key, an unknown `decision`) is dropped and counted, and the rest of the document is used.
- Writers emit each map's keys in ascending byte order. Readers accept any order.
- **Ties**, which exist only so that every device reaches the same document: two contact records with the same key and the same `verifiedAt` resolve to the larger `label`, then the larger `madeOn`; two `accepted` records with the same `addSeq`, `at` and `decision` resolve to the larger `owner`; two `recovery` records with the same `at` and the same posture resolve to the smaller `fp`. Every such comparison is on the field's text exactly as stored (a `label` is its base64url string), byte by byte.
- **The rest of a merge.** Two documents are merged record by record whatever their `seq` values; the result's `seq` is the larger of the two, and a write then uses that plus one. An unknown key present in both stays as the copy with the larger `seq` has it, and as the local copy has it when the two are equal. A vault record that forks (the same `seq`, another `hash`) keeps the local record and reports the fork (§3.9). A served copy that is a rollback is still merged the same way: a record only ever refuses, and anything it would bring back is removed again by the tombstone the local copy holds. At the caps the newest are kept (`verifiedAt` for contacts, `removedAt` for tombstones), and among equals the smaller `userId`.
- **A record that supersedes another is stamped later than it.** When a device writes a record meant to replace one it holds (a new recovery choice, a contact verified again after it was removed, a tombstone for a contact), it stamps it at `max(its clock, the held record's time + 1)`. Otherwise a device whose clock runs behind would write a choice that loses the merge to the record it was meant to replace, and turning operator recovery off would silently not stick.
- **Edges.** A record that carries a field this build does not know is kept, and the field is preserved as written, as an unknown top-level key is. A document that lacks one of the four maps is read as having that map empty; one whose `seq` is not an `int` is refused whole. Tombstones are applied to the contacts of both copies before the tombstone cap is applied, so a tombstone that the cap then drops has still removed its contact in that merge. "Left" means the vault's `accepted` record says `decline`, and "oldest" is the smallest `at` of that record, then the smaller `vaultId`. A document that still does not fit after every drop is not written (`account_state_full`); a document whose `seq` cannot be advanced is not written either (`account_state_damaged`); in both cases the merged document is still used for reading. The pure merge takes the three limits (contacts, tombstones, sealed size) as parameters, so the vectors can grade it on small documents; the apps pass the constants.

Records written by an older build do not exist (clean break). An unknown key in the document is preserved, never dropped, so a newer build's records survive an older merge. That holds only while the document fits its size cap: when a merged document would exceed it, a device drops unknown keys first, the largest first, before any known record, so that junk written by a holder of the UVK cannot stop every later write. If it still does not fit, the `vaults` and `accepted` records of vaults the account has left go next, oldest first. The `recovery` record is never dropped. The caps on contacts and tombstones are enforced on merge as well as on write. A document whose in-blob `seq` cannot be advanced any further is reported as damaged and nothing is written; a holder of the UVK can do this, as R35 says, and no merge rule prevents it.

**Size (SR-2).** The sealed account state may be up to **1 MiB of base64** (`ACCOUNT_STATE_SEALED_MAX = 1,048,576` characters, about 768 KiB of plaintext). Today's 64 KiB, about 48 KiB of plaintext, would be full at about 140 shared vaults. The new ceiling covers the largest account the limits allow, with about 30% to spare:
- each shared vault adds a `vaults` record (about 207 bytes of JSON) and an `accepted` record (about 141 bytes): about 350 bytes, so the 500-membership limit (§3.7) needs about 175 KB;
- each contact takes at most about 315 bytes (with a 64-byte name), and an account holds **at most 1,000 contacts** (the app refuses a new one beyond that): about 315 KB;
- contact tombstones are capped at 1,000, the oldest dropped first: about 53 KB;
- in all about 541 KB, against 786 KB of room.

The ceiling applies to `GET` and `PUT /account/state`, and to the `accountState` inside `PUT /account/recovery`, whose body cap rises to match. Nothing else carries the blob: the device-link bundle carries only a `{seq, hash}` checkpoint of it (§8.3).

**Vault records are batched (SR-2).** A device writes a new vault's record at once, and later heads at most once every 10 minutes, so an active account does not re-upload the blob on every log entry. A stale record is still a valid lower bound for §3.6 (e). The interval limits the rate; it cancels nothing: a head that was not written because the interval had not passed is written at the first sync, unlock or lock after it has, whether or not another entry arrives. Otherwise an owner who creates a vault and adds its members within ten minutes would leave the record at the genesis for good, and fresh devices and device-link bundles would have nothing to detect a cut-off log against.

---

## 6. Links v2

There are four kinds of link, all in one URL format:

| Kind | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **connect** | Two people pin each other. If the recipient has no account yet, sign-up comes first. |
| **register** | An operator-made invitation to register on an instance that does not allow open sign-up. |
| **setup** | Made by `andvari setup` to enrol the first operator. |
| **enrol** | Made on the host by `andvari admin enrol-link` to register an ordinary member with no inviter (§6.4). |

Device links (§8) use a different path.

### 6.1 URL and QR

```
https://andvaripass.com/l#v2.<O>.<S>.<F>
O = b64url(UTF-8(origin of the instance))        S = b64url(s), s = 32 CSPRNG bytes
F = the creator fingerprint (§2.3, 22 characters), or "-" for host-minted setup and enrol links
```

- **Parsing is total**: null on any malformed input, never a throw.
  - The input may be the whole URL or only the fragment.
  - ASCII white space is stripped first (chat apps wrap lines). White space here means space, tab, LF and CR only: a form feed or a vertical tab is not stripped, so the input is malformed (`bad_link`). The fragment may be given with or without its `#`.
  - The fragment must be exactly four `.`-separated parts: `v2`, `O`, `S`, `F`.
  - `O` must decode to an `origin` (§2.2), and both `O` and `S` must re-encode identically.
  - `S` must decode to exactly 32 bytes.
  - `F` must be `-`, or decode to exactly 16 bytes and re-encode identically.
- **Why `F` is there (T-B6).** It ties the link to the key of the person who made it. Anyone who learns `s` some other way (a web bundle that read it, a compromised join page, the server) cannot swap in an offer under their own key: the recipient's app requires the offer's `fromKey` to match `F` (§6.8).
  - Total length is at most 512.
- The host and path before `#` are **not** authenticated: any page could carry the fragment. Apps act only on the fragment.
- **The QR** carries the full URL string, in byte mode at error-correction level M.
- **The join page** at `https://andvaripass.com/l` is static HTML on a static host, under CSP `default-src 'none'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self'`. It runs **no script** and reads no fragment. It shows "Open this invite in the andvari app" with the store badges and "If you just installed andvari, tap the link again". The same rules apply to `/d`, whose page says instead: "To add a device, open andvari on it, choose **Sign in → Link from your other device**, and scan this code there." `/d` is not an app link, so a tapped `/d` link only ever reaches this page (§8.2 step 0, T2-B1).
- **Pasting.** Every app, phones included, also accepts a pasted link: "Paste a link" sits beside "Scan" (§12.1). In-app browsers often swallow universal links.

### 6.2 Derivations

From `s` (§2.3): `linkId`, `offerKey`, `answerKey`, and `registerToken` (register and setup kinds only). The two comparison codes are computed from the two frame hashes (§6.11).

### 6.3 Connect and register offers (purpose 0x06)

```
{"v":2,"purpose":"link-offer","linkId":<b64_16>,"kind":"connect"|"register","origin":<origin>,
 "instanceId":<uuid>,"cardHash":<hex64>,"from":<uuid>,"fromKey":<b64_32>,"name":<name>,
 "commit":<hex64>,"expiresAt":<ms>,"issuedAt":<ms>}
```

- `from` and `fromKey` are the creator's account and identity key.
- `name` is the creator's display name, as they chose it. An answer carries the answerer's the same way (§6.5).
- **A device never invents the name an account goes by in its links** (rule of 2026-11-17; spec 03 §15.1). The name is the person's own word to the people they connect with, signed by the person's key, and the server serves no account its own name for this: it is not the server's to vouch for. Both halves are exact: the server stores the name given at registration and serves it in the operator's list of accounts and in a shared vault's list of members, the caller's own row included; a device never takes its links' name from any of those, since a name read back from the server would be a name the server chose. A device that does not hold it (the account was made on another device) asks the person for it once, before it makes or answers a link, and keeps it; it never fills in the address's first part by itself. Until then nothing is made, answered or sent. The shared code keeps this: making a connect link or an invitation, answering a link, and a connection a sign-in still owes each say that the name is needed, and go on once it is given; the name survives a lock. Both apps ask for it once where the device keeps none, then make the link or connect (2026-11-17), and `andvari-cli` takes it from `--name` or asks for it at a terminal and keeps it for the account on that home, and without either sends nothing of the link (2026-11-17). Where the account is registered with `andvari-cli`, the tool keeps `--name`, or the address's first part when none is given; that stands, since the person registered there and could give a name.
- `cardHash` is the hash of the newest card the creator's device has verified.
- `commit` commits to the creator's code nonce `nonceA` (§2.3). The nonce is revealed only after the answer arrives (§6.11).
- The creator seals it as `Envelope(offerKey, frame, AD = "andvari/v2|link-offer|" + linkId)`, at most 2,732 characters as sent (§6.6; that is 2,049 bytes of envelope).

### 6.4 Host-minted offers: setup and enrol (no signature)

Two kinds of link are minted **on the host** by `andvari admin` or `andvari setup`, not by an account. There is no account key to sign them, so their offer is canonical JSON authenticated only by the AEAD:

```
Envelope(offerKey, {"v":2,"purpose":"setup-offer","linkId":<b64_16>,"origin":<origin>,"instanceId":<uuid>,
                    "name":<name>,"kind":"public"|"private","recoveryFp":<hex64n>,"expiresAt":<ms>},
         AD = "andvari/v2|link-offer|" + linkId)

Envelope(offerKey, {"v":2,"purpose":"enrol-offer","linkId":<b64_16>,"origin":<origin>,"instanceId":<uuid>,
                    "cardHash":<hex64>,"email":<email_b64n>,"expiresAt":<ms>},
         AD = "andvari/v2|link-offer|" + linkId)
```

**Setup** enrols the first operator (§6.7, §9.5).

**Enrol** registers an ordinary member with no inviter: no contact is pinned on either side. It exists for tests, demos, seeding and operator-assisted onboarding (B4).
- It is minted by `andvari admin enrol-link [--email E] [--expires D] [--count N]`, and is valid in every sign-up mode, `closed` included, because the host is the operator.
- It is minted **only once card 0 exists** (FI-B9): its `cardHash` names a card, and every registration other than setup waits for card 0 anyway (§7.3).
- `email` binds the link to one address when given. Registration then requires that address, with no email code.
- Enrol links count against the cap like any member.
- Its `cardHash` is the host's own claim. It is shown in the Trust Gate and is **never a pin root** (§4.4, U3).

**Recognising an opened offer (R3).** After the envelope opens:
- a plaintext starting with the four bytes `61 76 73 02` is a signed frame, and must be purpose 0x06;
- a plaintext starting with `{` is a host-minted offer, and its `purpose` must be `setup-offer` or `enrol-offer`;
- anything else is `unknown_format`, and so is a `{` plaintext whose `purpose` is neither (VR-15, §2.4).

The offer's kind — the frame's `kind` field, or `setup` or `enrol` from the JSON `purpose` — **must equal** the `kind` the server reported for the link. A mismatch is `kind_mismatch`, an anomaly: nothing is sent, and nothing is pinned.

### 6.5 Answers (purpose 0x07)

```
{"v":2,"purpose":"link-answer","linkId":<b64_16>,"offerHash":<hex64>,"to":<uuid>,"toKey":<b64_32>,
 "from":<uuid>,"fromKey":<b64_32>,"name":<name>,"nonce":<b64_32>,"issuedAt":<ms>}
```

- `offerHash` is `H(the offer frame)`.
- `to` and `toKey` are the offer's `from` and `fromKey`.
- `from` and `fromKey` are the answering account.
- `nonce` is the answerer's code nonce `nonceB`: 32 CSPRNG bytes, sent in the open inside the sealed answer.
- It is signed by the answerer and sealed as `Envelope(answerKey, frame, AD = "andvari/v2|link-answer|" + linkId)`.

### 6.6 What the server stores and sees

**Table `links`:**
- `linkId` (primary key), `kind` (`connect`, `register`, `setup` or `enrol`);
- `creatorUserId` (null for setup and enrol), `createdAt`, `expiresAt`;
- `offer` (the envelope, at most 2732 chars);
- `tokenHash` (register, setup and enrol only);
- `boundEmailHash` (enrol links minted with `--email`: hex SHA-256 of `emailNorm`);
- `state` (`open`, `answered`, `revoked` or `expired`);
- `answererUserId`, `answer` (the envelope), `answeredAt`, `deliveredAt`.

**The server sees:** the link id, the kind, who made it, who answered and when, sizes, the token hash, and request IPs (audit and rate limits).

**The server never sees:** `s`, any key derived from it, or the contents of an offer or answer. That covers names, the card hash and the comparison codes. Who connected with whom becomes server-visible metadata (spec 02 §5 gains this row).

**Routes:**

| Route | Rule |
|---|---|
| `POST /api/v1/links {linkId, kind, offer, expiresAt, tokenHash?}` | Authenticated. Answers `201 {expiresAt, ttlMs}`; the server's expiry check is §7.8's (SR-1). **`connect`:** any active account; the apps offer 5 min to 7 days; at most 20 open links and 30 creations a day per account. **`register`:** the apps offer 5 min to 72 h; `tokenHash` required. Who may create one depends on the instance: on a `private` instance with `ANDVARI_REGISTER_LINKS=members` (the default there), any active member; otherwise the operator only (R13). **`setup` and `enrol`** are never created here; they are minted on the host (§9.5). |
| `GET /api/v1/links/{linkId}` | Unauthenticated, 30 per minute per IP. Answers `200 {kind, offer, expiresAt, state, creatorUserId, creatorIdentityPub, reveal}` for an `open` link, and `200 {kind, state, reveal}` for an **`answered`** one, so the answerer can fetch `reveal` after answering while the offer and its creator stop being served once the link is used (FI-B4). An app that finds `answered` on a link it did not answer itself shows "already used". Otherwise `410 link_expired` for an `open` link past `expiresAt`, or `404` for unknown or revoked. An `answered` link answers `200` until the janitor deletes it, whatever its `expiresAt`, so a creator who reveals late still gives the answerer a code (FC-5). `409 link_used` belongs only to the answer and register routes. `creator*` is null for host-minted links. `reveal` is the creator's code nonce once posted (next row). It is **not secret**: it is bound by the offer's commitment and useless without the two frames, which only the two parties hold. So it is returned to any caller (N3). |
| `POST /api/v1/links/{linkId}/reveal {nonce}` | The creator, after processing the answer (§6.11). Stored on the row **once**: a second post is `409 link_state` (T2-R-b). Rings the answerer's bell. |
| `POST /api/v1/links/{linkId}/answer {answer}` | Authenticated as the answerer. `connect` only. State must be `open`, the link unexpired, and the answerer not its creator. `answer` is at most 2732 characters, like the offer (FI-R6). The first answer wins; later ones get `409 link_used`. Rings the creator's bell. |
| `POST /api/v1/auth/register` with `auth.link`, `auth.enrol` or `auth.setup` (§7.3) | `auth.link` is for a `register` link, `auth.enrol` for an enrol link and `auth.setup` for a setup link; a key that does not match the row's `kind` is `409 kind_mismatch` (FI-B5). The token hash must match, the state must be `open`, and the link must be unexpired. For an enrol link with a bound email, the body's `emailNorm` must hash to `boundEmailHash`. The answer (register kind only) is stored in the registration transaction. |
| `GET /api/v1/links/answers` | The caller's answered, unacknowledged links: `[{linkId, answer, answererUserId, answeredAt}]`. Also flagged in `/sync`. |
| `POST /api/v1/links/{linkId}/ack` | The creator processed the answer; sets `deliveredAt`. |
| `DELETE /api/v1/links/{linkId}` | The creator revokes an open link. |

The janitor expires only `open` rows past `expiresAt`, and deletes rows 30 days after they leave `open` (FC-5). **It never touches `connections`** (§3.6): answering a connect link, or registering through a register link, writes `connections(min(a,b), max(a,b))` in the same transaction. Enrol and setup links write none.

### 6.7 Flows

**Connect, both people have accounts.**
1. A taps "Connect with someone". A's app makes `s` and the code nonce `nonceA`, and builds and seals the offer, with `commit` covering `nonceA`.
2. **First** it writes a record to its **own device state**, durably, before anything leaves the device (R14): `links[linkId] = {secret: s, nonceA, kind, offerHash, expiresAt, shares: [{vaultId, role}]}`. Only then does it `POST /links`.
   - **The secret never leaves the creating device** (T-B6). It is not in account state, so no other device of A, and above all no web session, can read it. Only the creating device can finish the link.
   - A crash between the write and the `POST` leaves a record with no link, which the device drops at expiry.
3. A's app shows the QR, the share sheet or Copy.
4. B's app opens the link and runs §6.8.
5. B sees one screen, "Connect with *name* (as they call themselves)?", and taps Connect.
6. B's app posts the answer, with its `nonceB`, and pins A with `compared = false`.
7. A's creating device processes the answer (§6.8) and pins B with `compared = false`. It then posts `reveal` with `nonceA`, and each app can show its own comparison code (§6.11).
8. **Shares wait for the codes (T-B2).** The vaults attached to the link are shared only after A's device has `compared = true` for B, meaning the codes were compared and matched.
   - Until then A sees "Share *Family* with *B's label* once you've compared codes", or can release them by hand behind the warning "You haven't compared codes with this person. Anyone who saw the link first could be on the other end."
   - What a `compared = false` contact may do: receive vaults only through that explicit, per-vault confirmation; send vaults only through §3.6's accept prompt; never trigger anything automatically.
9. **Expiry binds the creator too (T-R3).** A's device deletes its record at `expiresAt` or when A revokes the link. An answer arriving later finds no record and is ignored (`unknown_link`), whatever the server says.

**Connect, recipient has no account.** B's app (§6.8) shows "Create an account on *instance name* to connect with *name*".
- On an instance with open sign-up, this goes through §7.2.
- B's app posts the answer right after registration.

**Dead ends, each with its own screen (R13).**

| Situation | Screen |
|---|---|
| **The link's instance is not the one the app is signed in to.** | "This invite is for *instance name* (*origin*). You're signed in to *current instance*. Connecting means using an account on that other server." [Cancel] [Switch to *instance name*], the way out first and the switch never the main button.<br>Switching is a sign-out of the current instance (§4.4): that server's pin and trust records stay in its namespace on this device (§0.5) and the account stays on its server, while this device's copy of the account goes as at any sign-out, never over changes not sent without the person being told (§4.4). Creating an account there needs nothing more. **Signing in** to an existing account there shows the Trust Gate first, because a link offer is not a password pin (§4.4, T2-B1). |
| **No account, and the instance is not taking sign-ups** (`links`, `closed` or `full`). | "*name* invited you to connect on *instance name*, but that server isn't taking new accounts right now." Then one of three explanations: "It is invitation-only: ask *name* for an invitation link." (`links`), "It is closed." (`closed`), or "It is full." (`full`). |
| On a private instance where members may create register links. | *name*'s app offers "Send an invitation instead", which makes a register link. |

**Register link** (instance in `links` mode). B's app shows the sign-up form with no email code; the link's token authorizes the registration. The register body carries `auth.link = {linkId, registerToken, answer}`, so the link's creator and B end up pinned to each other in one step.

On a private instance any member may create register links (default P), so B is pinned to the family member who invited them, never only to the operator account.

**Enrol link** (host-minted, §6.4). The sign-up form with no email code (the email is prefilled and fixed when the link is bound to one). No contact is pinned.

**Setup link** (§9.5).
1. The first operator's app opens the setup offer. It requires that the instance serves **no** cards yet. Until card 0 exists the server refuses every other registration (`409 setup_incomplete`, §7.3, FI-B9).
2. It keeps the opened offer in device state until card 0 is accepted. Then it registers with `auth.setup = {linkId, registerToken}`, and the server makes this account the operator.
3. It signs card 0 from the offer's `instanceId`, `origin`, `name`, `kind` and `recoveryFp`, uploads it, and pins it.

**Resume (R10).** If the app stops between steps 2 and 3, the next unlock of the operator account on any device finds no card on the instance. It offers "Finish setting up this server": it signs card 0 from the kept offer, or, on a device that never saw it, from `GET /instance`'s `setup` block (§4.3). The server's card 0 checks bind it to the operator account either way.

**In person.** The same connect link shown as a QR with a short expiry (default 15 minutes). Because both people are there, the codes are compared at once, **each typing the code shown on the other's screen**. A types B's digits and B types A's (two different codes, §6.11). Each app sets `compared = true` only for its own correct entry, and B never relies on A's say-so. Any attached shares go out right away.

### 6.8 Verification order

**Verdict strings and their order are in §2.4, which governs (VR-14).** In particular, the signed-statement helper's checks (parse, `F`, signer, signature) run before the field checks (`linkId`, origin, expiry, the server-reported creator), as everywhere else.

**Recipient** (B's app). The first failure decides.
1. Parse the link (§6.1). Derive `linkId` and the keys.
2. `GET /links/{linkId}` from the link's origin. An expired, used or unknown link stops here with its own copy.
3. Open the envelope under `offerKey` with the AD (`unopenable`).
4. Recognise and parse the offer (§6.4: frame or host JSON, and the kind must match the server's).
   - **Connect or register:**
     - the 0x06 frame and strict parse;
     - `linkId` equal to the derived one, and `origin` equal to the link's;
     - `expiresAt > now`;
     - the signer maps to `fromKey`, and the signature verifies;
     - **`fromKey` hashes to the link's `F`** (`creator_mismatch`, T-B6);
     - `from` equals the server-reported `creatorUserId`, and `fromKey` equals `creatorIdentityPub`. A mismatch is an anomaly, and nothing is sent (T-R5).
   - **Setup or enrol:** strict JSON parse, and the same `linkId`, `origin` and expiry checks. `F` must be `-`.
5. Card chain from the origin, per §4.4's roots:
   - connect and register: it must contain `cardHash` with `instanceId`;
   - setup: it must be empty;
   - enrol: the typed-address rule with the Trust Gate (or the baked root for the public instance).
   - **at the baked origin, the baked root first, whatever the kind** (§4.4): then the link's own check above, for connect and register; nothing more for enrol, with no Trust Gate; and a setup link is refused there (`withheld` on an empty chain, `already_set_up` on a served one).

   If this device already pins a card for that origin, the chain must extend that pin.
6. **Key-change check (T-R5).** If this account already has a contact for `from` with a **different** key, nothing is replaced. The screen says "*label*'s key is different from the one you connected with before. They may have reinstalled or made a new account. Compare codes to accept the new key." Only a matched comparison code (§6.11) replaces the pin.
7. Show the screen. Only after B confirms is anything sent.

**Creator** (A's creating device, processing `GET /links/answers`).
1. Find `links[linkId]` in this device's state (`unknown_link`). A record dropped at expiry or revocation means the answer is ignored (T-R3).
2. Open the answer under `answerKey` with the AD.
3. 0x07 frame, strict parse, `linkId`, and `offerHash` equal to the record's.
4. `to` is A and `toKey` is A's key; the signer maps to `fromKey`; the signature.
5. `from` equals the server-reported `answererUserId`. A mismatch is an anomaly, and nothing is pinned.
6. The step-6 key-change check, from A's side.
7. Pin with `compared = false`, post `reveal`, release shares only under §6.7 step 8, delete the secret, acknowledge.

### 6.9 Contacts

An account-state contact is `{userId, identityPub, label: <name b64>, method: "link", verifiedAt, compared: bool, madeOn: <deviceId>}`.
- **`method` has one value, `"link"`** (T2-T-R5). Every contact comes from a connect or register link (§6.7), remote or in person. `"setup"`, listed in an earlier draft, has no producer: setup and enrol links pin no contact (§6.4). A decoder refuses any other value, so no other way of making a pin can enter through account state. The field stays so that a later pin method is a visible format change.
- `madeOn` is the device that made the pin. It is shown in the per-device prompt; a hostile web bundle can forge it (R24), and the name the person recognises is the real check. Removing a contact writes a tombstone, merged as today, and the newest act wins.

- **One key per userId.** A second key for a pinned userId never replaces the first without a matched comparison (§6.8 step 6).
- **Labels follow keys.** A label is shown for a log member or a grant sender only where that key equals the pinned key (§3.9).

**When a contact counts on a device ("current").** A contact is current on a device when one of these holds:
- (a) this device processed or answered the link;
- (b) the contact arrived in a device-link bundle (§8.3) with the same key hash;
- (c) the person confirmed it on this device.
  - **New contacts from other devices (T-R5)** appear as "New person from your *iPhone*: *label* — Use on this device?", one tap each. That gives G3 (no second ceremony) on devices linked earlier.
  - It also exposes contacts that a hostile web bundle wrote into account state (T-R6): the web never creates contacts (§13), so an unknown name is a warning sign, and the prompt shows where the contact came from.
- (d) on a device that signed in with only the password, the person confirmed the one-tap list (§8.5).

The device keeps `pins.confirmed = {userId, keyHash}` durably, as today. A contact that is not current on a device still syncs. It is listed as "confirm on this device", and grants from it wait (§3.6).

Deleted: typed, QR and enrolment pins; the `email` field; the add-member email check; `LegacyPinReverifyRequired`.

### 6.10 Sharing at link time

The creator may attach vaults to a connect link ("also share *Family* with them as writer"). Choosing them is the human confirming each (vault, person) pair. A link with shares is therefore never a bearer token for those vaults (T-B2):

- The creating device signs the `add` entry and grant for each pair **only after the comparison code matched** (`compared = true`), or after a per-vault release behind §6.7 step 8's warning.
- It also requires that it is still the owner and the vault is not deleted.
- The recipient still accepts each vault (§3.6).

No other path signs a grant without a person choosing that vault for that contact.

### 6.11 Comparison code (committed, T-B1)

The comparison code is built so that **neither side can steer it**. A party commits to its nonce before it sees the other's; a party that sends its nonce in the clear does so only after the other has committed. Grinding a key, an `issuedAt` or a nonce to match a target code therefore gains nothing: each code an attacker can reach is fixed before they see what they are trying to match.

| Pass | Message | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | the offer | `commit` to `nonceA` (§6.3) |
| 2 | the answer | `nonceB` in the open (§6.5) |
| 3 | the reveal | `nonceA`, via `POST /links/{linkId}/reveal`. The answerer checks it against `commit` (`bad_reveal`). |

Each side has its own code: `code(side, linkId, offerHash, answerHash, nonceA, nonceB)` (§2.3), six digits, with `side` = `creator` for the code the link's creator shows and `answerer` for the code the answerer shows. A person reads out the code on their own screen and types the code the other person reads. The app checks the typed digits against the OTHER side's code, which it computes and never displays, so the digits a person must type are not on their own screen: they cannot be copied from it, and an app cannot offer a "they match" button, because the value it would need is not the one it shows. (With one shared code, as this design first had it, a person could type what their own screen showed, and a careless app could confirm with the value it held.)

- **Remote:** both apps offer "Compare codes" once the reveal has arrived. On a call they trust, each person reads their own code out, and **types the six digits the other reads** (T2-Rec); the two codes differ. There is no "they match" button. The copy names the other person's device: "Ask Alice to read the code on her iPhone, then type it here."
- **In person:** each person types the code shown on the other's screen (§6.7), which is not the code on their own.
- **Typed input.** A typed code is compared as text. Every ASCII space is removed (the code is shown as `481 207`), and what is left must be exactly six ASCII digits, equal to the other side's code. Nothing else is normalised: a dropped leading zero is a mismatch, and so is any other character. The device-link code (§8.2) is typed under the same rule.
- **A wrong code is not a verdict.** Nothing was verified or refused, so §2.4 has no string for it: the check answers yes or no, the app says the code is not the one on the other screen, and the person may type again. Typing is local to the person and their own app, so there is nothing for an attacker to retry. (The device link is different: a wrong code there cancels the link, `code_mismatch`, §8.2.)
- **Equal codes.** For about one link in a million the two codes are the same six digits. Nothing special happens: each app still accepts exactly the other side's code, which is then also the one on its own screen. An implementation must not refuse a typed code for being equal to its own. So no app refuses a person's own code by itself: every typed code goes to the shared code, which checks it against the other side's code only and answers a mismatch for anything else; an app may word a mismatch that is its own code as that. The Android app keeps this (2026-11-17), and so does the desktop app (2026-11-17, late): a typed code equal to its own goes to the shared code like any other.
- **The comparison is a screen of its own.** A person who chooses to compare, from the notice of a contact's changed key (§6.8 step 6) wherever that notice shows, or from "Compare codes" in People, gets the comparison itself: this side's code, the field for the other side's, and what each answer does, with the way back to the page they came from; leaving it decides nothing and returns to where the person was. Nothing else stands in its place. The Android app keeps this (2026-11-17): the comparison opens as a screen of its own over whichever page the notice stood on, and People's "Compare codes" opens the same screen. **Not kept yet (2026-11-17):** the desktop app draws the comparison only inside the People section, so choosing to compare from the notice elsewhere shows nothing of it.
- **Who sets `compared` (T2-R-i).** Each app sets `compared = true` only on its **own** correct entry. Nothing the server or the other app sends can set it, and no wire message carries it.
- **Single-assignment nonces (T2-R-b).** Each side computes the code once, from the first value it accepted for the other side's nonce, and never recomputes it.
  - The answerer accepts only a reveal that matches `commit`; the commitment leaves no choice.
  - The server stores one reveal per link (§6.6).
  - A second, different value, from either the server or the other side, is an anomaly: the link is marked failed and no code is shown.

A failed comparison changes nothing: it is shown as "The codes don't match. Don't share anything with this person until you've talked."

The odds of beating the code are 1 in a million per attempt, and each attempt costs the attacker a fresh link that the real people can see.

## 7. Sign-up, email verification, cap, quotas and recovery posture

### 7.1 Instance modes (environment, read at boot)

| Variable | Values | Default |
|---|---|---|
| `ANDVARI_SIGNUP_MODE` | `open` (email-verified sign-up plus links), `links` (register links only), `closed` (sign-in only) | `links`; the public instance uses `open` |
| `ANDVARI_SIGNUP_CAP` | **active** member accounts allowed (the operator, disabled and deleted accounts excluded) | 200 |
| `ANDVARI_SIGNUP_DAILY_MAX` | new accounts per UTC day | 25 |
| `ANDVARI_SIGNUP_SECRET` | 32 random bytes, base64url, for code MACs | generated by `andvari setup` |
| `ANDVARI_SIGNUP_POW_BITS` | leading zero bits for the optional proof of work (§7.5) | 0 (off) |
| `ANDVARI_SIGNUP_BLOCKLIST` | path to a file of refused email domains | unset |
| `ANDVARI_REQUIRE_AGE_CONFIRM` | `1` requires `ageConfirmed: true` | 0; the public instance uses 1 |
| `ANDVARI_INSTANCE_KIND` | `public` or `private` (default P, §0.2). Carried in the card. | `private`; the public instance uses `public` |
| `ANDVARI_REGISTER_LINKS` | `operator` or `members`: who may create register links (§6.6) | `members` on private instances, `operator` on public ones |
| `ANDVARI_TEST_MODE` | `1` enables the test mail sink and the test clock. The server refuses to start with it unless `ANDVARI_CANONICAL_ORIGIN` is `http://localhost[:port]` or `http://127.0.0.1[:port]`, so no deployed instance can run in test mode. | 0 |
| `ANDVARI_MAIL_SINK` | Test mode only: a directory where each outgoing email is written as one JSON file `{template, to, subject, text}` instead of being sent (B4) | unset |
| `ANDVARI_TEST_CLOCK` | Test mode only: a file holding a millisecond timestamp the server uses as "now", for expiry and rate-limit tests in the hostile-server harness (§18) | unset |

**Which setting wins (R7).**
- The environment is the source of truth and is read at boot.
- `andvari admin signup open|links|close|cap <n>` edits `andvari.env`, **clears the database's tighter overrides** for what it sets, and restarts the server (N8). It is the only way to raise the cap or loosen the mode.
- The operator panel can only **tighten**: close sign-up, move `open` to `links`, or lower the cap. Its values are stored in the database and win when tighter. They travel as `signup {mode, cap}` inside the policy of `PUT /admin/policy` (§9.2); a value looser than the environment is refused with `400 not_tighter` (S3-Q5).
- The effective cap is `min(env, db)`, and the effective mode is the stricter of the two (`closed` is stricter than `links`, which is stricter than `open`).

**Other rules.**
- `open` mode refuses to start without a mail transport (§11.1).
- `GET /client-policy` gains `signup: {mode, full: bool, ageConfirmRequired: bool, powBits}`. All of it is decorative or a UI hint under the policy-trust boundary (spec 03 §1.1): the server enforces.

### 7.2 Email verification

**`POST /api/v1/signup/start {email, ageConfirmed, pow?: {date, nonce}}`**
- **Proof of work (FI-R4).** `pow.date` is the `utcDate` the client hashed (`YYYY-MM-DD`, §2.3) and `pow.nonce` the `int` it found. The server accepts today's or yesterday's date (UTC) and recomputes the hash.
- **Refusals:**
  - `403 signup_closed`, `403 signup_full` (shown before any typing; the first screen reads `signup.full`);
  - `400 invalid_email`, `400 age_confirm_required`, `400 pow_required`, `400 bad_pow`;
  - `400 email_domain_blocked` (S3-Q3), checked before any account lookup and the same for every address in a blocked domain. It reveals the domain policy, never whether an address is registered, and an honest person is not left waiting for a code that will never come;
  - `429 rate_limited`;
  - **`503 mail_unavailable`** while the mail outbox is stalled (§11.4), for every address alike. The app says "We can't send email right now. Try again in a while." (R8)
- **Otherwise the answer is always `202 {"status":"sent"}`.**
  - The address has an account: an "already registered" email goes out, at most once a day per address.
  - Otherwise: a uniformly random 6-digit code is stored as `signup_codes(emailHash = the address's keyed hash (spec 03 §14.1, since 2026-11-18), codeMac (§2.3), expiresAt = now + 15 min, attempts = 0)`, and the code email is sent. A new code replaces the previous one for that address.

**`POST /api/v1/signup/verify {email, code}`**
- Answers `200 {signupTicket, expiresAt}`, or a uniform `400 bad_code`, whatever the cause: no code, wrong code, expired, or attempts used up.
- Each wrong code increments `attempts`. At 5 the code is dead.
- `signupTicket` is 256 random bits. Its SHA-256 is stored, bound to `emailHash`, valid 30 minutes, single use.

**Rate limits.**

| Bucket | Limit |
|---|---|
| `signup_start` per IP | 3 per hour, 10 per day |
| `signup_start` per address | 5 per day |
| `signup_verify` per IP | 20 per hour |
| `register` per IP | 10 per hour |
| Daily account maximum | §7.1 |

### 7.3 Register v2

`POST /api/v1/auth/register`:

```
{ "auth": {"signupTicket": …} | {"link": {"linkId","registerToken","answer"}} | {"enrol": {"linkId","registerToken"}}
          | {"setup": {"linkId","registerToken"}},
  "email", "displayName", "userId", "kdfSalt", "kdfParams", "authKey", "wrappedUvk",
  "identityPub", "encryptedIdentitySeed",
  "genesis": <b64 0x05 frame>,
  "personalVault": {"vaultId","wrappedVk","metaBlob"}, "personalWrap": {"vaultId","wrappedVk"},
  "memberRecovery": {"recoveryWrappedUvk","recoveryAuthKey"},
  "recovery": {"posture":"private"} | {"posture":"operator","escrow":{"sealed","fingerprint"}},
  "ageConfirmed": true|false, "device": {"platform","name","deviceSecret"} }
```

`device.deviceSecret` is defined in §8.7 (FI-R10).

**The server checks, in order:**
1. Shapes, and the existing key-material caps.
2. That the `auth` kind is allowed in this mode:
   - `open`: a ticket, a register link or an enrol link;
   - `links`: a register link or an enrol link;
   - `closed`: an enrol link only;
   - `setup`: only while **no operator account** exists: before the first setup, or after `reset-instance-identity` has demoted the old operator (§4.6, N2) (FI-B9);
   - **every other kind waits for card 0**: until the instance serves a card, it is refused with `409 setup_incomplete` (FI-B9). So an enrol link or a sign-up can never take the instance before its operator, and the setup link can always be re-minted.
3. The ticket: valid, unexpired, unused, and its `emailHash` equals the keyed hash of `emailNorm` (spec 03 §14.3). Or the link row: kind, token hash, `open`, unexpired, and for a bound enrol link the bound email.
4. Age confirmation when required.
5. The cap (counted inside the transaction) and the daily maximum. Setup is exempt from both. Enrol links count against the cap but not against the daily maximum.
6. The email is not taken, and the `userId` is new and not retired (unchanged rules).
7. The genesis (§5.2).
8. The recovery posture (§7.7).
9. Personal vault and wrap: `personalWrap.vaultId = personalVault.vaultId = genesis.personalVaultId`.

**Refusals by step (FI-R4):**

| Step | Codes |
|---|---|
| 1 | the existing shape and key-material cap errors (`400 bad_request`) |
| 2 | `403 signup_closed` (this `auth` kind is not allowed in this mode), `403 setup_done`, `409 setup_incomplete` |
| 3 | `400 bad_ticket` (uniform: unknown, expired, used, or for another address). For links: `404 link_unknown` (unknown, revoked, or a token that does not match), `410 link_expired`, `409 link_used`, `409 kind_mismatch`, `403 email_mismatch` (a bound enrol link) |
| 4 | `400 age_confirm_required` |
| 5 | `403 signup_full`, `429 signup_daily_max` (with `Retry-After`, the seconds to the next midnight UTC, when the day's count starts again; rule of 2026-11-17, spec 03 §14.3 and §8) |
| 6 | `409 email_taken`, `409 user_id_taken`: the existing code names, now with status `409`, retired ids included (S3-C1) |
| 7 | `400 bad_genesis` |
| 8 | `400 escrow_not_allowed`, `400 operator_recovery_unavailable`, `400 bad_escrow` |
| 9 | `400 personal_vault_mismatch` |

**Then one transaction:**
- create the user (with `role` `operator` for setup, `member` otherwise), the personal vault and its owner grant, the personal wrap, the member-recovery row, the escrow row if the posture is `operator`, and the genesis;
- consume the ticket or link (for a register link, store `answer` and set the link `answered`);
- create the first device and session.

The response is unchanged: the login response plus `recoveryPieceId`.

**Removed from today's register:** the invite rows, the invite `escrowPolicy` truth table, `escrow_required` and `escrow_not_allowed_when_waived`, and the bootstrap `"*"` invite.

**On a device that keeps another account**, an account at another server, or its server's operator account (rules of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7): a registration of every kind (ticket, register link, enrol link, setup link) stops before anything is committed: the device asks before the key derivation and again just before it sends the registration, and a no stops it with nothing derived, sent, pinned or signed, and the link not spent. The stop is said as what it is, as for a recovery (§10). Where what the device keeps changed while the registration was under way and its session is refused after it, the account stands at the server under the chosen password; the registration then stops (nothing more is sealed, sent, pinned or signed) and hands its kit over with the refusal, and the kit reaches the screen as a recovery's does (§10); the account opens once the other account is signed out. A register link's flow is ended when its registration stops. The shared code, the app interface and `andvari-cli` keep this (2026-11-17). **Kept since:** both apps draw the kit with its line and results after such a refusal (Android since 2026-11-18, the desktop since 2026-11-19), the result of a registration made while the device came to hold its server's operator account included, which the app interface tells by the keeper's own reason with the removal as its way out; `andvari-cli` keeps the kit (to the file given, on the screen or printed), says that the account was made and why nothing is signed in, and ends exactly that session at the server (2026-11-19). A setup link whose registration is refused at the last moment is a known remainder (§19).

### 7.4 Cap and quotas

**The cap** counts `users` with role `member` and status `active` (T-R10). Disabling an abusive account frees its slot at once, so filling the cap with junk accounts is answered by disabling them, not by closing sign-up for everyone. The daily maximum (§7.1) bounds how fast junk can refill it. **Re-enabling an account does not check the cap** (S3-Q7): the cap gates registrations only, so slots strangers filled can never keep out a person the operator re-admits. The count may then sit above the cap, and sign-up stays full until it falls below.

**Per-account quotas** are enforced server-side, with per-account overrides from the operator:

| Variable | Default | Enforced at |
|---|---|---|
| `ANDVARI_QUOTA_ACCOUNT_BYTES` | 104857600 (100 MiB): item ciphertext plus attachment bytes in vaults the account **owns** | push, attachment upload, genesis, transfer accept. Writers to a shared vault use the owner's quota. |
| `ANDVARI_QUOTA_ITEMS` | 5000 items across owned vaults | push |
| `ANDVARI_QUOTA_OWNED_SHARED_VAULTS` | 50 | genesis, accept |
| `ANDVARI_QUOTA_OPEN_LINKS` | 20 | `POST /links` |

The existing per-file and per-item attachment ceilings stay (`attachmentMaxBytes`, `itemAttachmentsMaxBytes`, each at most the account quota). `userAttachmentsMaxBytes` is deleted: the account quota replaces it.

**Over quota:**
- On `POST /sync/push`, each mutation that would exceed is answered **per mutation**, with status `rejected: quota_exceeded` (spec 03 §5's per-mutation status). The rest of the batch proceeds.
- Single-request routes (attachment upload, genesis, accept) answer `413 quota_exceeded {quota}`.
- Either way the app shows "This vault's owner is out of space on this server."

**Overrides.** The operator raises a person's limits with `PUT /admin/accounts/{id}/quota`, or `andvari admin quota`. For example, it is set before a large restore (§14.1).

### 7.5 Abuse controls

There is no third-party CAPTCHA. The controls, in the order they bite:
1. Per-IP and per-address limits (§7.2).
2. The daily account maximum.
3. The cap, which bounds the total damage.
4. The operator closing sign-up with one switch.
5. An optional proof of work, off by default. When `powBits > 0`, `signup/start` requires a `nonce` such that `SHA-256("andvari/v2|signup-pow|" + emailNorm + "|" + utcDate + "|" + nonce)` has `powBits` leading zero bits. The server is stateless about it: it checks today's and yesterday's date. Clients compute it in a background thread with a progress note. 20 bits is about a second on a phone.
6. The optional domain blocklist.
7. Per-recipient email caps (§11.4), so no address can be mail-bombed through the instance.

No email lookup exists (`/users/lookup` is deleted, §15), so the instance has neither a "does this account exist" oracle nor a stranger-sharing channel.

### 7.6 Age and region

- **Age:** sign-up shows "I am 13 or older" (required when `ageConfirmRequired`), and the server stores nothing beyond the fact that the box was ticked.
- **Region (US at launch):** set by store distribution and the terms. The server does not geolocate.
- Both are stated in the terms on `https://andvaripass.com`.

### 7.7 Recovery posture

The person's choice is recorded in **UVK-sealed account state** (§4.5: `recovery = {posture, fp, at}`), which is what every client acts on. `users.recovery ∈ {private, operator}` on the server is a mirror for the server's own route checks (T-B4). The member-recovery kit (§10) is **mandatory** in both postures.

- **`private`:** an escrow in the body is refused (`400 escrow_not_allowed`).
- **`operator`:**
  - the pinned card's `recoveryFp` must not be `none` (`400 operator_recovery_unavailable`);
  - the escrow must be a version-3 escrow (purpose 0x02, spec 04 §3), which names the account's address inside the statement the account's own key signs (rule of 2026-11-16), sealed to the served recovery public key;
  - its `fingerprint` must equal `card.recoveryFp`, and the client must have checked the served key against the card at full length.
  - The server refuses, wherever a recovery copy is stored (registration, turning operator recovery on, the re-seal after a rotation), a copy whose size is not the version-3 size for that account's address, so that no client can store the old form or replace a new copy with an old one (rule of 2026-11-16). The server keeps this (`400 escrow_wrong_form`, spec 03 §3.1), and the web client seals version 3 at all four of its places. The older core client code (the iPhone app until its move) still seals the old form, so it cannot turn operator recovery on, which is the right failure; that the iPhone app no longer seals it is a condition of its release.

**Changing it later:** `PUT /api/v1/account/recovery {posture, escrow?, currentAuthKey, accountState: {sealedState, baseSeq}}` (FI-R5).
- It needs a fresh password proof, verified like a password change.
- The client seals the new account state, with the new `recovery` record, under the UVK; the server cannot write it. The server stores the escrow change and `sealedState` in one transaction, with the usual compare-and-set on `baseSeq`. On `409 stale_state` nothing changes, and the client merges and retries.
- Refusals: the uniform `403 reauth_failed` for a wrong password, as on every re-authenticated route (a `401` would read as an expired session, S3-K1); `400 escrow_not_allowed`, `400 operator_recovery_unavailable`, `400 bad_escrow`; `409 stale_state`.
- `private` deletes the escrow rows, with secure delete and a WAL truncate.
- `operator` stores a new escrow.
- It sends the "recovery settings changed" email and writes an audit row.

**What operator recovery really gives the operator (T-R6).** The escrow yields the UVK, the UVK opens the identity seed, and the seed is the account's signing key. An operator who restores, or who simply opens, an opted-in account's escrow can therefore read that person's vaults, **act as that person**, and read every shared vault the person belongs to. The warning says so in plain words.

**Exact copy.** This is normative; all clients use the same strings, pinned by tests.

> **How should this account be recovered if you forget your master password?**
>
> ◉ **Only me** *(selected)* — You'll save a recovery kit now. If you lose both your master password and your recovery kit, nobody can get your vault back, not even the people who run this server.
>
> ○ **Me, or the operator of {instance name}** — The operator keeps a key that can restore your account if you ask.

Choosing the second option shows this before anything changes:

> **Let the operator of {instance name} ({origin}) restore your account?**
>
> With operator recovery on, the people who run {instance name} hold a key that can restore your account. Restoring it means they can open your vault, every password, note and card in it, and act as you, including in vaults others share with you. They will only use it if you ask, but you are trusting them not to misuse it and to keep that key safe.
>
> Anyone who gets into your email can ask them to reset your account. A request waits 7 days, and your signed-in devices can cancel it.
>
> You will still get your own recovery kit, and you can turn this off later in Settings.
>
> ☐ I understand that the operator could open my vault and act as me.
>
> [ Turn on operator recovery ] [ Keep it private ]

When `recoveryFp` is `none`, the second option is not shown at all. Turning it off later shows: "This deletes the operator's recovery copy from the server. It cannot undo a copy the operator may already have made."

**On a private (family) instance** (card `kind = private`, default P), the same choice is worded for people who know each other. The default stays **Only me**.

> ○ **Me, or the person who runs {instance name}** — They can restore your account if you forget your master password.
>
> **Let the person who runs {instance name} ({origin}) restore your account?** — With this on, they keep a key that can restore your account. Restoring it means they could open your vault, every password, note and card in it, and act as you. Anyone who gets into your email can ask them to reset your account; a request waits 7 days, and your signed-in devices can cancel it. Choose this only if you trust the person who runs this server with your passwords. You will still get your own recovery kit.
>
> ☐ I understand that the person who runs this server could open my vault and act as me.

**Names in recovery copy (T2-R-f).** `{instance name}` is the pinned card's `name` and `{origin}` its origin (§4.4), both signed by the operator's key and rendered as plain text. Recovery copy never shows a name the server reports for an account, such as the operator account's display name: a hostile server could make that read "Mom".

**Operator recovery on the public instance: two variants.** **IF-2 is written for variant R** (W1, §0.1), which the threat re-review and the plan recommended and the owner confirmed on 2026-11-15 (§0.6). Variant K for the public instance is the bounded delta listed in Appendix A, so switching is a known amount of work (FI-B11). Private instances are the same under both: operator recovery is available with the family copy and every §9.3 safeguard.

| | **Variant K: kept, hardened** | **Variant R: removed from the public instance** |
|---|---|---|
| Setup | `andvari setup --kind public` runs the recovery-key ceremony when the operator chooses it (§9.5 step 4). Card 0 carries `recoveryFp`. | `andvari setup --kind public` sets `recoveryFp = none` and does not offer step 4. |
| Server | Register accepts `posture: operator` (§7.3 step 8). The assisted-recovery routes (§9.3), `POST /admin/accounts/{id}/escrow`, `fulfil`, `PUT /escrow/self` and `PUT /instance/rotation` are live. | When `ANDVARI_INSTANCE_KIND = public`: `posture: operator` is refused at register and at `PUT /account/recovery` (`400 operator_recovery_unavailable`). The `/recovery/requests*` routes, `GET /account/recovery-requests`, `GET /recovery-pubkey`, `GET /admin/recovery-requests`, `POST /admin/accounts/{id}/escrow`, `fulfil`, `PUT /escrow/self` and `PUT /instance/rotation` answer `404` (FI-R3). `PUT /instance/card` refuses a card with `kind = public` and a `recoveryFp` other than `none` (`400 bad_card`). |
| Client rule | Seal only under §4.5: the account-state record, the pinned card's `recoveryFp`, the device's own mirror, signed rotations. | **When the pinned card's `kind` is `public`, the client never shows the operator choice or "Ask the operator", never seals or re-seals an escrow, and ignores `recoveryFp`, whatever the card or the account state says** (T2-Rec). The rule is compiled into every client, so a stolen operator key cannot turn escrow back on with a new card. A `kind` change needs the Trust Gate, and store builds never follow one for the public instance (§4.4). |
| Copy | The public copy above, the warning, and the §9.3 screens. | Sign-up shows no choice, only "You'll save a recovery kit now. If you lose both your master password and your recovery kit, nobody can get your vault back." The help page "I lost my master password and my kit" says: if a device is still unlocked (by fingerprint or face), export your data now and start a new account; there is no other way back. |
| Operator's work | Keep the recovery sheet offline; drills; identity checks with strangers; 7-day waits; any legal demand for a person's keys lands on the operator. | None for recovery. |
| Tests | `recovery.json`; the assisted-recovery e2e on a public test instance, with veto, hold and code checks. | The same vectors and e2e on a **private** test instance. A public test instance asserts the `404`s, the card refusal, and the client rule: a public card that names a `recoveryFp` shows no choice and seals nothing. |
| Residuals | R3 for opted-in people, R27, and the "what remains" paragraph below. | R3 and R27 do not apply to the public instance. Losing password and kit there loses the account. |

**What remains under K.** An opted-in person's vault can still be taken:
- by the operator, or by anyone holding the recovery sheet and a copy of the server's data, silently; and by a court order served on the operator;
- by anyone who reads that person's email for seven days while none of their devices is unlocked to cancel, and who then passes the operator's identity check. For a stranger who gave only an email, that check comes down to a reply from the same inbox;
- by anyone who steals the operator key and gets the person to type their password at a "keep operator recovery?" prompt (§4.5, which now defaults to off and warns);
- **by whoever controls the server, even without the recovery sheet** (FS-R5). They open a request in the person's name with their own key, mark it verified and its hold passed, suppress the bell and the emails, give the operator contact details of their own, and pose as the person, reading out the request code. The operator types the address of the person they believe they are helping, the copy names that person, and an honest operator then runs `assist` and seals the person's UVK to them. The 7-day hold, the veto and the emailed code bind only an honest server, and no small fix exists.
  - What stands in the way is the operator's own knowledge of whom they are talking to. On a family server that holds when they reach the person through a channel they already had (in person, or a number they already know), never one the request supplies. The typed address and the person's device stop the other substitutions (another account's copy, an answer for another address); someone the operator knows and helps as themselves, under their own address, gets only their own account (spec 04 §7, "What these checks do not cover").
  - Separately, on a fresh install pinned by typed address, such a server can answer the person's own request itself, without the operator, with a minted account that claims the person's address. It does not reach the person's vault that way; it gets an account whose keys it holds, protected by the person's next password, and the person's comparison of the account ID (the kit first) is the only check (spec 04 §7; rule of 2026-11-16).
  - On the public instance there is no such channel, which is one more reason for W1.

Once opened, the keys never change (R33): the access is permanent, and it covers the vaults others shared with that person.

**What gets awkward under R.** Losing both password and kit is final, even for someone who still has a device unlocked by fingerprint: their only route is export and a new account, and the help page must say so. The operator-recovery code paths stay in the apps for private instances. A stolen operator key could re-enable escrow through a new card if clients did not enforce the client rule; they do.

### 7.8 One origin; sessions, refresh and two-step sign-in (B1)

**Single origin.** Every instance, the public one included, serves one origin. `ANDVARI_PUBLIC_HOSTNAME` and the break-glass twin origin are **deleted** (§15). Two reasons:

- The public instance is an internet origin by design, so a second "emergency" origin with stricter rules has nothing left to protect.
- The code calls that twin "the public origin", which would collide with "the public instance" in every review.

With it go `isPublicOrigin` and every refusal keyed to it: `register_public_disabled`, `public_refresh_disabled`, `account_delete_public_disabled`, `recovery_public_disabled`, `sharing_public_disabled`, `public_login_requires_totp`, and the per-origin `signupMode = closed` overlay.

**Restated rules for every instance:**

| Topic | Rule |
|---|---|
| Sessions | Access tokens last 1 hour. Refresh tokens last 30 days, rotate, and are single-use; reusing one revokes the device's session (spec 03 §2, unchanged). Refresh works on the instance's one origin. **A device has one live session, and the server keeps that rule itself** (rules of 2026-11-17; spec 03 §2): a sign-in that gives a known device a new session ends that device's earlier sessions in the same transaction, only when the whole sign-in succeeded, and the client sends nothing to end the one it replaces (after a lost refresh answer its token would read as reuse and end the new session too); a session the device refuses is still ended by the device alone, by its own token. A browser's row is the exception: each tab of a browser without the offline copy holds its own session on the browser's one row (decided on 2026-11-16), so a web sign-in ends none of them; the exception follows the row's kind recorded when the row was made, never what a sign-in claims. The sign-in may name the session it replaces by the token it last held; the server then ends exactly that one (same account, same row; anything else ignored), also when the token was already replaced, and records that it was (§9.4); the name travels in the login's optional field `replacesRefreshToken` (spec 03 §2). The kind of a device row decides at every place a session is made (a sign-in, a refresh, a registration, both recoveries' commits, a link's pickup), never the request's claim. A replaced refresh token presented again within sixty seconds of its replacement gets the same pair that refresh answered, while that pair is untouched, and is no reuse; each such answer draws the caller address's allowance for refreshes and is recorded (§9.4), and the server drops the kept pair when its minute ends. Inside that minute whoever holds the replaced token gets the same live pair as the device and can use it beside it; the record is what shows it, and that trade is kept as the released line has it. After the minute, or after a restart of the server, a repeated refresh is a reuse. A device records that a refresh is out before the request goes, and only an answer in the server's own envelope clears that record; while it stands, the device tries the refresh once more, within fifty seconds of the first try's sending (so that it arrives inside the server's minute), and never after: where no try is left, the session ends on the device with a reason of its own (andvari could not confirm this device's session with its server in time and sent nothing again, so no other session was ended), and its token is kept only for the next sign-in to name as the session it replaces (rules of 2026-11-17 and 2026-11-18). A sign-in whose answer the device refuses ends the session the server gave it, by that answer's own token, and keeps the device row the answer named (rule of 2026-11-18). A sign-in that fails or is refused ends only a session it was itself given. Sessions nobody holds (a success answer the browser could not read, a refused session whose ending got no answer) end by themselves, after an hour for access and thirty days for refresh.
