A copied desktop profile or a copied home of `andvari-cli` is one device to the server, so each sign-in on one signs the other out; removing that device in the device list makes each a device of its own. **Kept (2026-11-18):** the server ends a known device's earlier sessions at a sign-in, ends exactly the session a sign-in names, decides by the row's kind and answers a repeated refresh within the grace; the apps and `andvari-cli` name the session a sign-in replaces and send nothing else for it; the browser ends only the session its own sign-in was given, names the session a sign-in replaces at every submit of the form (none where every tab shares one session, accepted), and ends the session of a sign-in whose answer it refuses. **Kept (2026-11-19):** the apps and `andvari-cli` record a refresh as out before the request goes, so that a lost connection, a timeout, a cancelled caller or the end of the app leaves the record; only an answer in the server's own envelope clears it (one over the device's size limit does not); they try once more only within fifty seconds and never a third time, and where no try is left they send nothing, end the session on the device with its own reason and keep the token for the next sign-in on the same row to name; the apps put the record back where their own guard stopped the request before anything was sent; the extension's password sign-in names the session it replaces, from the pair kept for quick unlock for this server and this address only. The record is put back where the transport can tell that nothing left the device since 2026-11-19 (closing the gap of 2026-11-18); the app interface hands the device's own end to the screens as its own since 2026-11-19 (a field of its own on the lock screen, an open copy and the first screen, across a restart), and both apps draw it since 2026-11-19, the desktop's own lines for work overtaken by the end and a change cut by it included; beside a way out that signs that account out first, the shared code hands a line for that end without "sign in again" and both apps draw that one there since 2026-11-19. **The browser and the extension** (rules of 2026-11-19, spec 03 §2; kept in both since 2026-11-19; before that, neither tried a refresh that got no answer once more): two sends at most inside fifty seconds (a web tab that is alive makes the second itself, twenty-five seconds after the first), then the client's own end, never said as the server's or as "expired", with the token kept for the next sign-in's name; an answer is the server's own only with both its error code and its message; with the offline copy on, a tab sends a refresh token only if the shared record holds that same pair at that moment, so such a browser holds one account's session at a time (a tab whose account the record no longer holds ends at its next renewal and its server session lapses unheld); a person's own sign-out of an account removes that account's kept token and says that the end could not be confirmed and how to end it (in the web client: sign in again on this browser and then sign out, or remove this browser from the devices list on another device; in the extension a sign-in again ends it); on the extension a lock while the mark stands and a quick unlock send nothing and end as not confirmed, never as "revoked", the quick-unlock record going and the caught logins kept and counted on the notice. Kept since 2026-11-19 besides: a standing line in the extension's locked popup for that end, kept at rest until the next unlock; the web client's own line where exactly one change was lost at such an end. |
| Login limits | The flat per-IP login limit and the email-keyed exponential backoff stay as they are (spec 03 §8). |
| Rate limits (S3-Q1) | **Every route has a bucket.** Unauthenticated, per IP: `GET /instance` 60 a minute; `GET /links/{id}` 30 a minute; device-link claim 10 a minute; device-link poll and reveal 60 a minute; `POST /recovery/requests` 5 an hour; recovery poll 60 a minute; and the sign-up and register buckets of §7.2. A bucket's name is its budget: routes share one only by naming the same bucket (spec 03 §0.1). Authenticated, per device, wherever this document names no bucket: reads 60 a minute and writes 60 an hour; device-link create 10 an hour; every re-authenticated route 5 a minute; attachment upload 600 an hour, beside its existing cap on concurrent uploads, so an import is not throttled (IN-1). Every bucket of a route that needs a session is kept per device, with a ceiling per account of four times the device's limit (rule of 2026-11-17, spec 03 §0.1): a session held without the password then spends only its own device's budget and cannot keep the owner from any route on another device, while the ceiling stops whoever has the password from multiplying a budget by signing in on more devices. The refresh is drawn from the device for a token that names a live session (`refresh_session`), from the caller's address only otherwise (`refresh`), and the reuse of a consumed token draws neither. Over a bucket: `429 rate_limited` with `Retry-After`; when the device's budget refuses while the account's ceiling is full too, the later of the two waits. |
| Client-set expiries (S3-Q6, SR-1) | **Links** (`POST /links`): the server accepts `now < expiresAt <= now + maximum + 10 min` by its own clock (maximum 7 days for `connect`, 72 hours for `register`) and stores the value as sent, which is also the value signed in the offer. The 5-minute minimum is the apps' rule, not the server's: a device whose clock runs behind still gets its link, which simply lives a little less by the server's clock. Anything else is `400 bad_expiry`. **Device links** (`POST /device-links`): the expiry exists only on the server, so the server owns it. It stores `min(expiresAt, now + 5 min)` and refuses only `expiresAt <= now` (`400 bad_expiry`); that stored value is the `open` state's timeout. **Both** answer `201 {expiresAt, ttlMs}`: the stored value and the milliseconds left. The creating device counts down from `ttlMs`, never from its own clock. The later device-link states keep their own server timers (§8.2). |
| Re-authentication (S3-K1, SR-3) | Every route that takes `currentAuthKey` on a live session answers a wrong one with `403 reauth_failed`: `PUT /account/password`, `PUT /account/recovery`, `PUT /recovery/self-setup` (rotating the kit), device revoke, `PUT /account/require-link`, and the operator's † routes. A `401` there would read as an expired session. Routes with no session keep the uniform `401`: login, device-link pickup, and `/recovery/self/verify` and `/commit`, which run on a recovery ticket. |
| Two-step sign-in (TOTP) | Optional per account. Enrolled TOTP is verified on every login. `ANDVARI_TOTP_REQUIRED=1` keeps today's restricted-session rule (spec 03 §2). The `totp_required` residual (spec 05 T11) is unchanged. On a signed-in session (rules of 2026-11-16 and 2026-11-17): a wrong code counts on a back-off of its own, with the password proofs' curve, which refuses only the checking of codes; that back-off and the proofs' back-off are kept per device, so a session held without the password can never make the owner's other devices wait (spec 03 §8). Setting two-step sign-in up always asks for the master password, also right after a sign-in, and turning it off, or replacing its secret on an account that has one, needs the master-password proof, checked before the code is looked at, so a session in the wrong hands can neither remove the second factor by guessing nor add one of its own. The server keeps all of this, and the shared code, the web client and the Android and desktop apps send the proof (spec 03 §2). The released 0.29 line and 0.30.0, which is cut as it is (§0.6, 2026-11-16 23:03), turn it off on the code alone. |
| Available everywhere | Registration, account deletion, self-recovery, operator-assisted recovery, sharing and links are all served on the single origin, under their own rate limits. |
| HSTS | `ANDVARI_FORCE_HSTS=1` is mandatory whenever the origin is https (`andvari setup` sets it). |

---

## 8. Device linking and the devices list

### 8.1 URL and QR

The existing device E shows:

```
https://andvaripass.com/d#v2.<O>.<S>        (§6.1's encoding without F; s = 32 CSPRNG bytes; expires after 5 minutes)
```

The fragment has **exactly three** `.`-separated parts: `v2`, `O` and `S` (FI-B12). Everything else parses as in §6.1: total parsing, white space stripped, canonical base64url, a 32-byte secret, the origin grammar, at most 512 characters. `devicelink.json` carries the parse rows.

From `s`: `linkId` and `macKey` (§2.3).

E shows the QR with the instance beside it: "On the new device, open andvari → **Sign in → Link from your other device** and scan this. It links to *name* (*origin*)." So the person can compare the address with the one the new device shows (step 0).

### 8.2 Protocol

The device link uses the committed code (T-B1) and **the person types the new device's digits** on the existing device (T-R9). The new device gets a session **only after it proves the master password** (T-R9), and it proves it only to an origin it pinned **before** the link began (T2-B1). The trust records travel under a key derived from the UVK, so only a device that can unlock the account can open or forge them (T-B1).

0. **N's entry and pin (T2-B1).**
   - **Entry.** N runs a device link only from its own **"Link from your other device"** screen: the scanner there, the paste field there (desktop, or a phone whose camera is unavailable), or `andvari-cli link-device <link>`. A `/d` link that arrives any other way starts nothing: tapped, opened by another app, or scanned or pasted on "Scan an invite". The app then shows "To add this device to your account, open **Sign in → Link from your other device** and scan the code there." The apps do not register `/d` as an app link (§0.3, §12.1), so a tapped `/d` link only reaches the scriptless page (§6.1).
   - **Pin before claim.** N needs a **password pin** (§4.4) for the link's origin: from the baked root, or one it made earlier through the Trust Gate. A pin that came only from a link offer, an enrol link, a kit or setup does not count for a device link (FS-B1). Otherwise it shows the Trust Gate with the device-link wording (§12.3), which names the origin and asks the person to check it against the address E shows (§8.1). Declining ends the flow, and nothing has been sent to the origin.
   - The CLI's `link-device` takes the origin from its own configuration, or asks the person to confirm the printed origin and operator key (its Trust Gate).
1. **E creates the link:** `POST /api/v1/device-links {linkId, expiresAt}`. Authenticated. The server stores `min(expiresAt, now + 5 min)` by its own clock as the `open` state's timeout, refuses only `expiresAt <= now` (`400 bad_expiry`), and answers `201 {expiresAt, ttlMs}`; E's countdown runs from `ttlMs` (SR-1, §7.8). An account has at most one live device link: creating one **cancels** every other link of the account that has not been picked up, whether `open`, `claimed`, `revealed` or `approved` (R12, S3-C3). No device or session exists before pickup, so nothing else needs undoing.
2. **N claims it.** The new device N, after step 0, generates an ephemeral X25519 key pair `(nPub, nSec)` and a code nonce `nonceN`, then sends `POST /api/v1/device-links/{linkId}/claim {nPub, name, platform, commitN, mac}` to the origin it pinned.
   - `commitN = commit(nonceN)` (§2.3).
   - `name` travels in the `name` type (§2.2: unpadded base64url of 1–64 UTF-8 bytes), and the same string goes into the MAC; `platform` is one of §2.3's values (FI-R6).
   - `mac = b64url(HMAC-SHA-256(macKey, "andvari/v2|device-link|claim|" + linkId + "|" + nPub + "|" + b64url(UTF-8 name) + "|" + platform + "|" + commitN))`.
   - The route is unauthenticated, limited to 10 per minute per IP. The first claim wins (`409 link_claimed`).
3. **E answers with its nonce.** E polls `GET /api/v1/device-links/{linkId}` (authenticated), which answers `{state, expiresAt, claim?: {nPub, name, platform, commitN, mac}, nonceE?, nonceN?}` (FI-B6). E receives the claim and verifies `mac` (a mismatch is an anomaly). It then posts `POST /device-links/{linkId}/nonce {nonceE, proofHash}`, with `nonceE` freshly drawn, **after** it has seen `commitN`.
   - `proofHash = hexLower(SHA-256(proof))`, for the `proof` of step 7, which E can compute from `macKey` and `nPub` (FI-B6). The server keeps it and checks every later `proof` from N against it: on the poll, the reveal and the pickup.
   - **E fixes the claim when it draws `nonceE`** (T2-R-b). A later poll that shows a different claim (`nPub`, `name`, `platform` or `commitN`) cancels the link.
   - The server accepts one `nonce` post per link (`409 link_state` on a second).
4. **N reveals.** N fetches `nonceE` with `POST /device-links/{linkId}/poll {proof}` (the proof of step 7), and posts `POST /device-links/{linkId}/reveal {nonceN, proof}` (FI-B6). E checks `commit(nonceN) = commitN` (`bad_reveal`).
   - **The poll's answers (FI-B6).** Until E's nonce exists: `{state}`. Then `{state, nonceE}`. After E approves: `{state, nonceE, outer, approveMac}`, which is how N receives `outer` and `approveMac` for step 7. The server returns `nonceE`, `outer` and `approveMac` only when `SHA-256(proof)` equals `proofHash`. Once `proofHash` exists, a poll, reveal or pickup with any other `proof` is `403 bad_proof`.
   - **N holds the first `nonceE` it received** (T2-R-b). It reveals only after holding it, computes the code from that value, and treats any different `nonceE` served later as an anomaly that cancels the link. Otherwise the server, with someone who read the QR, could show E their own claim, then pick a new `nonceE` after seeing `nonceN` and make N display the code E computes for that claim.
   - The server accepts one reveal per link.
5. **Both compute the code** from `linkId`, `nPub`, `nonceN` and `nonceE` (§2.3). N shows it. E shows "Add *name* (*platform*)? Type the six digits shown on the new device". **The digits must be typed into E**; a tap is not enough. They are compared as text under §6.11's rule (spaces removed, then exactly six digits). A mismatch cancels the link, and a typed string that is not six digits after removing spaces is a mismatch like any other, so the confirm control is enabled only once six digits are present.
6. **E approves.** After Face ID, fingerprint or the master password, E sends `POST /api/v1/device-links/{linkId}/approve {outer, inner, approveMac}`.
   - **Approving with the device's own lock** (rule of 2026-11-17) means the strong fingerprint or face check that quick unlock uses, or the master password, and never the device's PIN or passcode. The session code has one call for it (`QuickUnlockKeeper.confirmPresence`): behind the system's prompt it opens the sealed quick-unlock key, compares it in constant time with the open account's key, wipes it and answers; nothing else may stand in for the approval. It sends nothing and does not restart quick unlock's 30 days. It answers confirmed only for the open account's own key; cancelled for a cancelled prompt; "use the password" for every reason quick unlock would ask for the password (not switched on for this account, no strong biometric, a policy that forbids keys at rest or was not seen within 7 days, 30 days since the full password, a reboot with no server time) and for a lockout; turned off, with the sealed record wiped, for a record that no longer opens or opens to another key; and stale when no vault is open, when a lock overtakes the prompt, or when a second confirmation is asked while one is up, so that an approval a lock overtakes sends nothing (spec 01, "Keys in memory and the lock"). A prompt shows the reason the app passes, which names the device being added: its title is "Add *name* (*platform*)?" (step 5's words), with a fallback title for a reason that is missing. **A sentence that a system prompt shows is worded and approved like every other sentence a person reads** (rule of 2026-11-17), although the system's prompt cannot carry the mark of a sentence that still awaits its words: until the title and its fallback are approved, the title stays as step 5 words it, and both show unmarked. **A cancelled prompt is a result of its own** (rule of 2026-11-17), on every road that asks for a fingerprint or face check: nothing was approved, nothing is wrong with the check, and the screen stays where it was. **A prompt answered after the link's time has run out** (rule of 2026-11-17) says that the link has expired, not that something failed. **What the code keeps (2026-11-17):** the app interface approves through that one call and offers the fingerprint or face approval only where quick unlock can be used on this device; a confirmed check approves exactly as the password does; a cancelled prompt answers that it was cancelled and the new device still waits; "use the password" and a lockout send the person to the password; quick unlock turned off by the check is shown as off; and a lock while the prompt is up approves nothing and wipes what was opened. The Android app offers the approval behind its own button where the app interface offers it, in a prompt titled with the device's name and with a fallback title of its own, never the unlock prompt's. **Kept (2026-11-17, evening):** a prompt answered after the link's time ran out is sent and refused by the server, and the app interface says that the link has expired, not that something failed; the device does not judge the time itself, since the server times each step from when it began.
   - `outer = crypto_box_seal(nPub, {"v":2,"origin":…,"userId":…,"email":…})`: just enough for N to sign in.
   - `inner = Envelope(bundleKey, bundle JSON (§8.3), AD = "andvari/v2|device-link-bundle|" + linkId)`, with `bundleKey` derived from the **UVK** (§2.3).
   - `approveMac = b64url(HMAC-SHA-256(macKey, "andvari/v2|device-link|approve|" + linkId + "|" + hexLower(SHA-256(raw outer))))`.
   - **It covers `outer` only (FC-3)**, so N can check it before the password, when the poll has given it `outer` but not `inner`. `inner` needs no MAC: it opens only under the UVK-derived `bundleKey`, with this link's id in its AD, so nobody without the UVK can make or swap it (a UVK holder working with the server can, R35, FN-3), and N checks it after the pickup (step 8).
   - The server records the approval and **creates no session yet**.
7. **N proves the password, then picks up.**
   - N opens `outer` with `nSec`, checks that its `origin` equals the origin it pinned in step 0, and checks `approveMac` over `outer` (FC-3). A server that alone made up an `outer` for an email of its choosing fails here, before any password prompt.
   - It asks for the **master password** on a screen that names the account and the instance: "Master password for *email* on *name* (*origin*)" (§4.4). It runs prelogin for `email`, derives `authKey`, and calls `POST /api/v1/device-links/{linkId}/pickup {proof, authKey, totp?, device: {deviceSecret}}` (`deviceSecret`: §8.7, FI-R10; the name and platform are the claim's), where `proof = b64url(HMAC-SHA-256(macKey, "andvari/v2|device-link|pickup|" + linkId + "|" + nPub))`. Prelogin and pickup go **only** to the origin pinned in step 0.
   - The server verifies `authKey` exactly like a login: verifier, backoff and the TOTP rules. An account with two-step sign-in answers `401 totp_required` until `totp` carries a valid code; N then asks for it (N3). A wrong password or code is the uniform `401`, counted against the email backoff.
   - Only then does it create the device and session (`how = linked`, `linkedFrom = E's deviceId`) and answer once: `{inner, session: SessionResponse}`, the same answer a login gives (the role, the two-step flags and the account keys are inside it). The row is deleted and the new-device email is sent.
8. **N finishes.** N unlocks the UVK, opens `inner` under `bundleKey` (`bundle_unopenable`), requires the seed-derived identity key to equal `bundle.identityPub` and the genesis to verify (§5.2), then imports the records (§8.3) and offers quick unlock.
   - **The bundle confirms the pin (T2-B1).** `bundle.origin` and `bundle.instanceId` must equal the pinned card's, and N's verified chain must contain `bundle.card` at its `seq` (N fetches forward first if its chain ends below). A mismatch signs N out, removes the pin it made in step 0, and shows "This isn't the server your other device uses. Nothing was linked."
   - **On a device that already keeps the account** (rules of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7): the pickup follows the sign-in's rule in full. The kept keys are made ready before anything is derived for the pickup, and the key the wrap opens is compared with what the device keeps before the account is opened; another key stops as "the server's keys are not this account's as this device knows it" and replaces nothing; the same key under another master password links and says so in one line. A pickup the device refuses ends exactly the session it opened, by its own refresh token, never with the logout. The shared code does this. A copy whose sealed identity seed does not open is refused here (no wrap of it could be compared when it was sealed under another password), and the screen says the way out: remove this device's copy (which first says how many changes were not sent, or that it cannot tell), then link again. The refusal reaches the screen as what it is: "the server's keys are not this account's as this device knows it", never "another account is kept" and never a general failure (rule of 2026-11-17). **Kept (2026-11-17):** the shared code, the app interface and `andvari-cli` end a refused pickup's session alone; the app interface tells each refusal as what it is and shows the one line; both apps draw each refusal of the session keeper as itself and offer the removal of this device's copy where the shared code names it as the way out (2026-11-17, evening): the desktop on the link's password screen and on the ended link; the phone on the ended link only, and only while its first screen names that copy (the password and code screens offer nothing, since the pickup ends the link first) (established by reading the screens' code, 2026-11-19).
   - **On a device that keeps ANOTHER account**, an account at another server, or its server's operator account (rules of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7): the link stops before anything is committed, at every step where the device can know: at its start, by the server alone, before the claim; once the link names the account, before the password is asked; and before every pickup. The stop is said as what it is: "this device keeps another account: sign it out first" (the sign-out says how many changes were not sent, §4.4), the identity fault for an address this device knows as another account, or, on a device that holds its server's operator account, that it must be removed from this device first. A link refused after its pickup keeps nothing: the link is spent, the password unchanged, and the session ended alone. **What the device cannot know before the pickup, said and not changed (2026-11-17; spec 05 T1):** the link's bundle does not carry the account's identity key in the clear, so the device's question before the pickup cannot compare it; an address this device knows bound to the same account under another key is refused only after the pickup, by the session keeper. Only a hostile server can produce that case; the link is then spent and the session it opened is ended alone. The shared code, the app interface and `andvari-cli` keep this (2026-11-17). **An address this device knows under another account** (rule of 2026-11-18, §4.4 "What a device remembers of an account"): the link stops with that address, at the approval, before the pickup and at the take, so that the earlier account can be forgotten there as at a sign-in. **Kept (2026-11-19):** the shared code stops so, as a field on the existing identity stop, names the address only where it is tied to ANOTHER account id than the one served (never for the same account under another key, §4.4), and offers the forgetting at the link's end only where its stop names the address. Both apps draw that offer at a link's end since 2026-11-19, by the same code as at a sign-in.
   - **Every loop around a link's steps waits between two asks, in every state** (rules of 2026-11-17), and a refusal for too many requests is never said as a server that cannot be reached. Where a step can wait within the link's time (a poll, a reveal, a view), it waits for the time the server named, at least a second and at most the link's own time; where it cannot (the nonce, the approval, the claim, the pickup), the refusal is said as what it is, with the wait the server named. Each client that drives the steps in a loop of its own says where its loop waits, with a test that counts the requests while the other side is slow. A device that asked again without a pause while the other device had not answered used up its own allowance within seconds, and the link ended as unreachable: a fault a person on a fast connection could meet. **Kept (2026-11-18):** the shared code waits out a refusal for too many requests on a waiting step for the time the server named (thirty seconds where it named none), at least a second, while the link's own time lasts: a step asked for before that wait ends is answered as it stood, with nothing sent, and a wait that outlasts the link ends it as expired. On the nonce, the approval, the claim, the pickup's first exchange and the pickup it stops with a reason of its own for too many requests and the wait the server named. The app interface asks a waiting link again every two seconds, and both apps' link screens every second. `andvari-cli` says each such wait once with the server's time and goes on, pauses two seconds on the approval's view while it waits, says a stop for too many requests with the server's wait ("a while" where it named none), and its device that shows a link waits at a person's pace. At a link's pickup a proof the server refuses for a password that opens this device's copy keeps the link within its time, as a refused code does, and whether a code was asked belongs to the link, not to one call (the shared code since 2026-11-18; spec 03 §2). **Kept (2026-11-19), closing the gap of 2026-11-18:** the app interface gives each of the shared code's new stops its own result: a refused proof keeps the link's password screen with its own sentence; a code refused at the pickup leaves the link open, adds no tries and returns to the password screen; a used code step goes back to the password; a link refused for too many requests ends with its own line and the wait, never "unreachable", and the showing device's own end says its line in place of "a check failed". Both apps draw the "too many requests" end with the wait and the showing device's own line since 2026-11-19; the desktop also says such a refusal with words and the wait where it has no sentence of its own (at a device link's scan and at a link's start), and once beside the ended link's card. **The wait of an ended link** (rule of 2026-11-19): the end keeps the moment its wait runs out; while it runs the line says what is left of it, and after it the line says that the wait the server named is over, never that the server named none. Kept in the shared code since 2026-11-19, on the new device and on the showing device: the end keeps that moment, on the clock the server's wait counts on, says what is left of it and, once it has run out, hands the line that the wait is over; where the server named no wait the end keeps none and its plain line stays. **Not kept yet (2026-11-19):** neither app reads the end's kept moment or draws that line yet.

If any check fails after the session arrived, N signs that session out at once.

**What this defeats.**
- A claim hijacked with a stolen QR, or with a key pair ground to match the code, cannot produce matching digits: the code is fixed only after the claimant committed.
- Even if the person types a wrong match carelessly, the claimant gets **no session and no `wrappedUvk`** without the master password, so there is nothing to crack offline.
- A forged bundle cannot open under a UVK-derived key.
- **A device link minted on another server** (T2-B1). An attacker runs `evil.example`, mints a `/d` link there and sends it to the victim. Their server plays E, skips the digits, returns an `outer` with the victim's email, and relays the real instance's prelogin, hoping for the real `authKey`. It gets nothing unless the person confirms the Trust Gate for its address (residual below):
  - N does not act on a `/d` link it did not open from its own "Link from your other device" screen;
  - N sends no prelogin, claim or `authKey` to an origin without a password pin;
  - for any origin but the baked one, the Trust Gate names that origin before anything is sent.

**Residual (R22).** A person who starts linking, is handed an attacker's QR instead of their own device's, and confirms the Trust Gate for an address that is not their server's, gives that server their `authKey`. Both devices show the address (§8.1, step 0) to make that visible. On the public instance in a store build the baked pin makes the gate unnecessary, and a link for any other address shows it.

**States (R12):**

| State | Leaves by | Timeout |
|---|---|---|
| `open` | a claim → `claimed`; cancel or replacement → deleted | at the stored `expiresAt`, at most 5 minutes after creation by the server's clock (SR-1), then deleted |
| `claimed` | E's nonce, then N's reveal → `revealed`; cancel → deleted | 5 minutes from the claim, then deleted |
| `revealed` | E's approve → `approved`; cancel or a typed mismatch → deleted | 5 minutes, then deleted |
| `approved` | N's pickup with a correct password → deleted, and the device is created | 5 minutes from the approval, then deleted. No device or session exists before pickup, so there is nothing to revoke. Repeated wrong passwords hit the email backoff. |

**Cancel:** `DELETE /api/v1/device-links/{linkId}` (E's session) in any state before pickup. Either side's screen shows "Linking was cancelled".

**Errors:** `409 link_claimed` (a second claim), `410 link_expired`, `404` (unknown or cancelled), `409 link_state` (a step out of order), `400 bad_reveal`, `403 bad_proof` (FI-B6).

**The server sees** the link id, both devices' name and platform, `nPub`, timing, and opaque blobs. Without `s` it cannot forge a claim, swap `nPub`, read the bundle, or produce an approval N accepts.

### 8.3 Bundle

The bundle is UTF-8 JSON, at most 64 KiB, carried as `inner`: an envelope under the UVK-derived bundle key (§8.2). Only a device that has unlocked this account can open it, and nobody without the UVK can forge one, the server and a link interceptor included. That is why its card checkpoint can confirm the pin the new device made before it claimed (§4.4, §8.2 step 8). The decoder is strict: exactly the keys below (in any order), no extra keys, every value of the listed type (R3).

```
{"v":2,"origin":…,"instanceId":…,"card":{"seq":…,"hash":…},"userId":…,"email":…,"identityPub":…,
 "personalVaultId":…,
 "contacts":[{"userId":…,"keyHash":…,"verifiedAt":…}],
 "vaults":[{"vaultId":…,"seq":…,"hash":…}],
 "accountState":{"seq":…,"hash":…},
 "issuedAt":…}
```

| Field | Type |
|---|---|
| `v` | the JSON number 2 |
| `origin` | `origin` (§2.2) |
| `instanceId`, `userId`, `personalVaultId`, each `contacts[].userId`, each `vaults[].vaultId` | `uuid` |
| `card.seq`, `vaults[].seq`, `accountState.seq` | `int` |
| `card.hash`, `vaults[].hash` | `hex64` |
| `accountState.hash` | `hex64`: SHA-256 over the ASCII bytes of the served `sealedState` string |
| `email` | the `emailNorm` string |
| `identityPub` | `b64_32` |
| `contacts[].keyHash` | `hex64`: SHA-256 over the raw 32 key bytes |
| `contacts[].verifiedAt`, `issuedAt` | `ms` |
| `contacts`, `vaults` | arrays of at most 1000 entries each |

How N imports each part:

- **Filling the 64 KiB (SR-2).** If the account's contacts and vault checkpoints do not all fit, E puts in the most recently verified first and leaves the rest out. N treats anything left out as a password-only device would: such a contact waits for the per-device prompt (§6.9), and such a vault is verified from its genesis against the account's record (§3.6 (e)). The decoder and its limits are unchanged.
- **`contacts`** make a contact **current** on N only if N's merged account state holds a contact with the same `userId` and key hash. The bundle confers currency, never contacts.
- **`vaults` and `card`** are verified checkpoints. A served log or card chain that contradicts one is a fork. One that ends below it is withholding (§3.9, §4.4).
- **`accountState`** is a high-water mark. Its `seq` is the counter **inside** the sealed account-state document, not the compare-and-set `seq` the server serves beside the blob (FI-B14, §5.4). A served state below it is a rollback, kept out; a different hash at the same `seq` is a fork. It is **not** a recovery mirror (§4.5, FS-R1).

### 8.4 Desktop and paste

A desktop without a camera pastes the link into its "Link from your other device" screen (E offers "Share link"). The codes and the pin step (§8.2 step 0) protect the paste path the same way as the QR.

### 8.5 Password sign-in, and the one-tap list

Signing in with email and password still works, and sends the new-device email. Such a device has no current contacts. It shows once:

> **People you've connected with**
>
> You signed in without linking from another device, so andvari can't yet tell whether this list is current. The server keeps it sealed under your account's key. On its own, the server can hide people or bring back someone you removed, but it can't add anyone. Whoever holds your account's key could: a page from a dishonest server while you used andvari on the web{, or the operator, because you chose operator recovery}. Untick anyone you don't recognise.
>
> [list with checkboxes, all ticked] [ Use these on this device ] [ Later ]

The part in braces appears only for an account whose recovery record says `operator`. Confirming makes the ticked contacts current on this device. This is the R23 residual (§16), and the copy states it as it is (T2-T-R6).

### 8.6 "Your devices"

**Listing:** `GET /api/v1/account/devices` returns, for each device:

```
[{ deviceId, platform, name, clientVersion, createdAt, lastSeenAt, live,
   how: "registered"|"password"|"linked"|"recovered", linkedFrom?, current }]
```

**Revoking:** `POST /api/v1/account/devices/{deviceId}/revoke {currentAuthKey}` revokes another of the account's own devices. Every revoke, by the person or by the operator (§9.2), also clears that device's `deviceSecret` hash, so it is no longer a known device (§8.7, FC-8). It needs a fresh password proof, sends `revoked` to that device's sockets, and sends the "device removed" email. The admin device routes become operator routes (§9.2).

### 8.7 Known devices, and "New devices must be linked" (default I)

**`deviceSecret` is in IF-2 now, as a defined field (FI-R10).** Collecting it from the first device on means that turning `requireLink` on never strands a device, and never reopens the frozen register, login and pickup bodies.
- Every request that creates a device carries `device.deviceSecret`: register (§7.3), password login (`POST /auth/login`, as `device: {platform, name, deviceSecret, deviceId?}`, S3-Q2), device-link pickup (§8.2 step 7), and the kit and assisted recovery commits (§10, §9.3).
- `deviceSecret` is 32 CSPRNG bytes as unpadded base64url, generated by the device and kept in its device state. The server stores `hex SHA-256(deviceSecret)` with the device row and never returns it.
- A password login always sends `device: {platform, name, deviceSecret, deviceId?}` (S3-Q2). It is from a **known device** only when `deviceId` names a device row of that account that is not revoked and whose stored secret hash equals the hash of the `deviceSecret` sent. No live session is needed: a device that signed out, sat idle or saw its password changed elsewhere is still known, and a device row is never pruned while its secret hash is set (only a revoke clears it), or `requireLink` would lock its owner out; the session then reuses that device row, and no new-device email goes out. That row's earlier sessions end with the sign-in (a device has one live session, a browser's row excepted; rule of 2026-11-17, §7.8, which the server keeps since 2026-11-18). Anything else is a new device: with `requireLink` on it is refused (below), and otherwise a device row is created and the new-device email sent. So a wrong or missing secret can never dodge `requireLink`.
- `users.requireLink`: when set, a password login that is not from a known device is refused with `403 link_required`. That answer comes only **after** `authKey`, and any two-step code, has verified, so it tells nobody without the password that the account exists or has the setting on; a wrong password still gets the uniform `401` (FC-8). A revoked device is not known (§8.6). It is set with `PUT /api/v1/account/require-link {enabled, currentAuthKey}` (fresh password proof). The route ships at launch; only the Settings switch that calls it may come later, and that changes no route or body.
- A successful recovery with the kit, or an operator-assisted recovery, clears `requireLink` and sends an email. Without that, a person who lost every device would be locked out.

---

## 9. The operator

### 9.1 The operator account

- It is created through the setup link (§6.7). There is exactly one per instance: `users.role = 'operator'` is enforced unique.
- The card names it.
- **Handover** is a signed card update (§4.3). After a handover the old operator account is an ordinary member.
  - From the command line: the new operator's `andvari-cli account signing-key` prints their user id and signing key from their own device's copy of the account, and the current operator runs `andvari-cli operator hand-over --to-user <id> --to-key <key>`. The tool checks both against their grammar and, where this account holds that person as a contact, the key against the contact's; says what the old operator keeps and loses (the recovery sheet still opens copies sealed to its key until a rotation); asks for the typed confirmation `HAND OVER <first 8>`; then signs the next card. The server refuses a card whose key is not that account's (`400 bad_card`). The new operator signs out and in again before signing a card, because a saved session keeps the role it was made with. On the integration branch (2026-11-16) a session saved before the hand-over is also refused the console and `andvari-cli`'s other operator commands until that fresh sign-in.
  - **On a phone** (owner, 2026-11-16): when a card this phone verified names the signed-in account as the operator, the phone stops using the account at once and sends nothing more with it, keeps its copy on the device, locked, and lets the person remove it. As in §12.2 rule 7, the verified card decides, not a role the server reports. The account is used from the desktop app or `andvari-cli`. Built in the app engine (2026-11-16): the phone wipes the account's keys and its quick-unlock record and keeps no token, sends nothing, not even a sign-out, and deletes nothing; its first screen says so until the person removes the account. **An account held this way stays held when a later card names someone else as the operator** (rule of 2026-11-16, open to the owner's review): nothing releases it by itself; the person removes it from the phone and signs in again. The app engine does this: it keeps the account held until the person removes it.
- It is excluded from the cap, and has a personal vault like any account. It cannot be deleted while it is the operator.
- **On a public instance** it is operator-only: the UI says it holds no everyday data, and a handover target must own no shared vault.
- **On every instance, public or private, it is operator-only** (T-B4, which revises the first draft of default P). On a private instance the person who runs the family server keeps an ordinary everyday account beside it. Family members are invited from that everyday account with register links (§6.6), so nobody needs to trust the operator account for sharing.
- **It never signs in on the web client (T-B4).**
  - Operator routes refuse a session minted for `X-Andvari-Client: web/…` (`403 operator_web_refused`), and the web client has no operator login.
  - The operator's master password is typed only into native apps or `andvari-cli`, never into code the server serves. So a later server compromise cannot harvest it, unlock the operator's seed and sign cards.
  - The web client has **no operator surface at all** (N9). Operator views ship in the **desktop app** and in **`andvari-cli operator …`**, which covers every §9.2 route, so a family with only phones and the server host can run their server from the host's shell or any computer.
- **Its recovery must not depend on itself.** The kit is printed and kept with the instance recovery sheet. Card updates are rare and can be signed from `andvari-cli` on an offline machine. Losing the operator: §4.6.

### 9.2 Powers

Every operator route needs the operator session from a native app or the CLI (§9.1). Routes marked † also need a fresh `currentAuthKey`, carried in the JSON body like every existing re-authenticated route; a † route that only reads is therefore a `POST` (FI-R2). Every call that changes something or hands out a recovery copy is audited; a read of a copy for an account that does not exist writes no row. The audit list is served in pages, newest first, and the console says when a page is full and older rows exist (rule of 2026-11-16). Audit and email are transparency for an honest operator, not controls against a hostile one (G7).

| Route | What it does |
|---|---|
| `GET /api/v1/admin/accounts` | Per account: email, displayName, created, lastSeen, status, recovery posture, storage used, item and owned-vault counts, live device count. |
| `POST /admin/accounts/{id}/disable {reason}` † | Revokes sessions and emails the person. The operator cannot disable or delete its own account (`400 cannot_target_self`, S3-C8). |
| `POST /admin/accounts/{id}/enable` † | Re-enables the account and emails the person. |
| `POST /admin/accounts/{id}/delete` † | Only for accounts disabled at least 30 days (W2, §0.1, confirmed by the owner 2026-11-15, §0.6). It runs the account-deletion transaction (spec 03 §13) **without leaves** (§3.11). Refused while the account owns a shared vault with other members. The `account_deleted_by_operator` email goes to the address captured in the request. |
| `GET /admin/accounts/{id}/devices`, `POST /admin/devices/{id}/revoke` † | Lists devices; a revoke emails the person. |
| `PUT /admin/accounts/{id}/quota` † | Per-account overrides. |
| `GET /admin/policy`; `PUT /admin/policy` † (the † is on the `PUT` only, FI-R2) | <ul><li>Quota defaults, and the tighten-only sign-up controls (§7.1).</li><li>The client policy fields that stay: `minVersion`, `kdfParams`, `autoLockSeconds`, `clipboardClearSeconds`, `offlineCacheAllowed` (operator floor, ANDed with `ANDVARI_OFFLINE_CACHE_ALLOWED`), the session TTLs, `attachmentMaxBytes`, `itemAttachmentsMaxBytes`.</li><li>`totpRequired` stays an environment setting.</li><li>**The hashing cost** (rules of 2026-11-17; spec 03 §7.2, spec 01 §7): the cost served is never below the server's floor, and the operator's view shows the cost in force, the stored one raised to the floor (a console that stores what it was shown keeps the raised figure, and lowering the floor later does not bring the earlier one back). A floor must lie inside the configuration's range, at or below the server's own limit for a stored cost (4 GiB, 64 passes) and at or below what the clients accept (1 GiB, 10 passes); a floor outside any of them stops the server at its start, naming the value and the range, and never falls back quietly. Every client makes a new wrap at the higher, figure by figure, of the account's cost and the served one, inside its own limits, and at the served cost where there is no account yet. A policy written later is held to the same limits, and the server refuses a new wrap below the served cost on either figure, so that on a server whose operator raised the policy a client that does not take the higher of the two on each figure cannot register, recover or change a password there. **Kept (2026-11-18):** the server keeps all of this, and every client of this version (the web client, and the apps and `andvari-cli` through the shared code) makes each new wrap at the higher of the two on each figure. The quiet re-key is due when the account is below the served cost on any figure, and raises that figure without lowering the other (rules of 2026-11-17 and 2026-11-18); the apps keep this through the shared engine since 2026-11-18. **Not kept yet (2026-11-18):** in the web client an account above the floor on one figure and below it on the other is not raised by the quiet re-key, only at its next password change or recovery (spec 01 §7).</li><li>Deleted from the policy: `recoveryFingerprint` (the card carries `recoveryFp`) and `userAttachmentsMaxBytes` (replaced by the account quota) (R6).</li></ul> |
| `GET /admin/audit` | Audit log, with filters. |
| `GET /admin/status` | Version, storage, sign-up state, counts. |
| `GET /admin/recovery-requests` | Verified assisted-recovery requests (§9.3): request id, account, the address the request was made for, account ID, `rPub`, state, times and platform. **Never the request code** (T2-R-c). The console and `andvari-cli operator` check every field against its grammar before they show it, and never offer the account ID as a value to check against (§9.3 step 3). |
| `POST /admin/accounts/{id}/escrow {currentAuthKey}` † | Operator-posture accounts only: answers the escrow blob. A `POST`, so it can carry the password proof (FI-R2). Emails the person "the operator downloaded your recovery copy". |
| `POST /admin/recovery-requests/{id}/fulfil {sealed, requestKey, commitHash, currentAuthKey}` † | §9.3 step 3; the body's checks and their order are spec 03 §18.6. It may be repeated until the commit. |
| `PUT /api/v1/instance/card {card, currentAuthKey}` † | §4.3. |
| `PUT /api/v1/instance/rotation {statement, currentAuthKey}` † | §4.3 (FI-R3). |

**Through these routes, and without the offline recovery seed, the operator cannot:**
- read vault contents or names;
- see contacts or link contents;
- sign as a member, or add or remove members;
- transfer ownership;
- create links on someone's behalf;
- raise the cap or open sign-up from the panel.

**Except for an account that chose operator recovery** (T2-T-R6). An operator who opens that account's escrow with the recovery seed holds its UVK and identity seed, and can do every item above **as that person**: read their vaults and the shared vaults they belong to, sign as them, add or remove members of vaults they own, and write their account state (§7.7, R3, R35). On the public instance under variant R, no account is in that position.

### 9.3 Operator-assisted recovery (operator-posture accounts only; absent on the public instance under variant R, §7.7)

This replaces the temporary-password bundle: no temporary password exists at any point. The threat review (T-B5) showed that an emailed code alone authenticates only the inbox. So the request now has to survive four checks:
- a **7-day hold**, during which the account's signed-in devices can **cancel** it;
- a **request code** that the person reads to the operator over a channel the operator trusts;
- a payload **signed by the offline recovery key**, so no server can fake the operator's answer;
- copy that says plainly who can ask.

1. **The person asks.** On a fresh install, "I lost my master password **and** my recovery kit" → "Ask the operator for help". The screen says: "Anyone who gets into your email can ask too. The operator waits 7 days and checks with you, and your other devices can cancel." The app makes an X25519 pair `(rPub, rSec)` and a 32-byte `pollSecret`, then sends `POST /api/v1/recovery/requests {email, rPub, pollHash = hex SHA-256(pollSecret), platform, mailboxValue?}` (the last only when the person entered a mailbox value, below). `platform` is one of §2.3's values; it is self-reported, and every list that shows it says so (S3-Q4).
   - `rSec` and `pollSecret` must survive the 7-day hold on a fresh install that has no account yet (N11). They are kept in device state protected like a session (Keychain or Keystore), until commit, veto or 30 days.
   - The route is unauthenticated, limited per IP. Every request it accepts is answered `202 {requestId}`, whatever the address (the refusals that come before, for a malformed body, a malformed address or a stalled mail outbox, are alike for every address; spec 03 §18.1).
   - For an operator-posture account, the email holds a 6-digit code. For a private account, the email says the account has no operator recovery and points to the kit. An unknown address gets nothing.
   - **The way out through the mailbox** (decided on 2026-11-17: built on the new line before the public launch; the nine points are spec 03 §18.1). The code mail of every request also carries a **mailbox value** (sixteen symbols of `ABCDEFGHJKLMNPQRSTUVWXYZ23456789`, 80 bits, shown in four groups of four; a typed value is brought to its form in ASCII only: spaces, tabs and dashes are removed and a to z raised, and any other character outside the alphabet, a letter from outside ASCII included, makes it a wrong value; stored only as a hash beside the address's hash, and only once its mail is queued; valid seven days; the private-posture notice carries none), and a short paragraph that says what the person needs: someone typed your address to ask for help with an account; if that was not you, you need do nothing else, but keep this value; if you ever ask for help yourself, enter it with your request, under the app's line for it (quoted as the apps word it); it works once, for this address, for seven days. The paragraph never sits under a sentence that tells the reader to ignore the mail. A request that names an unused, unexpired value of its address is taken as a real one even when the address's five of its window are spent (it uses the value up; such requests have a ceiling of their own of five per window, a value that ceiling refused is kept, and their mail carries a fresh value under a mail budget of its own of five per window; within the five a value is not needed and stays usable). Each cap counts in a window of 24 hours that starts at the first request it counts, and the address's window and the value's run independently: five plus five request mails per window, and across the windows' edges up to twenty inside 24 hours, more than ten only through values, which only a reader of the mailbox has. For an account in the private posture a request never uses a value, and a switch to that posture forgets the address's values; a password change keeps them. Any other value changes nothing, and anything in the field that is not a string, or a string over 64 characters, is refused as a fault of the request's form, alike for every address; the request's field is `mailboxValue`; the answer and its timing never differ. On the device, "Ask the operator for help" takes the value behind one quiet line that opens the field ("have a value from a mail about a request you did not make?"), in the Android, desktop and iPhone apps through the shared code, and as an option of `andvari-cli`. The mail's paragraph and the screen's line are entries on the owner's wording page; until approved they are marked placeholders. A device brings a typed value to its form exactly as the server does (ASCII only), and may refuse a value not of a value's form before anything is sent, saying only that it is not a value from such a mail, which tells nothing about any account. **What is kept (2026-11-17):** the server keeps all of it: the form in ASCII only, the private posture (no value used, the values forgotten at the switch), and a code mail whose paragraph stands under "Nothing happens without this code." with no line telling the reader to ignore the mail (its words still a marked placeholder); the shared code sends a value with a request (a typed value not of the form stops on the device as `bad_mailbox_value` with nothing sent, and the step stays). The shared code brings a typed value to its form in ASCII only, as the server does (2026-11-17), and `andvari-cli recover assist` takes the value from a file or the environment, never the command line, or asks one question for it at a terminal (2026-11-17). **Not kept yet (2026-11-17):** no app shows the line and the field (spec 03 §18.1).
   - **Lifetimes (FI-R9).** The emailed code is valid for 15 minutes. An unverified request expires 24 hours after creation. A verified request lives until commit or veto, or until 30 days after its hold ended, when it expires and the app drops `rSec` and `pollSecret`.
   - The app shows the **request code** (§2.3: 96 bits from `rPub`, six groups of four) with: "Read this to the operator when they contact you."
2. **The person proves the address.** `POST /recovery/requests/{id}/verify {code}`, with **5 attempts per request**; over that even the right code is refused with the same uniform `400 bad_code` as a wrong code (spec 03 §18.2). There is no separate cap on attempts per address: requests are themselves capped at 5 per address per window of 24 hours (below), so the guesses at the 6-digit code are bounded at 25 a window (the mailbox's holder, who reads the code anyway, can open up to five more with a mailbox value), and a stranger's wrong codes on a request of their own no longer use up the owner's attempts (a per-address cap let anyone lock the owner out for a day). A wrong, expired or used-up code is a uniform `400 bad_code`, as at sign-up (FC-6). The route also has a rate bucket per address of the caller, as the sign-up code's route has, under a name of its own (`recovery_request_verify`, so that it shares no budget with the kit's verify), and each wrong code is recorded in the audit, naming the account only when the request is real, so that a decoy answers exactly as a real request does (rules of 2026-11-16 and 2026-11-17). A request the account's owner cancelled before its code was entered (by a password change, a switch to private, or a recovery) is told so only here, at the code step: its poll never changed, so whoever opened it without reading the mailbox learns nothing (spec 03 §18.4). For the device that asked, that answer is a final stop: it forgets the request, offers no further code for it, and says that the request was cancelled because the account's password or recovery setting changed, or the account was recovered, after it was asked, and that a new request can be started; the shared code does this (the final stop `request_cancelled`), and a wrong code stays a refusal at which the step stays. The request becomes `verified`, its **hold starts (7 days)**, and two things happen:
   - **Every signed-in device of the account** gets a bell, and on its next unlock shows "Someone asked the operator to reset your account. If this wasn't you, cancel it."
     - **How the device finds the request (FI-B7).** `GET /api/v1/account/recovery-requests` (session) lists the account's live requests as `[{requestId, state, createdAt, verifiedAt, holdEndsAt, platform}]`, and `/sync` carries `recoveryRequests: <count>`.
     - Cancel sends `POST /recovery/requests/{id}/veto {statement}` **with the device's session for that account** (FI-R9); the session's `userId` must equal the statement's. The statement is a 0x0A frame, `{"v":2,"purpose":"recovery-veto","requestId":<b64_16>,"userId":<uuid>,"issuedAt":<ms>}`, signed by the account key; the server verifies it against the identity key it stores (N3). The request dies, and the cancellation is emailed.
   - The account's email gets a notice with the request's time and platform. No mail the server sends because of an assisted recovery carries **an account ID** (rule of 2026-11-16; the full list of templates is spec 03 §14.5): this server sends them, so they cannot be a source for the comparison in step 4. The account ID the person compares comes from their kit, or from a notice older than the request; which sources count, and what holds for a person with neither, is spec 04 §7. The server keeps this for every template on that list.
3. **The operator acts, after the hold and outside the server.**
   - The operator contacts the person through a channel they trust independently of the server and the inbox. On a family server that is natural: in person, or a phone number they already know. **For a stranger on the public instance there is usually no such channel** (no phone number is stored), and the check falls back to whatever the person proposes, such as a video call showing the app's request code (N11). That weakness is why the plan recommends variant R for the public instance (§7.7). They ask for the request code, and if possible the account ID.
   - **The panel and `andvari-cli operator` never display the request code** (T2-R-c). The operator gets it only from the person and types it into `recovery-cli assist`, which compares it with the code it computes from `rPub`. A displayed code could be copied without ever asking the person, and the check would be void.
   - This keeps an honest operator from skipping the check. It does not stop a hostile operator, who holds `rPub` and can compute the code (G7).
   - **Nothing the server says is offered as something to check against** (rule of 2026-11-16). The console and `andvari-cli operator` show a request's id and key (the tool needs both; a swapped key is caught by the code the person reads out) and the address the request was made for, each checked against its grammar before it is shown or copied. They do not show the server's account ID as a value to copy, and no sentence tells the operator to copy one.
   - On an offline machine the operator runs `recovery-cli assist`. Among its arguments is the **address of the person they are helping, which the operator types from their own knowledge of that person** (`--email`); a y/n question or a value offered for copying does not replace it. The tool refuses unless the **typed request code equals the one it computes from `rPub`**, which stops a server that swapped `rPub`, before it reads the copy or asks for the recovery seed. It then opens the copy, which names its account's address inside the account's own signature, and refuses one that names another address (`email_mismatch`, saying whose copy it is) or that is of the old form and names none (`no_address`). For an old-form copy the tool says the truth: this copy cannot be checked against the person, so the operator cannot recover this account with it; only if the person can still sign in on some device can they seal a new copy there, by turning operator recovery off and on again (rule of 2026-11-16).
   - The answer is a 0x09 statement, **signed by the recovery-key signing key** (§2.3) and sealed with `crypto_box_seal` to `rPub`. It names the address, and carries a fresh random **commit value** whose hash the tool prints. The tool's last line is a token that names the request, its key and the commit hash beside the sealed answer.
   - The exact arguments, the checks and their order, the statement's template and the token are spec 04 §7 step 3; this section does not restate them.
   - The operator uploads the token with `fulfil` (†), from the console or with `andvari-cli operator fulfil`. Both read it with one shared reader, which refuses an answer for another request before the master password is asked. The server refuses before the hold has passed, after a veto, and for an upload whose request key is not the row's or whose sealed part is not the one size for the request's address; it stores the commit hash with the answer. Until the commit the operator may upload again, and the newer answer replaces the older (spec 03 §18.6).
4. **The app picks it up.** It polls `POST /recovery/requests/{id}/poll {pollSecret}`, which keeps the secret out of URLs and access logs (T2-Rec).
   - **Before fulfilment (FI-R9)** the poll answers `{state, holdEndsAt?}`, with `state` one of `unverified`, `holding`, `awaiting_operator`, `vetoed` and `expired`. A wrong `pollSecret` is `404`.
   - Once fulfilled it answers `{state: "ready", sealed, encryptedIdentitySeed, identityPub, commitHash}`. The app opens the seal with `rSec`, then checks the answer as spec 04 §7 step 4 lays down, in its order. Among those checks: the 0x09 statement is of the current version; the **signer maps to the recovery public key that the pinned card's `recoveryFp` names**; `instanceId`, `userId`, `requestId` and `requestKey` match; the answer names **the address the person typed when asking, on a fresh install too** (`request_mismatch`); and the identity seed opens under the UVK to a key equal to the served `identityPub` (R27, §16).
   - The signer check binds only a server that does not hold the key the pin names. On a fresh install pinned by typed address (the usual device for this flow) the pin is whatever card chain the server served, so a hostile server can serve a chain whose recovery key it holds and answer the request itself with a minted account that claims the person's address. Spec 04 §7, "What these checks do not cover", says what such a server gains and what stops it.
   - The poll's `ready` answer also carries the stored commit hash, and the device compares it with the hash of the commit value it opened before it asks the person for anything. A difference means that the answer on the server is not the one this device opened, and the device says so in those words ("the operator must upload again") and keeps the request (rule of 2026-11-16). The shared code does this: once the answer has opened, it compares the stored hash in constant time with the hash of the opened commit value; a difference, or a value that is not a hash, drops what was opened and stops at `commit_mismatch` with the request kept, and a `403 commit_mismatch` at the commit does the same. A poll answer without the field is read as before.
   - **Then the person checks the account, before any new password is set.** The app shows the delivered account ID and asks the person to compare it with their own, naming the kit first and saying why an email is the weaker source: this server sent the emails (rule of 2026-11-16). Continuing is an explicit choice (the app interface takes it in two steps: the first shows the screen again with a line asking the person to confirm the comparison, the second goes on). The app interface offers the line that names the kit, and both apps show it in both steps, in place of the older sentence that names only an email, so the kit comes first on both.
5. **The person sets a new password.** `POST /recovery/requests/{id}/commit {pollSecret, commit, newAuthKey, newKdfSalt, newKdfParams, newWrappedUvk, newMemberRecovery: {recoveryWrappedUvk, recoveryAuthKey}, device: {platform, name, deviceSecret}}`, where `commit` is the commit value from the answer the device opened: the server accepts the reset only with a value whose hash is the one stored with the newest upload (`403 commit_mismatch`), so only a device that opened the answer can commit. A wrong value never cancels the request: it is audited, and the route keeps its ordinary rate limit (rule of 2026-11-16, which replaces the earlier limit of five wrong values; the server on the integration branch does this). The value is 32 random bytes, so a limit of attempts protects nothing, and it would let a damaged upload, or anyone who saw a poll secret, destroy a request that took seven days. In the apps `commit_mismatch` has a result of its own and is never worded as "start again". The exact checks and their order are spec 03 §18.5. The server applies it like the self-recovery commit, revokes every session, clears `requireLink` **and two-step sign-in** (sending `two_step_off`, N12), deletes the request, and emails "your master password was reset with the operator's help".
   - **It replaces the kit in the same transaction** (FS-B1, as §10 does for kit recovery), so a lost kit stops working at once, and it answers with a session for the new device plus the new `recoveryPieceId` (S3-C6). The app shows the new kit through the capture gate before the vault opens.
   - **On a device that already keeps the account** (rules of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7, as for a kit recovery): the person has lost the password, so no kept wrap can be opened or compared, and both the kept wrap and a pending one are replaced. The rule does not give way on what is sealed under the account's key itself: the recovered key must open the kept copy's sealed identity seed to the kept identity key. The check is made at the pick-up, before the person is asked anything, and again before the commit is sent. Another key stops as "the server's keys are not this account's as this device knows it": no commit is sent, no session exists, nothing kept changes, and the request stays as it was. A kept copy whose seed does not open always refuses; the way out is to remove the copy first, which says how many changes were not sent (§4.4), and the screen says so. A session the device refuses after the commit was answered is ended alone, by its own refresh token. The new wrap's cost starts at the client's default and is raised to the kept copy's cost and to the server's policy (§10). The shared code does this. **An answer that names another account than the one this device keeps for the request's address is refused at the pick-up** (rule of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7), before the person is asked anything and before the device asks whether it would take the account: no account is shown to check, no password is asked, no commit is sent, the request stays, and the refusal says, with a reason of its own, that the answer names an account that is not the one this device keeps. Whoever holds the operator's recovery key can make such an answer. A refusal reaches the screen as what it is, never as "another account is kept" and never as a general failure. **Kept (2026-11-17):** the shared code refuses at the pick-up an answer that names another account than the kept one, and the app interface tells each refusal as what it is. Both apps draw the request's refusals at the pick-up on the request's screen in place of its stage, and under a damaged copy offer its removal, with its count first (2026-11-17, evening).
   - **On a device that keeps ANOTHER account**, an account at another server, or its server's operator account (rules of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7): the recovery stops before anything is committed: the device asks at the pick-up, before the account is shown to check (the request is kept, and a later poll goes on once the other account is signed out), and again just before the commit; not when the request is opened, since a request may be answered after the other account was signed out. The stop is said as what it is: "this device keeps another account: sign it out first" (the sign-out says how many changes were not sent, §4.4), the identity fault for an address this device knows as another account, or, on a device that holds its server's operator account, that it must be removed from this device first, also when the recovery is of that very account. Where what the device keeps changed while the commit was under way and the session is refused after it, the new kit is handed over with the refusal and reaches the screen: it is shown with the refusal and a line that the old kit no longer works and that the account opens with the new password once the other account is signed out, and kept until the person has typed it back or a lock wipes it, which is then said (§10, "A kit on show after a late refusal"). `andvari-cli` stops already at the request, on a home that keeps another account at that server (spec 01 §7). The shared code, the app interface and `andvari-cli` keep the stops (2026-11-17). Both apps draw a stop at the pick-up and the result of a session refused after a commit as itself (2026-11-17, evening). Both apps draw the kit's line after a refused commit of a recovery with the operator's help too (the phone moves the person to the kit and draws, above it on every step, the kit's line that it was not kept, the result's own words and how long the kit holds; the desktop draws the kit's line, its holds line and then the answer that brought it), Android since 2026-11-18 and the desktop since 2026-11-19, as §10 says (established by reading the screens' code). `andvari-cli`, after a commit whose session it does not keep, ends exactly that session, keeps the new kit (written or shown) and says that the recovery went through and why nothing is signed in (2026-11-18).

**Limits.** Per IP for creating requests; per address for the requests opened and the emails sent (5 per window of 24 hours that starts at the first one counted); per request for verify attempts (5, step 2), and per caller address for the verify route (step 2). Every accepted request stores a row, a decoy when the address is unknown or its account is private, so that poll, verify and commit answer alike for all three and nobody learns which accounts use operator recovery; and every accepted request writes one audit record of one shape, naming the account only for a real request (spec 03 §18.1). Anyone can open a request in a victim's name, but against an honest server it does nothing without the code from the victim's inbox, the 7-day hold, the person's own confirmation to the operator, and no veto. Repeated requests are a nuisance bounded by the email cap, recorded as a residual (R37); the mailbox value (step 1) keeps them from also keeping the address's holder from asking, and lets at most five plus five request mails per window reach an address (up to twenty inside 24 hours across the windows' edges, more than ten only through values). Someone who reads the person's mailbox can spend both caps and so close that way out; that is part of what reading a mailbox means (R37).

**What these checks do not cover (FS-R5).** The hold, the veto and the emailed code bind only an honest server. A server that is itself the attacker can fake all three. What the operator's typed address and the person's device then stop, and what remains, is listed in spec 04 §7, "What these checks do not cover", and is not restated here (R27, §7.7 "What remains under K").

### 9.4 Audit and notices

**New audit types**, ids only and never emails:
- `operator_disable`, `operator_enable`, `operator_delete`, `operator_device_revoke`, `operator_quota`, `operator_policy`;
- `operator_escrow_read`, `operator_recovery_fulfil`, `instance_card_update`;
- `signup_start` (meta: the prefix of the address's keyed hash), `signup_verify_fail`;
- `login_wait`, `reauth_wait`, `totp_verify_wait`, `account_delete_stale` (2026-11-18; spec 03 §8): a refusal by an address's or a device's wait, or an account deletion refused over a moved log, leaves one record per address, device or account per period (fifteen minutes for the waits, an hour for the deletion), never one per refused request. Until the consoles have words for a kind, they show its name, as for every new kind;
- `link_create`, `link_answer`, `link_revoke`;
- `device_link_create`, `device_link_claim`, `device_link_reveal`, `device_link_approve`, `device_link_pickup`, `device_link_pickup_denied`;
- `recovery_rotation_stored`;
- `recovery_request_create`, `recovery_request_verify`, `recovery_request_verify_fail` (spec 03 §18.2), `recovery_request_veto`, `recovery_request_commit`;
- `vault_log_append` (meta: the kind);
- `email_sent` (meta: the template name);
- `account_recovery_change`, `account_device_revoke`.

Retention and pseudonymisation stay as in spec 03 §13. Section 11 lists the operator actions that email the person.

**What the log shows when a sign-in ends a session, and what it cannot** (rules of 2026-11-17; spec 03 §2). A device has one live session, and a sign-in on a known device ends its earlier ones (a browser's row excepted, §7.8). That end is recorded as the end of a session by a sign-in, and that is all the server can know: a chain someone else had been refreshing with a stolen token ends there quietly, where before the person's own next request would have run into the reuse rule and left its record. The reuse rule is unchanged for every token of a live session. Where the sign-in names the session it replaces by a token the server had already replaced, the record of that end says so, which is the sign of a lost answer or of someone else having used the token (`session_end` with meta `sign_in_named_replaced`; `sign_in_named` for a live one; `sign_in` otherwise). A refresh whose answer was lost and that is tried again within sixty seconds is no reuse and leaves no reuse record; it is recorded as a refresh answered within the grace (`refresh_grace`: the account, the device and the caller's address, nothing else), so that the operator and the person can see that a replaced token was presented again and answered (spec 03 §2). Inside that minute the record is also the only sign that someone else who held the replaced token was answered with the device's live pair. A refusal by a limit leaves one record per period, not one per request: when an address or a device runs into its wait at a sign-in, and when an account deletion is refused over a moved log. **Kept (2026-11-18):** the server ends a known device's earlier sessions at a sign-in and records `sign_in`, writes `sign_in_named` and `sign_in_named_replaced` where a sign-in names the session it replaces, and records each answer of the grace as `refresh_grace`. **Not kept yet (2026-11-18):** neither the operator's console nor the web app's list of a person's own audit lines has words for `refresh_grace` (a marked placeholder is to stand there until its words are approved), nor for the four records of a refusal by a limit, which the consoles show by their names. Since 2026-11-18 the browser names the session its sign-in replaces, so its sign-ins write `sign_in_named` or `sign_in_named_replaced` too, and the server records the refusals by a limit as `login_wait`, `reauth_wait`, `totp_verify_wait` and `account_delete_stale` (above). **What a reader of the log learns of an address** (2026-11-18; spec 05 T1): every record of a typed address other than an account's own is the address's keyed hash, so rows of one address still tie together, and someone who reads the log, the journal or a copy of the database and guesses an address confirms nothing without the server's secret; whoever can read the server's environment file can.

### 9.5 `andvari setup` and `andvari admin`

**`andvari setup`** is a subcommand of the server image. It supersedes `bringup.sh` and runs interactively or with flags:

1. **Origin and TLS.** Validates the origin against §2.2: an https address with a domain name. Plain-http LAN setups are no longer offered (R4); phones and browsers refuse them anyway.
   - The address needs a **publicly trusted certificate** (N10). The supported paths, each documented in `docs/self-hosting.md`:
     - the bundled caddy with HTTP-01, for a server reachable from the internet on 80 and 443;
     - the bundled caddy with a **DNS-01** token (for example Cloudflare's), for a LAN-only server with a public domain;
     - a tunnel (Cloudflare Tunnel);
     - `tailscale serve`, which gives an https `*.ts.net` name; only devices on the tailnet can reach it;
     - your own reverse proxy with a valid certificate.
   - Behind your own front, the trusted-proxy CIDR check runs.
2. **Kind and mode.** Public or private (`ANDVARI_INSTANCE_KIND`). `open` (needs mail), `links` or `closed`. Then the cap, quotas, age confirmation, and on a private instance who may create register links.
3. **Mail.** Graph or SMTP, with a test send.
4. **Operator recovery for this instance: yes or no.** It is offered only when mail is configured, because assisted recovery needs the emailed code (N9). Without mail, `recoveryFp = none`. Under variant R it is not offered with `--kind public` (§7.7).
   - Yes: the existing key ceremony (`recovery-cli keygen` with no network and no log driver, the sheet, the canary, "saved") pins `ANDVARI_RECOVERY_PUBKEY` and `ANDVARI_RECOVERY_FINGERPRINT`.
   - No: `recoveryFp = none`.
5. **Secrets.** Generates `ANDVARI_ENUM_SECRET` and `ANDVARI_SIGNUP_SECRET`, writes `andvari.env` and refuses to overwrite one. The instance id and the setup name are written to the database, not to the environment file.
6. **Start and check.** Starts the stack and waits for `/healthz` and the policy smoke check.
7. **Mint the setup link.** Generates `s` and writes the `links` row directly into the database: kind `setup`, the token hash, the setup offer envelope, 24-hour expiry. Prints the link and a terminal QR:
   > Open this in the andvari **desktop app** on the computer that will hold this server's **operator** account, or run `andvari-cli operator enrol <link>` on any computer. Not in an app you use for your everyday account: an app holds one account per server. Whoever uses it first becomes the operator. It works once and expires in 24 hours.
   - **The phone apps do not take setup links**, because operator views are desktop and CLI only (§9.1). A phone that opens one shows "This link sets up a server's operator account. Open it in the andvari desktop app, or with `andvari-cli operator enrol`." and does nothing else.
   - Someone who wants the desktop app for their everyday account keeps the operator account in `andvari-cli`.
   - A family with only phones keeps the operator account in `andvari-cli` on the server host or any computer. On the host it is online, so the host can sign card updates: moves, renames, and even a new `recoveryFp` without a rotation statement. Existing opt-ins, and new ones whose card 0 is anchored before that change, see it only behind the takeover warning (§4.5, FS-R2, FC-2). It can never sign a rotation statement, which needs the offline seed. This is recorded under R30.
8. **Wait.** Waits for card 0, then prints "Instance ready: *name*. Operator *email*. Operator key xxxx xxxx … (compare with the app if you like)."
   - **Only then does it mint the host enrol link** for the person's own everyday account (FI-B9): an enrol offer names `cardHash`, and every registration but setup waits for card 0 (§7.3). It is bound to the email they give, and printed with a second QR: "Scan this with your everyday phone to create your own account" (N9).
   - A family member is then invited from that everyday account (§6.6). The operator account is used only for operator work.
   - A setup interrupted after registration resumes from the operator's app (§6.7 **Resume**).
   - `andvari setup --status` shows where it stopped.

`--reset-setup` mints a new setup link, and only while no operator account exists (FI-B9).

**`andvari setup --test`** (B4) is the non-interactive form used by every test script. It sets:
- origin `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`, `ANDVARI_TEST_MODE=1`, a mail sink directory and a test clock file (§7.1);
- mode `open`, the given cap, and no operator recovery (or `--test-recovery`, which generates a throwaway recovery key and writes its seed to a file for drills).
