By default `--test` finishes setup headlessly (FI-B9): it enrols a test operator through the setup link with `andvari-cli operator enrol`, which signs card 0, and only then mints the enrol links. `--test --stop-at-setup` stops after minting the setup link, for tests of setup itself.

It prints one JSON object on stdout: `{origin, instanceId, setupLink, card0Hash?, operatorFile?, enrolLinks: […], mailSink, clockFile}`, with `--enrol <n>` controlling how many enrol links. `operatorFile` holds the test operator's credentials; it and `card0Hash` are absent with `--stop-at-setup`, and `enrolLinks` is then empty. The server refuses test mode on any non-loopback origin (§7.1).

**`andvari admin`**, on the host, with access to the database and environment. These are server-trusted by nature: the host is T1.

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `status` | Instance state. |
| `accounts`, `disable`, `enable` | As the panel. |
| `signup open\|links\|close\|cap <n>` | Edits `andvari.env`, clears the database's tighter overrides for what it sets, and restarts (§7.1, R7, N8). The only way to raise the cap or loosen the mode. |
| `quota --account <email> [--bytes N] [--items N] [--vaults N]` | Per-account override, for example before a restore (§14.1). |
| `enrol-link [--email E] [--expires D] [--count N]` | Mints host enrol links (§6.4, B4). Prints each as a link and, with `--qr`, a terminal QR. Used by tests, demo accounts and seeding. Refused until card 0 exists (FI-B9). |
| `setup-link` | A new setup link (only while no operator account exists, FI-B9). |
| `operator` | Prints which account is the operator. |
| `reset-instance-identity` | §4.6. In one transaction it demotes the current operator to member, deletes the stored cards and rotations (FC-4), and mints a setup link for the new chain (N2). Asks for typed confirmation of the instance name, and states that every client and every store build must re-pin. |
| `audit` | The audit log. |
| `restored` | Run once on a database put back from a backup, before the server starts (§9.6). |
| `backup [--keep N]` | The snapshot of the database, taken only while a running server holds it (decided on 2026-11-22): written under a temporary name in `backup/` in the data folder, checked, then named `andvari-nightly.db` and by its date; the newest 14 dated snapshots stay (`--keep N`, 1 to 999). The server's start reads the file key (device and inode) of the database file before it opens the database. After its database is open and caught up and before it listens, it takes a lock on `server.lock` in the data folder in two parts, both exclusive, through one opening of that file, and keeps both until its process ends: the first on the file's first byte; then it writes one line into the file through that opening (a value drawn at random at each start and the database file's file key, read again); then the second on the file's second byte. Where it cannot have a part within about two seconds, or cannot write the line, or the system gives the database file no file key, or the file at the database's path is not the one that was there before it opened its database, it lets go of the lock, prints one warning line and starts without it; a database in memory only takes no lock. The command's first step looks at that lock and line, before anything reads or opens the database and before the backup folder is made: where the file is missing, nobody holds either part, a part of the lock is held and the second part is not, the line names no database file or names another file than the one at the command's own path, it stops with one line and has changed nothing; a file it cannot look at stops it too. It opens the database in the driver's read-only open mode, so the system refuses any write to the database file through it (SQLite may still make the `-shm` file, and an empty `-wal`, beside it). Once the snapshot is made and checked, it looks again: the second part must be held with the same line, or the snapshot is removed, no name is given, and the run ends with one line. After the first look and before it opens the database, it refuses a database file whose header does not say write-ahead mode (one plain line), a database whose marker makes the server's start refuse it, and a rollback journal beside it (the line says to move the journal out of the data folder and keep it). It adds no row and no table to the database and leaves its journal mode as it is, prints none of the warnings the server's start prints about its settings, and ends with status 0 and no output or status 1 and one line. |

**Demo accounts (R11).** The App Review demo is a set of ordinary member accounts on the public instance. A systemd timer **on the instance host** runs `andvari admin enrol-link --email <demo address>` and `andvari-cli enrol / seed / check`. The tool reaches the server by the instance's public name (see the next point): either through the public address, or through a hosts entry on that host that points the name at the TLS front-end on its loopback address.
- **The tool connects by the origin's own address (R11, changed 2026-11-15).** `--origin` is the instance's identity: card checks, ADs, links and the name every request carries. The tool does **not** dial a different address under that name. The owner declined, on 2026-11-15, the extra HTTP library that naming the origin on a connection to another address needs, and without it the request would tell the server the wrong name. `--connect` is therefore accepted only when it is the origin's own address (same scheme, host and port); any other value is refused before anything is sent, with a sentence that says why. A job on the instance host reaches the server by its public name, or through a hosts entry that points that name at a TLS front-end on the loopback address.

- No email codes are needed, and no operator session ever leaves the host.
- Demo accounts count against the cap.
- The timer recreates an account a reviewer deleted.

### 9.6 A server restored from a backup

The operator's steps are in `docs/self-hosting.md`, "Before you restore a backup"; this section says what the product does. The threat model's row is spec 05 R47.

**What a restore brings back.** The database as the snapshot held it, and with it everything the server had changed or ended after the snapshot as it was before: saves made after the snapshot are gone from the server and are left only on the devices that hold them (the server had acknowledged them, so no device keeps them to send again); a master password changed after it is undone, and the former one signs in; devices revoked after it are known to the server again; accounts disabled or deleted after it are back, and a member removed from a shared vault after it is a member again and can read it; a recovery kit or a two-step setup replaced after it is undone; mail waiting at the snapshot is sent again; invites, links, device links and requests for the operator's help ended after it are in progress again (a request the account refused is waiting again, one the operator answered is ready again). The server's change counter goes back with the database. Shown over a real server by tests since 2026-11-20: a device that synced after the snapshot refuses the older state with its notice, applies nothing and keeps everything it holds; a session renewed since the snapshot ends as unknown at its next contact, with no other session ended; the former master password is in force; a deleted item is brought back from the trash with or without a restore; a shared vault whose members changed after the snapshot is read-only on a device that saw the change and freezes there once another member changes the members or leaves (spec 05 R45), and that device leaves that state only by forgetting the server and signing in again, which also drops what only that device held; and forgetting the server and signing in again gives a device exactly what the server holds.

**`andvari admin restored`** (kept since 2026-11-21). Run once on the put-back database, before the server starts. In one exclusive transaction it:
- ends every session of every account, the operator's included, so every access token and refresh token a device holds is refused from then on and every member signs in again;
- marks every device that is not revoked as signed out, keeping it in its account's devices list;
- closes the invites and links in progress (open or answered) and removes the device links in progress;
- closes every request for the operator's help in progress, for every account: one past its mailed code, waiting or already answered, is ended as refused with its answer dropped, so that nobody can finish it; one before its code is cancelled; a request that names no account is left as it was.

It removes no data, changes no password, key or setting, leaves the change counter where the snapshot left it, writes nothing a device must believe and tells the devices nothing; it writes nothing to the audit log. It prints what it did and keeps one record of it (the time and the counts) in the server's own settings, served by no route and shown by `andvari admin status`. A second run signs everyone out again. **It refuses, and changes nothing:** where there is no database at the path; before it opens anything, where a write-ahead file, its companion or a rollback journal lies beside the database, with one way out that is right whatever left the file (move the leftover files out of the folder and keep them, copy the backup's database file into place again, never moving it, and run the command again; an operator who no longer has the backup's file moves nothing and does not start the server, and where a journal was found, opening the database once takes a stopped run of the command back); while the server or any other process has the database open on the same host; and on a database of another build. A run stopped part-way leaves a rollback journal, so the next run refuses as above. A run that finds the database unreadable says so and says to copy the backup's file into place again; "nothing was changed" is said only where it is so. **A limit:** on a network volume whose locks do not reach across hosts, only the server's write-ahead file beside the database shows that the server has it open. **Not kept yet (2026-11-21):** a put-back database whose own record of its build cannot be read is said as a database of another build (that nothing was changed is true), without the way out of copying the backup's file in again; the message for a missing database says to put the backup's file back where the others say to copy it; and the server's other commands have no guard against a leftover file beside the database (a write-ahead file of the replaced database beside the put-back file is applied to it at their first opening, so the put-back file is damaged for good, the command then ends with an error, and the leftover files are deleted, leaving only the backup's file as the way back; a rollback journal of another database is rolled into the file without a word), so the sentence that sends an operator without the backup's file to open the database once is right for a journal of the operator's own stopped run only; and where the database's path is a symbolic link, the command looks for the leftover files beside the link, while SQLite uses the files beside the file the link leads to, so a leftover write-ahead file there gets past it and damages the put-back file for good in the same way.

**The guard of the server's other commands, decided and not merged yet (rules of 2026-11-21).** A write-ahead file beside a database whose own header does not say write-ahead mode belongs to another database (the server's database is always in write-ahead mode, and a put-back snapshot is in rollback mode), so it is refused before anything opens the database: by the server's start and by every command of the tool, with the files named and the same way out as the `restored` command gives. A database in write-ahead mode with its own write-ahead file (an unclean stop of the server) opens as before. Where the path is a symbolic link, the guard and the `restored` command both work on the file the link leads to, for the header they read and for every leftover name they build, and the refusal names the link and its file. `andvari setup` in a data folder that holds leftover files and no database is refused before anything is created, with a sentence of its own: which files lie there, that setup makes a new instance only in a folder without them, that they may be moved out and kept and setup run again, and that nothing was created or changed. A database path that is a folder, or a file this user may not read, is said in one plain line with exit 1. **Its limits, as decided:** a link whose target does not exist yet still gets past the guard (a command then creates a new database there, and SQLite deletes a leftover write-ahead file beside it; no database is harmed, and the file it removes is one the tool's words elsewhere say to keep; to be mended before the first public release); a hard link is another name for the same file, and its leftover files lie beside the other name; a link swapped between the guard's look and the opening; a put-back file that is itself in write-ahead mode (a file-level copy of a live database, which the guide says not to restore) cannot be told from the server's own unclean stop, so only the `restored` command, which refuses any leftover file, refuses a write-ahead file beside it; and nothing ties a rollback journal to one database file (its header holds no name and no identifier of the database), so no command can tell a journal of another database from the database's own, and the guard does not look at journals; only the `restored` command refuses a journal beside the database. Today the server's opening of a database sets write-ahead mode before it reads the database's record of its build (`Db.kt`), so every refusal of the opening (among them "from andvari 0.x", "created by a different andvari build", and a settings table that cannot be read) leaves a put-back file switched to write-ahead mode; its header then says write-ahead mode, so a foreign write-ahead file put beside it later would get past the guard, as if it were the server's own after an unclean stop. With the guard, a leftover write-ahead file is refused before that in the usual order of events; an opening that decides without switching the mode is to be built before the first public release (decided on 2026-11-21).

**What the command cannot end.** A device revoked after the snapshot is known to the server again: after the command it can no longer sync or renew its session with what it holds, but with the account's master password it signs in as a known device, with no link where the account requires one for new devices and no new-device mail (accepted on 2026-11-20: the server keeps what makes a device known so as to lock nobody out). Neither the server nor the operator can tell which devices those were, and the revocation is no longer in the audit log; the operator revokes them again at once, before members are told to sign in. What the snapshot undid (an account disabled, a member removed, a kit or two-step setup replaced, a password changed) is the operator's and the members' to do again.

**What a requester's device says when the command closed its request** (rules of 2026-11-20): the request is over, and the device offers to ask again. The three lines a requester's device can show then (a request ended from the account's side; a request cancelled because the account's master password or recovery setting changed or the account was recovered; an answered request that ran out of time or was used) are each to say also "or it was ended by whoever runs the server", naming no restore, and the first to add: if that was not you, tell whoever runs the server. **Kept in the shared code since 2026-11-21** (`AppCopy.kt`, `EntryApi.kt`), as three lines of its catalogue, none naming a restore: a request the server reports as ended from the account's side (`OperatorHelpStage.VETOED`) carries `ASSIST_REQUEST_ENDED` in place of its stage's line (the request was ended from the account's side, by one of its signed-in devices or because the account's master password or recovery setting changed, or by whoever runs the server; if that was not you, tell whoever runs the server; you can ask again); a request cancelled before its mailed code answers `REQUEST_CANCELLED` with `ASSIST_REQUEST_CANCELLED` (the approved words, with "or it was ended by whoever runs the server" added after "after it was made"); and after the operator's answer was opened, a commit that can no longer be finished is a result of its own, `ReplacePasswordResult.REQUEST_ENDED` with `ASSIST_ANSWERED_REQUEST_ENDED` (it ran out of time or was used, or it was ended by whoever runs the server; nothing was changed; start again with a new request), while a recovery with the kit keeps "too late". Each line awaits the owner's words (the second only for its added clause). **Not kept yet (2026-11-21):** neither app draws the first line yet: the phone draws its own narrower line for an ended request, and the desktop a general line for the request's stage.

**Decided, not built: the change counter's jump, and what each device does about what the server lost** (decided on 2026-11-20 that both are built before the release, and on 2026-11-21 as below; **nothing of it is built**, and the two parts merge together: the jump is never on its own in a build). The rule of 2026-11-19 stands beside it: devices are never sent a signal by which they are to accept the older state, and nothing here is something a device must believe.
- **The two harms.** Say the backup was at change number 4 and a device B had synced to 7; after the restore the server is at 4 again and hands out 5, 6 and 7 a second time. **The silent skip:** once the server has passed 7 again, B syncs and never receives what others saved under 5, 6 and 7 after the restore, and nothing says so. **What only a device still holds:** items, and newer versions of items, saved between the snapshot and the failure, which the server no longer has, and nothing says so. The jump closes the first and alone makes the second worse (the older state's warning goes, and a full pull the server demands replaces the device's rows); the devices' part closes the second.
- **The server's part.** `andvari admin restored` raises the change counter, in the same transaction, by a margin worked out to the millisecond from the time since the snapshot's newest change, at 10^9 for every minute plus one minute (so the smallest jump is 10^9, and the same backup restored twice within a minute still gives two numbers). Every jump is kept as a pair (from, to) in the server's own settings, so the pairs a database holds are those of its own history. The housekeeping's retention fence follows one formula: where the fence it computes is above `from`, the fence stored is at or below `to`, and no change is numbered above `to` and below the computed fence, the fence is `from`; otherwise it is the computed one (the row that carries the jump may age out like any other). The command refuses, changing nothing, a new counter above 2^52, a host clock before the snapshot's newest change, and a database with no change at all. No field is sent to devices.
- **Noticing.** At a pull a device knows its own cursor c and reads the server's number R. Where c is above zero and R − c is at least 5 × 10^8, the counter jumped since the device last synced, whether the server answered with the difference or demanded a full pull. The device records durably that the step below is owed before it does anything else with the answer, and applies nothing of that answer first (in the web app the record is what keeps other tabs from skipping the step).
- **The step**, for every vault the device holds, whatever the log engine says of it. It fetches the server's full list and sorts what it held before this answer against it: **lost items** (held as taken by the server, not waiting in its own queue, and in the list neither live nor deleted); **lost newer versions** (an item both hold at different versions, where the item's history at the server does not hold the device's version and the two contents differ; an item brought back from the trash is not one; where the server pruned its history the step errs toward keeping a copy too many); **vaults the list does not hold** (made after the snapshot), with every item in them; and **items the list holds that the device does not**, which it takes where it has no trace of them, and does not take back by itself where its own trash shows it saw them deleted. It keeps and marks the first three across the replacement of its rows, and applies the rest as it does at a full pull today. A stop between noticing and the step, or inside it, is finished at the next start.
- **Keeping and marking.** Every such item stays on the device marked "only on this device"; a lost newer version of an item the server still has stays as a copy beside it on this device only, never sent until its person sends it. A marked item is never replaced or dropped without a word by a later answer: when the server later serves a row for it, the device takes the row and drops the mark where the contents are the same, and otherwise keeps its own version as a marked copy, not sent. A mark travels with its item into the holding area when its vault goes. The web app's cache spares marked items when it is cleared, and a tab reads the marks from the cache under the cache's own lock before it runs the step.
- **One notice and one list.** The server no longer has N items (and M newer versions) that this device holds. For each item the list says which case it is (no longer on the server; a newer version than the server's; in a vault the server no longer has; back on the server after you deleted it) and when this device last saw it change. The choices: see them; send them back, all or one by one; copy them into another vault; remove them from this device (asked a second time); not now, which is the resting state: the notice and the marks stay and nothing is lost by waiting. Items the device saw deleted that are live again on the server are listed as "back on the server", each with "keep it deleted" (the device sends the deletion again) or "take it back". The notice is raised again only when the list of marked items has changed.
- **Send back and copy.** Sending back (to a vault the device may write, that the server still has and the log engine accepts) saves the item through the ordinary queue, after a comparison with what the server now serves that drops the mark instead where they are the same (so two devices sending the same item make no copies). Files do not come back: a device keeps no files, the item goes back without the files the server lacks, and the notice names each. A vault the device may only read, a withheld or frozen shared vault and a vault the server no longer has take no send-back: the person copies the item into a vault they can write, as a new item, without the files that cannot be read.
- **One change beside it:** when the server says a save was applied, the engine writes the taken version into its store at once, so that a save taken in the last moments before the failure is held on the device.
- **Where there is nothing to compare.** A device under a policy that forbids a copy, a browser without an offline copy and the browser extension hold nothing across the forced sign-in: whatever they showed that the server no longer has is gone from them, and they offer nothing. Logins a phone has caught and keeps sealed were never taken by the server and are untouched.
- **The apps.** One notice and one list in each app that keeps a copy (the phone, the desktop, the web app with its offline copy, the iPhone app), drawn from the shared code's view; the extension shows nothing of it. Each new line is a placeholder until its words are approved.
- **What a server that lies can do with it.** Raise its counter and leave items out: the device marks them and offers; it gains nothing it does not have today and loses one thing (today it can make a device drop items without a word by demanding a full pull; a device that sees a jump keeps them). It can jump at every answer: the step runs each time and nothing is overwritten. It can make a device ask for one item's history per item at every pull, which costs load only. A device that had not yet synced a real deletion made elsewhere lists that item as no longer on the server and its person can send it back, most plainly a device back after a long time, which lists what was deleted elsewhere while it was away; the notice's wording says so. A person who can write to a vault could always add again an item they still hold: no new power.
- **Accepted:** a server busy enough to reach 5 × 10^8 changes by ordinary use (about 500 days) makes a device away that long list what was deleted meanwhile, as above; an item moved to another vault after the snapshot comes back as two (the source "back on the server", the target "no longer on the server"); two runs of the command less than about half a minute apart can leave a device that synced between them unaware of the second jump (it skips nothing, but it does not list what that half minute lost). Once the jump exists, any member's device can work out from its counter that a restore happened and roughly how old the backup was; it is not a signal to accept anything.

---

## 10. The recovery kit

- **The secret is unchanged:** the 32-byte `recoverySecret` (spec 01 §2.1), never typed into the KDF.
- **Display:** base64url, 43 characters, in **11 groups** (ten of 4, one of 3), separated by spaces. Parsers ignore spaces and line breaks only. A dash is **not** ignored, because base64url uses `-` (FI-B10; `MemberRecovery.kt` already strips only white space).
- **Confirmation:** three distinct group indices chosen uniformly by CSPRNG from 1..11 ("Type groups 2, 7 and 10"), compared exactly and case-sensitively once space, CR and LF anywhere in each typed group are ignored. After 3 failures the kit is shown again.
  - There is **no Copy button** on the reveal or confirm screens.
  - The binding to `pieceId` and the vault-entry capture gate stay as they are. Only the type-back changes, from the whole secret to the three groups.
- **The kit document** is generated on the device and never uploaded. It is offered as Print, Save to Files or Save PDF (whatever the platform offers without leaving a temporary copy behind). Contents:
  - the title "andvari recovery kit";
  - "Keep this private. Anyone with this kit and your email address can reset your master password and open your vault.";
  - the email, the instance name and origin, the date;
  - the grouped secret, large and monospace;
  - the kit QR;
  - "Your account ID: <32-hex short identity fingerprint>", for an operator-assisted recovery's identity anchor;
  - "To recover: open andvari → Sign in → Forgot your master password? → Scan your recovery kit".
- **Kit QR:** `andvari-kit:2:<O>:<C>:<E>:<S>`, where:
  - `O` is the unpadded base64url of the origin;
  - `C` is the instance's `card0Hash` as 64 lowercase hex;
  - `E` is the unpadded base64url of `emailNorm`;
  - `S` is the unpadded base64url of the 32-byte secret.

  It is deliberately not a URL, so a system camera does not offer to open it in a browser or a search.
- **`card0Hash` in the kit** lets a fresh device recovering with the kit pin the instance from card 0, so a self-hosted instance is never trusted on first use at recovery time.
- Parsing is total and canonical, exactly as for links (§6.1), except for the length: the kit QR text is at most **1024** characters, not 512, because it carries a whole email address and an origin. A typed kit secret ignores spaces, CR and LF only; a tab is not ignored.
- **Where a kit is accepted (FS-B1).** Only on **Sign in → Forgot your master password? → Scan your recovery kit**, by scanning or typing it there.
  - "Scan an invite" and its paste field refuse kit payloads with "This is a recovery kit, not an invite.", and start nothing. The message gives no directions, so it does not walk someone who was handed a kit as an "invite" to the recovery screen (FC-9).
  - Before anything is sent, the recovery screen names the account and the server: "Recover *email* on *name* (*origin*)", with the email from the kit, and says that this sets a new master password for that existing account.
  - Why: an attacker could otherwise sign up, keep that account's kit and show its QR as an invite. A victim who "recovered" it would live in an account whose UVK, identity seed and still-valid kit the attacker holds, and a kit pin on the attacker's own server would also have passed later sign-ins without a Trust Gate.
- **Recovery with the kit:** pin the card chain from `card0Hash` (§4.4; this pin counts only for the kit's own account, §4.4's password rule), then the two-phase self-recovery (spec 03 §12), then a new master password.
  - **The commit replaces the kit (FS-B1).** `POST /recovery/self/commit` gains a required `newMemberRecovery: {recoveryWrappedUvk, recoveryAuthKey}`, made from a fresh recovery secret, and `device: {platform, name, deviceSecret}` (§8.7). In the same transaction the server replaces the member-recovery row (a new `pieceId`, `recoveryConfirmed = 0`), so the scanned kit stops working at once, and it answers with a session for the new device plus the new `recoveryPieceId` (S3-C6). The app shows the new kit through the capture gate before the vault opens.
  - So an attacker who handed over a kit cannot use it again later. Against an honest server that locks them out: the commit also revokes every session. On the attacker's own server they still hold the UVK, and the screen naming that server is the guard.
  - The commit also **clears two-step sign-in** and sends `two_step_off`. Someone who lost the phone holding their authenticator would otherwise be locked out after recovering (N12). The person can turn it on again afterwards.
  - **On a device that already keeps the account** (rules of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7, as for an assisted recovery): the password is lost, so no kept wrap can be opened or compared, and both the kept wrap and a pending one are replaced; but the key the kit opens must open the kept copy's sealed identity seed to the kept identity key. The check is made right after the kit opens the key, before the person is asked anything, and again before the commit is sent. Another key stops as "the server's keys are not this account's as this device knows it": no commit is sent, no session exists, nothing kept changes. A kept copy whose seed does not open always refuses; the way out is to remove the copy first, which says how many changes were not sent (§4.4), and the screen says so. A session the device refuses after the commit was answered is ended alone, by its own refresh token. The shared code does this. A refusal reaches the screen as what it is, never as "another account is kept" and never as a general failure; a kit whose identity does not match at its check is an identity fault (rule of 2026-11-17). **Kept (2026-11-17):** the app interface tells each of these refusals as what it is, at the kit's check and at the commit; both apps draw each of them as itself, where the person lands, a stop that sends the person back to the kit's entry included, and offer the removal of this device's copy beside "this device's copy is damaged" (2026-11-17, evening; the way out beside a stop is the shared code's, below).
  - **On a device that keeps ANOTHER account**, an account at another server, or its server's operator account (rules of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7): the kit recovery stops before anything is committed: at its start, by the server alone, before anything is sent; after the kit's check is answered, before a new password is asked; and again just before the commit. The stop is said as what it is: "this device keeps another account: sign it out first" (the sign-out says how many changes were not sent, §4.4), the identity fault for an address this device knows as another account, or, on a device that holds its server's operator account, that it must be removed from this device first, also when the recovery is of that very account. Where what the device keeps changed while the commit was under way and the session is refused after it, the new kit is handed over with the refusal and reaches the screen: it is shown with the refusal and a line that the old kit no longer works and that the account opens with the new password once the other account is signed out, and kept until the person has typed it back or a lock wipes it, which is then said (§10, "A kit on show after a late refusal"). The same holds for a registration's kit (§7.3). The shared code, the app interface and `andvari-cli` keep the stops (2026-11-17). **Kept since:** both apps draw the kit after a refused commit with its results and its line (Android since 2026-11-18, the desktop since 2026-11-19); `andvari-cli` writes the kit to the file given, or shows it, or prints it rather than lose it, says that the change went through and why nothing is signed in, and ends exactly the refused session at the server (2026-11-19).
  - **The cost of the new wrap** (rule of 2026-11-17, spec 01 §7): a recovering device cannot know the account's own cost (no wrap opens, and the first exchange's answer is the server's word). The new wrap starts at the client's default, is raised to the kept copy's own cost where the device keeps the account, and to the server's policy, read through the pinned server just before the commit; each raise only inside the client's limits, and a policy that cannot be read raises nothing. The shared code does this for both recoveries. The policy the server serves is never below its floor, and each raise is made figure by figure, so a policy higher on one figure than the kept cost raises that one and keeps the other (rules of 2026-11-17, §9.2). The server serves its policy raised to its floor since 2026-11-17. **A known limit** (accepted on 2026-11-17; spec 05 T1): on a device that keeps no copy of the account, the new wrap is made at the cost the server serves, which a hostile server can hold at the floor; the floor is the least any client takes. (Closed on 2026-11-18: on a device that keeps the account the shared code raises each figure by itself, to the highest of the default, the kept wrap's, the pending wrap's and the served cost.)

- **A kit on show after a late refusal** (rules of 2026-11-17; spec 01 §7). A kit that a recovery's commit or a registration made, and that reaches the screen with a refusal that came after the commit, holds only until the next sign-in to the account on any device, which shows a new kit and replaces it; the line beside it says so. A lock, by hand or by the idle limit, wipes it on every road, also while another account's session is established, because a secret on the screen of a device nobody may be watching must not outlive a lock; in a flow with no account open the apps keep the browser's limit on a kit screen (five minutes without a touch or a key, a warning thirty seconds before, the pointer's movement not counting) and lock at its end. The screen the person comes back to says that the kit is gone, that the change stands (the new master password is in force, or the account was made) and that the next sign-in shows a new kit; where this device itself cannot take the account now (another account kept, a fault of the account's identity, the server's operator account held here) it does not say "on any device", and the refusal's own line says what stands in the way. That notice goes only when the person dismisses it: not with the opening of an account, a quick unlock included, and not with a start of the app. The same is said after the app was closed with such a kit on show, from a mark at rest that holds the road, the address and the name of the refusal's line, never anything of the kit, and that stays until the notice was dismissed; at a restart the notice says what stood when the mark was written. An ordinary kit on show with its account's keys open is covered by the app's own lock at the policy's time (the next unlock makes a fresh one); the five minutes are for a kit with no account's keys open. The refusal of some other session during the call, and the end, sign-out or removal of another account kept on the device, are not a lock: the kit stays on show. **Kept (2026-11-18):** the app interface wipes such a kit at every lock and keeps the notice and its mark as above, keeps the kit through the refusal of another session during the call, says a commit that happened and could not be opened as that, gives the kit the line that says how long it holds, and hands the apps the idle limit and its warning line for such a kit screen. The browser's kit screens show the idle limit's warning before the limit acts, and the line that stays after it took a kit off the screen says that the kit went and that the next sign-in asks for a new one. Since 2026-11-18 the Android app draws such a kit with its results and ways out, wipes it at every lock and says so until the notice is dismissed; since 2026-11-19 the desktop app does the same, with the line beside the kit, the line that says how long it holds, and the limit and warning the shared code hands it.
- **Every kit on an app's screen** (rules of 2026-11-18, for both apps as for the browser; spec 01 §7). A kit is a secret on a screen nobody may be watching, so:
  - **Its limit and its warning come from the shared code, for every kit**, not only for one shown after a refusal: the lock time of the policy the device keeps for that server, five minutes where it keeps none, and never more than five minutes for a kit shown with a refusal (no account is open behind it). Every kit has a warning thirty seconds before; where the limit is not longer than that, the warning is shown from the start of the screen, so that a person is always told that a touch keeps the kit. The warning is announced to a screen reader. On the desktop the warning also asks the operating system for attention on the window and sounds the system's alert once, which reaches a person who is in the system's print dialog, where it cannot be drawn; where the window has the focus, the warning line takes the accessibility focus.
  - **The lock clock runs everywhere a kit is in the journey:** while the person is in the system's print screen or has left the app from there, after an unlock from the autofill window, and for as long as the app is visible, with the focus or without (beside another app in split screen, under another app's dialog); it counts the time the phone slept, so a lock that fell due during sleep is made before a frame of the kit is drawn. The time in the system's print dialog counts toward the kit's limit (accepted).
  - **A kit that comes on show while the app is away is locked at once**, never drawn at the return; the notice or the next unlock says what happened. A kit made while the app was away and wiped unseen is not said as "the kit that was on the screen", and no line says "save this kit now" where no kit is left.
  - **While a kit is shown the phone's screen stays on**, up to the kit's own limit (decided on 2026-11-18), so that the kit's limit and its warning are what end it, never the screen's own timeout without a word.
  - **An unlock that cannot show a kit makes none:** the autofill window opens the vault for filling and leaves the kit to the app's next opening, which locks first where a kit is due.
  - **A kit that replaces one shown before says so** on its first step: a kit shown before and not confirmed no longer works and must not be kept. It is not said where the device cannot know (a kit shown on another device, or before this rule).
  - **What the person types back cannot be copied out:** the fields offer no Copy and no Cut, by menu, by key or through an accessibility service; every lock clears what the app put on the clipboard, and only that.
  - **A lock reaches what a kit left behind:** what the app holds of a kit for printing goes; a system print dialog still open can no longer print it (the job is cancelled, or its page source answers that there is no page), and a print that a lock stopped is said at the return; the kit's document, its QR picture and the groups typed back are held by the screen's model, which every lock and every cover empties, whether or not anything is redrawn; a kit left for the system's print screen is covered, as on every other road away, and is shown again at the person's tap.
  - **Under a notice that names what stands in the way here, the screen offers that way out**, reachable without first trying a sign-in, with its usual count and confirmations, and only while what the notice named still stands: one cause, one way out (this device's own copy of the account: its removal; another account kept here: its sign-out; the server's operator account held here: its removal; a fault of the account's identity: none). The notice itself carries no button but Close. "The server ended this session" is said only where the server did.
  - **Kept (2026-11-18):** the Android app locks a kit that comes on show while it is away before it is drawn, keeps the lock clock on the print road and the autofill road and while it is visible without the focus (an open vault visible without the focus now locks at its idle time too), keeps the screen on while a kit is shown and announces the warning, offers no Copy or Cut from the menu or a keyboard in the fields a kit is typed into, drops at a lock what it held for printing, offers the way out under a notice, says work overtaken by the person's own sign-out as that and never for an end the server made later, and keeps its windows out of screenshots; an ordinary kit on show there is taken by the app's own lock at the policy's time. **Kept (2026-11-19):** the shared code hands every kit its limit and its warning (a policy may give as little as one second; the warning's line names no figure), keeps the record that a kit was shown as a record of its server, gives a kit that replaces one shown before its line, has the unlock for filling that makes no kit (its opening ends with every end of the session), and hands one way out per cause with a reason line of its own. The Android app's tests hold every kit, the ordinary one too, to its limit (five minutes where no policy is kept for its server) and its warning, and the app locks at the kit's limit; a lock empties the model that holds what a kit's screens draw, also while the app is away; a kit left for the system's print screen is covered, with a clock that counts the time the phone slept; an accessibility service cannot copy or cut what is typed into a kit's fields; the way out under a gone kit's notice is the shared code's. The desktop app takes every kit's limit and warning from the kit itself, a kit made in Settings over an open vault too (a moving pointer does not count as use while a kit is on show); a warning on show from the start stays through a touch and is not signalled again at every touch; a kit that replaces one shown before says so first, in the error tone; after a lock nothing of a kit can be printed, a print that a lock stopped is said in one line at the return, the fields offer no Copy and no Cut, and every lock clears what the app put on the clipboard; when the warning first shows, the app asks the operating system for attention on its window and sounds the system's alert, once per warning and never for the ordinary lock, and where the window has the focus the warning line takes it; the way out beside a stop, under a notice, on a device link that ended and on the request for the operator's help is the shared code's. That Windows flashes the taskbar button, sounds the alert, or that a screen reader speaks the focused line has not yet been seen on a real Windows computer. **Kept since 2026-11-19 besides:** the app interface says a kit made while the app was away and wiped unseen as never shown, once the app tells it that it left the screen without a lock; a notice that a kit is gone names the kept session it means, and a way out under it is offered only while the kept session's mark equals the one the notice names; a kit limit of thirty seconds or less gets a short warning line of its own. The Android app makes no kit from an unlock in the autofill window (it uses the shared code's unlock for filling) and locks first where a kit is due when its own window comes on show, so the next unlock shows the fresh kit; a kit made in Settings runs from its own limit; a kit that replaces one shown before says so first; and the way out beside a stop, under a notice, on a device link that ended and on the request for the operator's help is the shared code's. The desktop's first screen after a kit that came with a refusal was typed back says the shared code's reason line, with the kept account named where its sign-out is the way out, and a recovery committed beside a kept account says the shared code's sentence. **Kept since 2026-11-19 in both apps:** each tells the shared code when nothing of it is on show without a lock (Android: the background, the print screen, the autofill window closing; the desktop: its window minimised or hidden, not another application in front while the window stays on the screen), and at the return a lock that fell due comes first, so a kit wiped unseen is said as never shown; a confirmation is closed at a lock; the warning for a short lock time is drawn from the kit's filled line; under a gone kit's notice a way out is offered only while the kept session's mark equals the one the notice names, and on the desktop the removal of this computer's own damaged copy is offered there again. A kit shown together with a refusal comes about only through a race that the shared code's own tests can bring about; no app's test can stage it yet, so on the apps' screens that state is exercised only through the shared code's tests (2026-11-19).

---

## 11. Emails

### 11.1 Transport

- **Microsoft Graph, app-only** (`POST /users/{sender}/sendMail`, the existing `GraphEmailSender`), with `ANDVARI_GRAPH_TENANT`, `ANDVARI_GRAPH_CLIENT_ID`, `ANDVARI_GRAPH_CLIENT_SECRET` and `ANDVARI_GRAPH_SENDER`. SMTP (`ANDVARI_SMTP_*`) stays for self-hosters.
- **Operator setup: one shared mailbox, `support@andvaripass.com`, both sends the automatic mail and receives replies and support.** A separate no-reply address is not technically needed.
  - The app registration gets **`Mail.Send` only, with no read permission**, restricted to that mailbox by an Exchange Online application access scope. The server can send as `support@` but cannot read what people write back.
  - Mail goes out with display name "andvari".
  - Bounces and auto-replies land in the same mailbox and are filed by an inbox rule.
  - **Retention** (owner, 2026-11-16): sent mail is deleted after 90 days and all other mail after one year, by an Exchange retention policy on that mailbox only; what that means in practice is in §0.6.
  - DNS for `andvaripass.com` publishes SPF, DKIM and DMARC.
- An instance in `open` mode refuses to start without a transport. Elsewhere, without a transport, notices are logged as not sent and the security actions still happen.
- **Test mode** (`ANDVARI_TEST_MODE=1`, §7.1) may use `ANDVARI_MAIL_SINK` instead: every message is written as a JSON file and nothing is sent (B4). The sink is never available outside test mode.

### 11.2 Content rules (normative)

- **Never:**
  - passwords, recovery data, link secrets, tokens or session ids;
  - vault, item, attachment or contact names;
  - links carrying a token, a fragment or any parameter that acts or signs anyone in.
- **Links** go only to static help pages, `https://andvaripass.com/help/<topic>/`, where `<topic>` is one of `lost-password`, `recovery-kit`, `add-device`, `connect`, `compromised` and `delete-account` (IN-2).
- **Codes.** The only code ever sent is the 6-digit sign-up or recovery-request code; beside the recovery-request code goes its mailbox value, which opens nothing but one more request for the same address (§9.3 step 1; rule of 2026-11-17). The code appears in the body, never the subject, with its expiry and "Nothing happens without this code. If you didn't ask for it, ignore this email." In the recovery-request code mail, which carries the mailbox value, no sentence that tells the reader to ignore the mail stands above the value's paragraph (rule of 2026-11-17; the server keeps it since 2026-11-17: with a mailbox value the code's lines end with "Nothing happens without this code." and the paragraph and the value follow, with no ignore sentence). A queued code mail holds its code, and its mailbox value, in clear until it is sent or its code expires.
- **Every notice states:**
  - the instance name and origin;
  - what happened;
  - when, in UTC;
  - where relevant, the device's platform and its own name, rendered as plain text;
  - the **account ID** (the 32-hex identity fingerprint, short form). It is public, but repeated in every notice it gives the person an anchor older than any later compromise, for an assisted recovery (R27). **No mail sent because of an assisted recovery carries it** (rule of 2026-11-16; the templates are listed in spec 03 §14.5): this server sends them, so for that request only a notice older than it counts as an anchor (§9.3 step 2);
  - what to do: "If this wasn't you, open andvari on a device you trust → Settings → Your devices, and change your master password", or "use your recovery kit" when no device is left.
- **No location from the IP.**
- **Format:** plain text with an HTML alternative of the same content. Every dynamic string is escaped. No images, no remote content, no tracking, no unsubscribe on security notices. The sender is the support mailbox itself, so a reply reaches support with no `Reply-To` needed.

### 11.3 Templates

| Template | Subject | Body essentials |
|---|---|---|
| `signup_code` | Your andvari sign-up code | code, 15-minute expiry, instance, the ignore line |
| `signup_existing` | Someone tried to sign up with your address | "You already have an account on *instance*. If that was you, sign in instead. Nothing changed." |
| `new_device` | A new device was added to your andvari account | platform and name, time, how (linked from *device* or signed in with your master password), the next step |
| `password_changed` | Your andvari master password was changed | time, device, the next step |
| `two_step_off` | Two-step sign-in was turned off | time, device, the next step |
| `recovery_changed` | Your recovery settings changed | the old and new posture, "a new recovery kit was made" where it applies, the next step |
| `device_removed` | A device was removed from your account | which device, by you (from *device*) or by the operator |
| `operator_escrow_read` | The operator downloaded your recovery copy | time; "This is the first step of restoring your account. If you didn't ask for help, contact support@andvaripass.com and consider turning operator recovery off." |
| `recovery_request_code` | Your andvari account recovery code | code, expiry, "the operator can only help after you enter this code"; the mailbox value, under a short paragraph that says what the person needs, with no line above it that says to ignore the mail (§9.3 step 1; its place is kept since 2026-11-17, and its words are still a marked placeholder on 2026-11-19) |
| `recovery_request_open` | Someone asked the operator to reset your account | the time, the platform, "it waits 7 days; any of your signed-in devices can cancel it; if this wasn't you, cancel it and change your email password" |
| `recovery_request_cancelled` | The request to reset your account was cancelled | by which device, and when |
| `recovery_request_private` | About your andvari recovery request | "This account has no operator recovery. Use your recovery kit." |
| `recovery_applied` | Your master password was reset | by the kit, or with the operator's help; every device was signed out |
| `account_disabled`, `account_enabled` | Your andvari account was disabled / re-enabled | time and reason category (abuse, request, other); support@andvaripass.com |
| `account_deleted` | Your andvari account was deleted | the email, instance and time; "Its vaults and everything stored only in them are gone." Queued in the outbox inside the deletion transaction, to the address captured in the request. The outbox row is deleted once sent and kept at most 24 hours (§11.4, R31). |
| `account_deleted_by_operator` | Your andvari account was deleted by the operator | as above, plus "It had been disabled since *date*." and the support address (R14) |
| `require_link_cleared` | New-device protection was turned off | "You recovered your account, so andvari turned off 'New devices must be linked'. Turn it on again in Settings if you want it." (R14) |

### 11.4 Sending rules

**A durable outbox (R8).** Every message is first written to `mail_outbox` **inside the transaction that caused it**. Columns: id, template, recipient, rendered subject and text, createdAt, attempts, nextAttemptAt, lastErrorClass.

- **Sending.** A sender worker retries with backoff (1 min, 5 min, 30 min, 2 h, 12 h) and gives up after 24 hours. Giving up is audited as `email_failed`, with the template name only.
- **Retention.** Rows are deleted the moment they are sent. A code message is deleted unsent when its code expires.
- **Failure logging.** Failures are logged by class only: an HTTP status, never a body.
- **Permanent failures.** A message that fails with a permanent error (an HTTP 4xx other than 429, or an SMTP 5xx) is dropped at once and audited `email_failed`. One bad address cannot hold the queue (N7).
- **Stalled.** The outbox counts as stalled when it is non-empty **and nothing has been sent successfully for 10 minutes** (N7). While stalled:
  - `signup/start` and `recovery/requests` answer `503 mail_unavailable`, uniformly (§7.2);
  - the operator panel shows a banner;
  - `/metrics` exposes `andvari_mail_outbox_oldest_seconds` and `andvari_mail_failed_total`, for an alert.

  Security notices stay queued and go out when mail recovers.
- **Per-recipient caps:** three separate counters, each counting in a window of 24 hours that starts at the first mail it counts: 5 codes; 5 messages of the kind an unauthenticated request can cause (the "you already have an account" and "someone asked for recovery" notes); and 20 security notices, which the other two never use up, so nobody can silence a person's security mail by asking for recovery in their name. Anything over is dropped and audited. The code mails of recovery requests taken past the address's cap with a mailbox value have a fourth budget of 5 per window (§9.3 step 1; rule of 2026-11-17), so that the mail cap does not drop them.
- **What the app says.** Outside a stall, sign-up and recovery-request answers stay uniform (`202`), and the app says "If no email arrives in a few minutes, check spam or try again."

---

## 12. First run, per client

### 12.1 Phone apps (iOS, Android) and desktop

The first screen shows three buttons of equal size and style. None is preselected or focused.

1. **Scan an invite**, whose screen also offers **Paste a link** (in-app browsers often break universal links). On desktop the button is **Paste an invite link**.
2. **Create an account on `andvari`**, with the line "Free · best effort · US only · 13+" (decided on 21 November 2026: the line says what a person may expect, and no longer who runs the server or where). When `signup.full` is set, this button reads "Sign-up is full right now" and is disabled.
3. **Use your own server**: type an address (§4.4, typed-address root). The desktop app also takes the server's setup link here (§9.5); the phone apps do not take setup links.

Below them, as text: **Already have an account? Sign in**, which offers:
- **Link from your other device**: scan the QR it shows, or paste the link on desktop. **This is the only place a device link is accepted** (§8.2 step 0, T2-B1).
- **Email and master password**: the server choice first (the public instance, or your own behind the Trust Gate), then sign in. The password screen names the instance and its origin (§4.4).
- **Forgot your master password?**: **Scan your recovery kit** (§10), the only place a kit is accepted (FS-B1); or, on an instance with operator recovery, "Ask the operator for help" (§9.3).

**Invite links:**
- A tapped invite or connect link (`/l`) opens the app on its join screen.
  - iOS uses universal links (`applinks:andvaripass.com`); the association file lists `/l` only.
  - Android uses verified app links: an `autoVerify` intent filter for `https://andvaripass.com/l` only.
  - **`/d` is not an app link** (T2-B1). A tapped device link opens the scriptless `/d` page (§6.1).
- `assetlinks.json` on `andvaripass.com` names the **new Android application id** (§0.3) and its one signing certificate, the same for the Play and the direct build (SHA-256 `3C:49:4F:27:57:7F:C3:5D:EA:0F:2A:D7:AF:32:B1:D4:76:BB:70:A2:4F:B7:F3:04:6A:B5:0F:A6:A0:FC:F7:F1`, decided on 2026-11-16). The Apple association file names the new iOS bundle id (§0.3).
- **Three scanners, three jobs** (FS-B1).
  - The scanner and paste field on "Scan an invite" accept `/l` only. A `/d` payload there shows "To add this device to your account, open **Sign in → Link from your other device** and scan the code there." A kit payload shows only "This is a recovery kit, not an invite.", with no directions (§10, FC-9). Neither starts anything.
  - The scanner and paste field on "Link from your other device" accept only `/d`.
  - The scanner and typing field on "Forgot your master password? → Scan your recovery kit" accept only `andvari-kit:` (§10).
- Android gains an in-app QR scanner (CameraX, with the camera permission asked on first use). iOS already has VisionKit.

### 12.2 Web and extensions

- **Web** (served by one instance): **Create an account here** (when the mode allows it) and **Sign in**, with "Joining with an invite or connecting with someone? Use the andvari app." There is no server choice; the web client binds no identities (§13). Its offline copy (spec 02 §8.1) serves only a tab that is already open when the server cannot be reached: the page itself comes from the server, so a reload or a fresh tab without the server cannot load it. There is no offline app shell in this release; adding one is a design decision of its own, for the reasons spec 02 §8.1 lists (a cached shell is older server-served code that the update, pin and consent rules must still govern).
- **Chrome and Firefox extension**, store builds: no default server. The first popup offers "`andvari`" or "Your own server" (behind the Trust Gate), then sign in. Host permission covers `https://vault.andvaripass.com`, and other servers are optional permissions.
- **Safari extension:** unchanged; it uses the app's server and Face ID, and inherits the app's card and log checks through the app's snapshot.

**The extension's reduced rule (B5).** The Chrome and Firefox extension cannot run the full verification. Its crypto is pure JavaScript (tweetnacl, @noble/hashes, @noble/ciphers), there is no Montgomery map, the MV3 service worker allows no WASM, and there is no build path from `extension/` to `web/src`. So it follows this rule:

1. **Keys come only from UVK wraps**: the personal wrap, owner wraps and member self-wraps that a full client minted after its own checks. This is today's Q8 rule. A shared-vault wrap's AD names the vault's genesis hash (§2.3). The extension reads that hash from the account state's `vaults[vaultId].genesisHash`, which it now opens under the UVK (XChaCha20-Poly1305 from @noble/ciphers). A wrong or withheld value simply fails the AEAD and the vault stays closed (N1). The extension opens no grant, and verifies no vault log. **It opens a shared-vault wrap only when `accepted[vaultId].decision = accept`** in the account state it already reads (FN-1). It cannot check `addSeq`, because it verifies no log, so after a re-add a replayed old wrap can still open there (R24); that wrap holds the same key as the current one. The rule covers owner wraps too: a vault the account created has an `accept` record written at creation, and a vault the account left or was removed from has `decline` (§5.4), so a replayed wrap of a vault the account has left stays closed. Because a server could serve the account state from before the leave together with the old wrap, the extension also keeps the highest in-blob `seq` it has opened and opens no shared-vault wrap while the served document is below it.
2. **The account genesis is verified.** The extension derives the Ed25519 and X25519 keys from the identity seed (tweetnacl `sign.keyPair.fromSeed` and the `crypto_box_seed_keypair` construction). It requires `signerPub` to equal its own Ed25519 key and `identityKey` its own X25519 key, checks the signature with `sign.detached.verify`, and applies §5.2's field checks.
3. **The instance card chain is verified.** Signers are compared byte for byte against `operatorSignKey` (§4.2). The first pin is the baked `card0Hash` for the public instance, or the Trust Gate's typed-address rule; both are password pins (§4.4), and the extension signs in only through one. A fork stops the extension (§4.4). A move or a `kind` change prompts, **except** that store builds never follow either for the baked public instance (§4.4, T-R7, T2-R-j): they show "This server says it changed. Update andvari."
4. **It writes only to the personal vault.** That holds for every kind of item and every write path, a card's update included: the one function that sends an item refuses any vault but the personal one. New logins are saved there. A matching login that lives in a shared vault is not updated by the extension: it says "This login is in *vault*. Update it in the andvari app."
5. **The strict statement parser** for purposes 0x04 and 0x05 is a third implementation, graded by the same vector files (§17).
6. **It keeps the account state's high-water mark** (§5.4) per origin and account in its trusted store, and opens shared vaults only under a served document at or above it. The mark follows a move with the pin, and a mark that cannot be read keeps shared vaults closed (§4.4).
7. **It refuses the operator account.** A sign-in whose session has the operator role is revoked at once and refused, as on the web (§13, §9.1). The pinned card also names the operator account: whenever the newest verified card names the signed-in account as `operator` (a handover made this member the operator), the extension and the web client sign it out with the same sentence; a phone stops using it and keeps its copy locked until the person removes it (§9.1, owner 2026-11-16). That needs no signal from the server, and it covers a quick unlock, which carries no role.
8. **It never seals or re-seals an escrow, and never changes the recovery posture** (T2-R-j). It cannot verify a 0x08 rotation statement (that needs the Montgomery map it lacks), so it cannot tell a signed rotation from a forged one. Recovery settings are changed in a full client.

**What a hostile server can and cannot do to an extension-only session (T2-R-j).**
- It **can** feed it stale or withheld data, and keep a shared vault readable after the person was removed from it, since the extension verifies no logs (R24).
- It **cannot** plant a key (keys come only from UVK wraps that open under the genesis-bound AD), move the personal vault (§5.3), or get a shared-vault write out of it (rule 4).

### 12.3 Trust Gate copy

- **The address is checked against §2.2's grammar first.** Anything else is refused before the gate opens (R4).
- **Title:** "Connect to this server?" on first use, or "Switch to a different server?" when one is set.
- **Body:** the existing baseline sentence, the card line from §4.4 and the existing cautions.
- **Device-link wording** (§8.2 step 0, T2-B1). Title "Is this your server?" Body: "*origin* — it calls itself “*name*”. This device hasn't used it before. Your other device shows the address it links to: continue only if it is exactly the same." Buttons [It's the same] [Cancel].
- **Sign-in through a link-offer pin** (§4.4): the typed-address wording, with "You're about to enter your master password for this server."
- **`kind` change** (§4.4): "*name* (*origin*) now says it is a {public | private} server. This changes who can be offered operator recovery. Continue only if you expected this."
- "Forget this server" in Settings removes the namespace's card pin and data. It is the way out of §4.4's stop state.

### 12.4 First launch after the cut-over (R9)

The first launch of a new-format build on a platform that **upgrades in place** deletes every pre-cut-over local store before anything else runs. Those platforms are desktop and the extensions. **Android installs under the new application id** (§0.3), so it starts empty, and the old Android app stays installed beside it as the fallback device (§14.1) until it is uninstalled. **iOS installs as a new app under the new bundle id** (§0.3), so it starts empty too; it cannot read or clear what the old iPhone app stored. The stores deleted:

- the offline cache;
- saved sessions and keychain or keystore items;
- device-local trust records;
- the personal-wrap cache;
- quick-unlock keys;
- the extension's IndexedDB records.

It does this once, keyed by a format marker, and then shows the first screen. New builds read none of these stores.

---

## 13. What the web client may do

The web client's code is served by the instance it talks to (T6). It may:
- sign up and sign in;
- use vaults;
- add a **current** contact to a vault it owns (§3.6, §6.9);
- leave a vault, delete or restore a vault it owns, and transfer ownership;
- change its master password and recovery posture;
- list and revoke its own devices;
- cancel (veto) a recovery request for its own account;
- recover its own account with the recovery kit, typed or pasted, from "Forgot your master password?" only (the kit was shown to this same web client at sign-up, so this exposes nothing new to its server);
- delete the account.

It must **not**:
- create, show, open or answer links of any kind;
- scan or display identity QRs;
- run or approve device links;
- sign instance cards;
- process link answers (sharing at link time happens on a native device);
- sign in as the operator account (§9.1).

**The web and server identity.** The web client pins the card chain of the origin that serves it, in the browser's storage, under the same pin-store rules as every client (§4.4): the baked root for the public instance, otherwise the Trust Gate once per browser in its typed-address wording (the person typed the address into the browser). It needs that pin for the genesis check (§5.2), and the pin lets it notice a fork or a rollback on a later visit. It cannot follow a move, because another origin serves its own web client: it reports "This server says it moved to *origin*" and keeps its pin.

Nothing secret about links reaches the web: link secrets stay on the creating native device (§6.7, T-B6). The web client still holds the account's UVK and could write account state, for example a fake contact. Native devices therefore ask before using a contact made elsewhere (§6.9 (c)).

**The line:** a web user's own vault is already exposed to its server, so nothing else they do on the web makes that worse. Identity binding is different. If the browser made or answered a link, the server would learn the link secret and could put its own key into *another* person's contacts, someone who never used the web. The web UI therefore has no code path for these actions: they are compiled out of the bundle, and a source pin test enforces that.

---

## 14. Cut-over

### 14.1 Recommendation: re-enrol and import

The owner is the only user. These steps are written for them, in this order.

**Only the owner's everyday account is carried across.** Any other account on the old server (today one test account of the owner's) is not.

1. **Drill first.** Run steps 4–8 against a scratch server and compare per-vault item and attachment counts.
2. **Drain the old devices.** On each device with an old build, unlock once while online, so queued saves reach the server. That includes the iOS App Group save queue and Android's offline queue. Then stop writing new data in the old apps.
3. **Export.** In the current app, make an encrypted `.andvari` backup with shared vaults and attachments included (spec 07 §2), and check it with `backup-cli verify`. Then note what the backup cannot carry, all named by the tools:
   - attachments over the 64 MiB total (`attachmentsOverCap`), to re-attach by hand;
   - server TOTP sign-in, which is re-enrolled;
   - item version history and trash, which are server-side and lost.
4. **New server.** At `https://vault.andvaripass.com`, run `andvari setup` against a **fresh** database. It gets a new recovery key only if operator recovery will be offered, new secrets and Graph mail.
5. **Operator account.** Open the setup link on the **operator device** (paste it into the desktop app, or `andvari-cli operator enrol <link>`), record the kit, and the app signs card 0. The operator and everyday accounts need **two devices**: the operator account on the desktop or in `andvari-cli`, the everyday account on the phone. An app holds one account per instance, so if the desktop app should hold the everyday account too, the operator account lives in `andvari-cli`. **Take `card0Hash` from the signing device** (the app's "Instance details", or `andvari-cli card show` on the device that signed), never from a fetch (T-R10). Record it for the store builds.
6. **Everyday account.** On the phone, create it through the public sign-up flow, or with the everyday enrol link `andvari setup` printed. Choose private recovery and save its kit.
7. **Import.**
   - **First** raise that account's limits: `andvari admin quota --account <everyday> --bytes … --items … --vaults …`, sized from the backup's preview. The defaults (100 MiB, 5,000 items) could refuse the restore.
   - Then run `andvari-cli restore --backup <file> --account <everyday>`. It implements spec 07 §3: preview, fresh ids, attachments re-encrypted, batches of 200, resumable. It **paces itself on `429` with `Retry-After`**, because shared-vault creation is limited to 10 an hour (`vault_create`, §3.7).
   - Shared vaults are recreated as vaults the everyday account owns. Verify the counts against the preview.
8. **Devices.** Install the new builds on the other devices. Their first launch wipes the old local state (§12.4). Link them from the first device (§8).
   - **Android arrives under its new application id** (§0.3), as a separate app beside the old one. The old Android app is the natural fallback device (step 9); uninstall it at step 10.
   - The interim 0.30.0 kept the old id in its direct build so the owner's phone updated in place; its Google Play build already carries the new id (§0.3).
   - **The signing key for the new id** (decided on 2026-11-16, §0.6): a new key, the same for **every** channel (Play, the direct APK, devstore and GitHub), so a phone can move between channels without reinstalling; the existing release key signs the old id only (§0.3).
9. **Rollback**, until step 10.
   - The old server keeps running **unchanged and unused**: no read-only mode exists, and none is built.
   - **One device keeps the old app installed** until the new instance has been used for a week. If the new instance fails, that device and the old server are the fallback.
   - New builds cannot talk to the old server (clean break). An old TestFlight build expires after 90 days, longer than the fallback window.
   - **Rolling back loses whatever was written on the new instance**, unless it is exported first: there is no path from new to old.
10. **Retire the old server** after 30 days.
    - Stop it.
    - Keep its encrypted database backup for 90 days, then destroy it.
    - `andvari.monahanhosting.com` then serves only a redirect page to `https://andvaripass.com`.
    - Destroy the old household recovery sheet once the last old backup has aged out.

### 14.2 Why not migrate in place

An in-place migration would keep item ids, history, trash and the usage ledger. It would also need:
- one-time code in the new server **and in every client**: genesis claims for existing vaults, converting device trust records and personal-wrap history, and moving the owner from admin to member;
- the old escrow posture carried into an instance that defaults to private;
- the origin changed anyway.

That code would be reviewed, shipped in store builds and run once. Review history shows migration paths are where defects hide. The losses of re-enrolling (version history, trash, the ledger, ids) are small for one user and are named in advance.

### 14.3 Server schema

- The new server creates a **fresh baseline schema** on an empty database.
- It **refuses to start** on a database created by a pre-cut-over build, with "This database is from andvari 0.x; restore its export into a new account instead (see the cut-over guide)."
- No forward migration from the old schema ships.

### 14.4 Families and people moving between instances (R13)

Accounts do not move between instances (§0.5). Moving means a new account on the other instance and an import. The owner's intent is that a family can always leave the public instance for its own server.

1. **Stand up the family's own server.** One member runs `andvari setup --kind private`. They open the setup link where the operator account will live (the desktop app or `andvari-cli`), and scan the **everyday enrol link** setup also prints with their own phone (§9.5, N9).
2. **Invite the family.** That member sends each family member a register link from their **everyday** account. Each new member is pinned to them in the same step (§6.7), and can invite others the same way.
3. **Personal vaults.** Each person exports a backup from the public instance and restores **only their personal vault** into their new account (`andvari-cli restore --vaults personal`, or the app's restore once it ships).
4. **Shared vaults.** These are restored **only by their owner**, who then adds the family again. If members also restored a shared vault, its items would be duplicated in their personal vaults. The restore preview says so, and skips shared vaults unless the person owns them.
5. **Delete the old accounts.** Each person deletes their public-instance account afterwards, and their slots there free up.

Going the other way (own server to the public instance) is the same, with sign-up by email instead of register links.
