## 16. Threat-model deltas (spec 05)

### 16.1 Rows that change

| Row | Change |
|---|---|
| **T1** | Unchanged in power. On an instance someone runs for the public, that person is T1. |
| **T6** | Adds §13: no identity binding in the web client. |
| **T10** | The escrow anchor is the pinned card's `recoveryFp` (baked, link-offered, or set up by the operator), not a typed sheet. A typed-address pin (R22) is the weak case. |
| **R3** | Applies only to operator-posture accounts, which chose it after the §7.7 warning. For them the operator can do more than decrypt: the escrow yields the identity seed, so the operator can **act as the member**, sign as them and read every shared vault they belong to (T-R6). On the public instance under variant R, R3 does not apply at all. |
| **R7** | Unchanged: no VK rotation. Stated more fully (T-R6): a removed member who kept the key, working with the server, can **read and write** later items, because items carry no author signature. The remove dialog says so and offers "Move everything to a new vault" (§3.13). |

### 16.2 New rows

| Row | Text |
|---|---|
| **T12 Link channel** | Whoever reads a link or QR first can answer or claim it first.<br>**Controls:**<ul><li>single use: the first answer or claim wins, and the real recipient then sees "already used";</li><li>the creator fingerprint `F` in the fragment (an interceptor cannot swap in its own offer);</li><li>creator-side expiry (T-R3);</li><li>the committed comparison code (§6.11), compared by typing, in person or on a call, and required before any vault is shared through a link (T-B2);</li><li>for device links, the committed code typed on the existing device plus the master password before any session (§8.2);</li><li>for device links, the new device acts only on a link opened from its own "Link from your other device" screen, and pins the link's origin before it claims, so the password goes only to an origin it pinned (§8.2 step 0, T2-B1).</li></ul>**Residual:** a remote connect link sent over a compromised channel, whose codes the two people never compare, pins the wrong person. Such a contact is `compared = false`: it cannot receive vaults automatically, and the UI says it is unconfirmed. |
| **T13 Sign-up abuse** | Mass accounts, code floods, cap exhaustion. Controlled by §7.5. **Residual:** an attacker can fill the remaining cap slots, which the operator answers by closing sign-up. |
| **T14 Mail** | The mailbox holds only sign-up and recovery-request codes and notices, never secrets or acting links. A compromised sending tenant can phish as the product (DMARC plus "we never send links that sign you in" on the website). A recipient's mailbox can complete a sign-up for that address, which is the address owner's own risk. |
| **R20 Withholding** | T1 can delay or hide new log entries, card updates, link answers and account-state tombstones. Devices detect it only against a head or checkpoint they hold, and otherwise see stale but genuine state. Every case fails safe: nothing unsigned is accepted. |
| **R21 Equivocation** | T1 can show different members different genuine prefixes of a log, or race two genuine entries at one position. A device that sees both freezes the vault; a device that sees only one cannot tell. **Partly detected now:** the account's own vault records (§3.6 (e)), and item-carried log heads (§3.9). Since FS-R4 an item head only makes a device fetch and, if nothing new comes, note it in the vault's details; it never freezes the vault on its own, so a server that keeps serving one consistent branch is no longer caught by items alone. No cross-member gossip beyond items exists. A withheld later `add` makes the shown member list **understate** the audience (T-R6). |
| **R22 Trust on first use** | A hand-typed self-hosted origin pins whatever card it serves first. So does the link's origin on a new device that links to a private instance (§8.2 step 0), and a link-offer origin that someone signs in to (§4.4). Each passes the Trust Gate, which names the origin; for a device link the existing device shows its address beside the QR, and the bundle must agree afterwards. Invites (for creating accounts) and setup links avoid the gate. **Residual:** a person who confirms the gate for an address that is not their server's gives that server their `authKey` (T2-B1). |
| **R23 Password-only devices** | The one-tap contacts list (§8.5) trusts the account-state copy the server serves. The server alone can hide contacts or restore a removed one, never add one. A holder of the UVK can add one (R35), and the copy says so. Linking avoids it. |
| **R24 Web client and extension** | A web user's own vault remains T6-exposed. Identity binding is unavailable there by construction. The Chrome and Firefox extension verifies the card and the genesis but not vault logs (§12.2). It relies on UVK wraps that full clients minted after their own checks, and never writes to a shared vault. So a log anomaly that only a full client would catch does not stop the extension from **reading** that vault until a full client acts on it. A hostile web bundle can also write contacts into account state; native devices ask before using any contact made elsewhere (§6.9). |
| **R25 Connection metadata** | Who connected with whom, and when, is visible to the server (links rows). Vault membership topology already was. |
| **R26 Cap denial** | Covered by T13. |
| **R27 Assisted recovery** (operator posture; private instances, and the public instance under variant K) | The request needs all of these:<ul><li>the emailed code;</li><li>7 days without a veto from any of the account's signed-in devices;</li><li>the person reading a request code to the operator over a channel the operator trusts;</li><li>a payload signed by the offline recovery key.</li></ul>**Residuals:**<ul><li>An attacker who holds the victim's inbox **and** passes the operator's identity check, while the victim has no signed-in device to veto, gets the account. The copy names this.</li><li>Since 2026-11-16 the delivered account is tied to the address by the protocol: the recovery copy names its account's address inside the account's own signature, the operator types the person's address and the tool compares, and the answer names the address and the person's device compares it with the one they typed (§9.3 steps 3 and 4). An opted-in member working with the server can no longer hand a victim the member's own account.</li><li>Without an account ID from the kit, or from a notice older than the request, the person's comparison in step 4 is void, and the operator's own knowledge of the person is the only check. A wholly server-forged identity on a fresh install is then caught only by the victim's old devices (spec 04 §7).</li><li>Repeated requests can pester a victim within the email cap. The emailed code allows 5 tries per request, and requests are capped at 5 per address per window of 24 hours, so at most 25 tries a window; the mailbox's holder, who reads the code anyway, can open up to five more requests a window (§9.3). A stranger who opens five requests for someone's address can delay that person's assisted recovery by a window at a time, until the way out through the mailbox reaches the person: the server keeps it, the shared code and `andvari-cli` send a value since 2026-11-17, and no app shows the field for it yet (R37).</li><li>**These checks bind an honest server only** (FS-R5). A server that is itself the attacker can open a request with its own key, mark it verified and its hold passed, suppress the bell and the mail, give the operator its own contact details, and pose as the person. An honest operator who believes it then seals the person's UVK to it. Only the operator's own check, through a channel they already had, stops this: natural on a family server, absent on the public instance (one reason for W1). No small fix exists. What else such a server can and cannot do is spec 04 §7, "What these checks do not cover".</li></ul> |
| **R28 Availability** | One server run best-effort, with tested off-site backups. Offline caches keep vaults readable: an app's copy whenever the app holds one, the web client's only in a tab already open when the server goes away (§12.2). "Best effort" is stated plainly on the first screen and on the website. |
| **R29 Age and region** | Enforced by terms and store distribution, not technically. |
| **R30 Operator signing key** | Whoever holds the operator's signing key (a stolen operator device, or a captured operator password with server access) can sign card updates. The limits:<ul><li>**Moves:** private-instance devices follow a move only after a one-tap prompt that shows both origins; store builds never follow a move of the public instance (§4.4, T-R7).</li><li>**Renames and handovers:** possible.</li><li>**Recovery:** it cannot redirect operator recovery **silently**. A rotation needs the offline recovery seed's signature (§4.5). A card with a new `recoveryFp` and no such signature does reach every opted-in person, as a prompt asking them to keep operator recovery with the new key. That prompt defaults to turning operator recovery off, says the change may mean the server's identity was taken over, and needs the person's master password to keep it (§4.5, T2-R-f). Anyone who keeps it has handed their escrow to the key thief. **New consents** are covered too: the first-consent walk starts at card 0 for every pin, so anyone turning operator recovery on after such a card sees the same warning, with "Only me" preselected. The exception is someone whose only pin is an enrol or Trust Gate pin made on the already-compromised server, which can serve a chain fabricated from card 0 (§4.5 first consent, FS-R2, FC-2). Under variant R the public instance has no operator recovery to redirect, and its `kind` cannot be changed in store builds (§4.4, §7.7).</li></ul>Controls: the operator account is never anyone's everyday account and never signs in on the web (§9.1). A phones-only family that keeps the operator account in `andvari-cli` on the server host accepts that the host can sign moves and renames too (N9). Recovery from a stolen operator key is §4.6. |
| **R31 Address kept for the deletion email** | The `account_deleted` message keeps the deleted address in `mail_outbox` until it is sent, at most 24 hours (§11.4). This amends spec 03 §13's "no column holds the address" and spec 05 R19. |
| **R32 The product origin** | `andvaripass.com` serves the join pages for **every** instance. Its host, CDN or DNS could serve a scripted page that reads the fragment of every link opened in a browser (when the app is not installed, or an in-app browser keeps the link). That would give the reader `s`, enough to race the real recipient. It cannot swap the creator's key (`F`, T-B6), cannot pass a compared code, and cannot get a session without the master password. Controls: a static host with no scripts and CSP `script-src 'none'`, separate from any instance; apps that handle links directly; and "Paste a link" (T-R8). |
| **R33 No account re-key** | The UVK and the identity key never rotate. Someone who captured them (malware, an operator restore under R3, a stolen unlocked device) keeps them after a master-password change. The remedy is **start fresh**: a new account, export and import, re-connect, then delete the old account. The app's "I think my account is compromised" help page says exactly that (T-R7). |
| **R34 Key changes of contacts** | A contact whose key changes (reinstall, re-registration) must be re-confirmed with a matched comparison code (§6.8 step 6). Until then the old key stays pinned and the new one is untrusted. People will see this after reinstalls; it is the price of not trusting the server's say-so. |
| **R35 Account-state writers** (T2-R-d) | Any holder of the account's UVK can write its account state: the account's own devices, a hostile web bundle while the person uses the web client (T6), and the operator of an operator-posture account who opened its escrow (R3). Such a writer can plant a contact (native devices ask before using it, §6.9), write a vault record that makes every device of the account refuse or freeze that vault (§3.6 (e), §5.4), or replay an old recovery choice (devices act on it only with their own mirror, §4.5). It can already read and change every vault the account holds, so these add denial of service and confusion, not access. A vault frozen this way is left through copy-out and "Move everything" (§3.6, §3.13). **Device links (FN-3):** because `approveMac` covers `outer` alone (§8.2), a UVK holder that also works with the server (one that captured the UVK through a web session, or an operator who opened an escrow) can pair the existing device's genuine `outer` with an `inner` of its own. It already owns the account; its one gain is that contacts it planted become current on the new device without the §6.9 prompt. |
| **R36 An old address after a move** | An instance that moved leaves its old address in the public chain. Whoever later controls that address can serve the genuine chain cut off before the move, and a device that never saw the move still trusts it: a sign-in there can be relayed, a session token sent there used, and a kit made before the move accepted there and its proof replayed at the real instance.<br>**Controls:** the operator keeps every address the chain has named (self-hosting guide); apps ask for a new kit after following a move; store builds never follow a move of the public instance.<br>**Not done, and why:** refusing kits made before a move at the server would lock out everyone who had not re-made theirs; binding the sign-in and recovery proofs to the address dialed (a signature over a server nonce and the origin, in place of a reusable key) would remove relaying altogether and is the right long-term change, but it replaces the login protocol and is out of this design's scope (§0.5). |
| **R37 A stranger can delay assisted recovery** | The per-address cap on opening recovery requests (5 per window of 24 hours that starts at the first request it counts) is what keeps a mailbox from being flooded through the instance, and it is shared by everyone who names that address. A stranger who opens five requests for someone's address uses it up, and that person's own request is then not accepted until the window ends.<br>**Controls:** it delays only assisted recovery, which already waits seven days; sign-in and the recovery kit are unaffected; the person is told by mail each time someone asks.<br>**Not done, and why:** the server cannot tell the owner from a stranger before the emailed code is entered, and lifting the cap would let anyone flood the mailbox. Its price, until the way out below reaches the person: anyone who knows a person's address can keep that person from asking the operator for help for as long as they keep it up, five requests a window; counting per caller network beside the address would add a rule and no protection.<br>**The way out through the mailbox** (decided on 2026-11-17: built on the new line before the public launch; §9.3 step 1, spec 03 §18.1): a value in every request's code mail lets the mailbox's holder open a real request past the cap. It closes the stranger's hold on that person's help; it does not help a person who cannot read their mailbox, and the person still receives up to five unwanted mails per window. Whoever reads the mailbox can spend both the address's five and the value's five and so close the way out; a queued code mail holds its code and value in clear until sent or expired; across the windows' edges up to twenty request mails can reach an address inside 24 hours, more than ten only through values; the alphabet's confusable symbols (S and 5, Z and 2, B and 8, G and 6) are a cost (spec 05 R37). Kept by the server, the shared code and `andvari-cli` (2026-11-17); no app shows the value's field yet, so in the apps a person cannot use it (§9.3 step 1 lists the rest). |
| **R38 An unbound register link tells its holder that an address is taken** | Whoever holds a register or enrol link not bound to an address can try addresses at registration and learn which are taken. Accepted: the holder was invited, the route is limited per caller address, and the address is looked up before any password hashing (spec 05 R38). |
| **R39 Neighbours share the limits per caller address** | Everyone behind one IPv4 address or IPv6 /64 (a household, an office, a VPN exit, a mobile carrier) shares the limits of the routes that need no session; the sign-in's matters most (spec 05 R39). Since 2026-11-17 the refresh is drawn from the device for a live token (spec 03 §0.1), so the address's refresh budget no longer keeps anyone with a live session from refreshing. |
| **R40 A removing device names itself** | "Removed by you, from *device name*" shows the name the removing device chose for itself: it tells that a device signed in with the password removed the other, not which of the person's devices did (spec 05 R40). |
| **R41 A replaced refresh token inside its minute** | The sixty-second grace for a lost refresh answer (§7.8) answers a replaced token with the device's own live pair while that pair is untouched, so inside that minute whoever holds the replaced token can use the pair beside the device, the released line's trade kept as it is. Each such answer draws the caller address's refresh allowance and is recorded as `refresh_grace`; the server keeps the pair for the minute only; after it, or after a restart, a repeat is a reuse (spec 05 R41). A device's own second try goes within fifty seconds, never after, so that it never produces a false theft; a clock set back inside that window can let a late try go, which then reads as a reuse (accepted). "Never left" is the transport's word, given only for a failure of the connect itself with no earlier attempt attached (a connection refused, no route to host, a timeout while connecting, a name that did not resolve), and the session keeper takes it as it is; a secure connection that fails while it is being set up, a refusing proxy and a network the platform reports as a plain socket failure count as a try that went, although nothing left, so that no token goes after the minute (accepted, 2026-11-19); a network the platform reports as a failed connect (on Android an unreachable one) is "never left". A wrong "never left" would cost one more presentation of a token the server already replaced, read as a reuse: every session of the device ends and the person signs in again; nothing is disclosed (spec 05 R41). In the browser and the extension (rules of 2026-11-19, kept since that day): a token kept after a renewal that got no answer stays until that account's own sign-out, its next sign-in, a forgetting or four newer accounts' keeps, and may meanwhile be a live credential; after a sign-out the server's chain lives until it expires or is ended by a sign-in followed by a sign-out, or by removing the browser from the devices list; a proxy's page in place of the server's refusal reads as "not confirmed"; the extension's kept token is lost at a worker's restart (spec 05 R41). |
| **R42 A signed change that reached the server, while its place is free** | A change of a vault's log that the device gave up, withdrew or called "not made" can still be appended by the server at its position for as long as nothing else took it (§3.7). The device remembers it by hash and position meanwhile and says it if it appears, with the way to undo it; the person's next change of that vault ends it (spec 05 R42). The web client remembers at most eight such changes for one vault, so a person who withdraws more than eight while nothing reaches it is not told if the oldest is made after all; a tab that still runs the code from before a deploy rewrites the records in the old form until it reloads (accepted, 2026-11-19). |
| **R43 A member's save between the read and the deletion** | A half-made vault is deleted only after a read shows it holds nothing but the move's copies (§3.13); a member's save in the second between that read and the deletion goes with the vault into its grace until the clients send the point they read, which the server refuses since 2026-11-18 when the items moved on (spec 05 R43). A copy the move put into the trash counts as the move's by its id. Any member who may rename a vault can write the entry that names a move's source into its description; the only effect is that a vault with nothing in it is deleted at its owner's next move out of the named vault, said and restorable. A device with a record of its own stopped run does not look for another device's by that entry. Against a server that hides items, nothing narrows the window (accepted, 2026-11-19). |
| **R47 A server restored from a backup** | A restore undoes everything the server did after the snapshot, and a device cannot tell a restore from a lie (§9.6). `andvari admin restored` ends every session and every request for the operator's help in progress and refuses beside a leftover file (kept since 2026-11-21); a device revoked after the snapshot signs in again with the master password as a known device until the operator revokes it again (accepted, 2026-11-20); the counter's silent skip and what only a device holds stand until the jump and the devices' part are built together (spec 05 R47). |
| **R48 What stays on a device of an account that ended** | The phone's and the desktop app's engine removes every record kept by the id of an account the device knows nowhere, at a start and at the end of a confirmed deletion, "Forget this server" and a completed forgetting (kept since 2026-11-21; not `andvari-cli`); it removes nothing while a record it decides by cannot be read, and a damaged store then keeps the records for good with no word said; a deletion stopped part-way leaves its server pinned (§3.11, spec 05 R48). |

### 16.3 Rows retired

R13 in its temporary-password form; R14(a), (d), (e), (f) and (g), as §15 deletes them (FI-C2); R15; R16; R8 (server-composed invites); and the in-person and typed-sheet parts of T10.

---

## 17. Test-vector plan

- Every new file is **hand-authored from an independent oracle**: Node with WebCrypto, @noble/ed25519, @noble/curves (x25519) and @noble/ciphers, written without andvari code. This is the house pattern since the root-cause-A vectors.
- Vectors are graded on core (all targets including iOS), the web twin, and the CLI. The **extension** grades, with its own tweetnacl implementation (§12.2), the 0x04 and 0x05 rows of `statement-v2.json`, all of `instancecard.json` except `operatorDevice` (the extension is never an operator's device: it refuses the operator account, §12.2) and all of `accountgenesis.json`, the origin rows of `encodings.json`, and the `vaults` rows of `accountstate.json` (it reads `genesisHash`). It handles no links and no kits (§12.2), so it grades none of their rows (FI-C3): that includes the `roots` rows of `instancecard.json` whose root is `kit` or `link_offer`, which its harness names as another client's rows instead of skipping them silently. It grades the baked and the typed-address roots. The **web twin of the password rule** has the purposes a browser can start (§13): `sign_in`, `register`, `reauth` and `kit_recovery`. It grades the `passwordgate.json` rows of those purposes whose pins a browser can hold (baked and Trust Gate) and whose entry a browser has (no app link and no scan).
- Every rejection row names its exact verdict.

| File | Contents |
|---|---|
| `statement-v2.json` | Valid frames for purposes 0x03–0x0A. Grammar rows for `int`, `origin` (https with a domain; `http://localhost` and `http://127.0.0.1` only; refusals of IP literals, single-label hosts, uppercase, port 443, paths and trailing dots; IDN in A-label form), `name` (the explicit code-point refusals of §2.2, length, padding, re-encode), `hex64n` and `email_b64n`. Purpose-code and literal mismatches. Reserved codes. The frame order of §2.4, including a short frame with version byte `0x03` as `unknown_format` (VR-8). `v` as an `int` (VR-4), the enumerated literals (VR-3), and the origin edges: digit-only and `0x` last labels, label and host lengths, the default ports, `xn--` labels taken as plain labels (VR-5). `email_b64n` that is not already normal (VR-7). |
| `vaultlog.json` | Chains with expected states after each entry. One rejection per row of §3.3: a non-owner add; leave by the owner; an offer to a non-member, with a wrong `toKey`, while another is pending, or too long; accept by a non-target, after expiry, or wrong `offerId`; cancel by a third party; add after delete; restore with the wrong `deleteId`. Bad `seq` and `prev`. Two entries at one position (fork). `vkCommit` rows. A `leave` with `at = 2^53 − 1` that the verifier **accepts**, after which an `offer` with `at = expiresAt = 2^53 − 1` fails `precondition`. The ceiling is the server's (`at_ahead`, §3.3); the row stops anyone adding a clock to the shared verifier (T2-R-a). Undefined signer keys (FI-B1): `accept`, and a `cancel` by the target, with no pending offer; `leave` by a non-member; all `precondition`. `accept` after expiry is `precondition`, because the clearing runs before step 5. Template rules (VR-1): a `genesis` with `seq` other than 0 or a non-zero `prev`, and any other kind at `seq` 0, are `bad_statement`; a valid `genesis` placed later in a chain is `bad_seq`. A `genesis` whose signer is not its own `ownerKey` is `not_authorized` (VR-2). Checkpoint rows: a served chain against a held head gives `ok`, `withheld` or `fork` (VR-17). |
| `sharedgrant-v2.json` (rewritten) | Grant plus log: the §3.5 check order. A non-owner signer. A key that fails the commitment. A grant whose `grantId` has no `add`. A grant after removal. |
| `instancecard.json` | Chains. A handover. A move. A recovery-key rotation. A `signerPub` that is not byte-equal to the previous `operatorSignKey`. An `operatorSignKey` that is not byte-equal to the operator's own Ed25519 key, its negation included (the operator's own devices only). Signed by the wrong key. A broken `prev`. A changed `instanceId`. Rollback and fork against a pinned card. Baked `card0Hash`. The first pin from each root, the typed-address root (a hand-typed address behind the Trust Gate, trust on first use) included, with `origin_mismatch` first of its verdicts (the newest verified card's origin is not the origin dialed). Verdicts and order per §2.4 (VR-10, VR-12): `origin_mismatch`, `instance_mismatch`, `card0_mismatch`, `card_not_in_chain`, `fork`, `withheld`; `operator_key_mismatch` only in rows where the verifier is the operator and is handed its own Ed25519 signing key (VR-11). |
| `recovery.json` | 0x08 rotation statements: signed by the outgoing recovery seed; signed by the wrong seed; a `fromFp` that is not the consented one; chains of two, as the `rotations` array of `PUT /escrow/self` (FI-R1); a chain with a `recoveryFp` change that no rotation covers, for the first-consent check walked from card 0 for every pin root, including a link-offer pin whose `cardHash` is newer than the change (FS-R2, FC-2). 0x09 assisted-recovery payloads, with §9.3's field types (`requestId` `b64_16`, `requestKey` and `uvk` `b64_32`, FI-B13): a valid signature; signed by an identity key instead of the recovery key; a `requestKey` that differs from the request; the request code from `rPub`. Verdicts per §2.4: `fp_mismatch` for a rotation, `request_mismatch` and `identity_mismatch` for an assisted-recovery payload (VR-9). |
| `wrapad.json` | The genesis-bound shared-vault wrap AD: a wrap made for genesis A refused under genesis B; the personal wrap unchanged. |
| `accountgenesis.json` | Valid. A signer that isn't the identity. A changed `userId`, `instanceId` or `personalVaultId`. Wrap-against-genesis rows. One verdict per check, in §2.4's order: `signer_not_pinned_key`, `bad_signature`, `identity_mismatch`, `user_mismatch`, `instance_mismatch`, `personal_vault_mismatch` (VR-13). |
| `links.json` | Secret → `linkId`, `offerKey`, `answerKey`, `registerToken`, and `tokenHash` (base64url SHA-256 over the ASCII token). The creator fingerprint `F` and the `creator_mismatch` rows. The committed code: `commit`, `reveal` and `bad_reveal`, the code from both nonces, and rows showing that changing any input changes the code. Setup and enrol offers. Telling an opened frame from host JSON, and `kind_mismatch` against the server-reported kind. URL compose and parse rows (white space, four parts, canonical base64, 32-byte secret, length cap, origin grammar). Offer and answer envelopes (AD binding, swapped link ids). The setup offer. Comparison codes. The creator's answer checks. Every recipient and creator verdict of §2.4 (VR-14), and a host JSON `purpose` other than the two as `unknown_format` (VR-15). |
| `devicelink.json` | Secret → `linkId`, `macKey`, and the UVK-derived bundle key. `commitN`, the reveal, and the code from `nonceN` and `nonceE`. The claim, approve and pickup MACs; the approve MAC over `outer` only, with rows where a swapped `outer` fails the MAC and an `inner` from another link fails its AD (FC-3). Comparison codes. Bundle strict-decode rows. Checkpoint verdicts. `outer.origin` different from the pinned origin (refused before any password prompt). A bundle whose `origin`, `instanceId` or `card` disagrees with the pin made in step 0 (`pin_mismatch`, sign out) (T2-B1). URL parse rows for `/d`: exactly three parts, white space, canonical base64url, a 32-byte secret, the origin grammar, the length cap (FI-B12). `proofHash` from `proof` (FI-B6). |
| `passwordgate.json` (new, T2-B1) | The decisions of §4.4's password rule and §8.2 step 0, as rows of `{purpose, entry, payload, origin, account, pins: [{origin, root, account?}], session?}` → `proceed`, `trust_gate` or `refuse_entry`. `purpose` is `sign_in`, `device_link`, `kit_recovery`, `assisted_recovery`, `register` or `reauth`; `entry` is `link_device_screen`, `kit_recovery_screen` (FS-B1), `scan_invite`, `paste_invite`, `app_link`, `cli_link_device` or `server_choice`; `payload` is `l`, `d` or `kit`; `root` is `baked`, `kit`, `setup`, `trust_gate`, `link_offer` or `enrol`, and a `kit` or `setup` pin names its `account`. Rows include: a `/d` link from `scan_invite`, `paste_invite` or `app_link` → `refuse_entry`; a `kit` payload from anywhere but `kit_recovery_screen` → `refuse_entry` (FS-B1); `kit_recovery` from `kit_recovery_screen` with a kit pin for that account → `proceed`; a kit or setup pin used to sign in to another account, or to link a device → `trust_gate` (FS-B1); `sign_in` through an `enrol` pin → `trust_gate` (FI-B8); `device_link` to the baked origin → `proceed`; `device_link` to an origin with no pin, or with only a `link_offer` pin → `trust_gate`; `sign_in` through a `link_offer` pin → `trust_gate`; `register` through a `link_offer` pin → `proceed`; `reauth` with a session for that account → `proceed`. A `proceed` is the only decision after which a client may send prelogin, a claim, `authKey`, `recoveryAuthKey` or a TOTP code. |
| `recoverykit.json` | Grouping (11 groups). Parse tolerance: spaces and line breaks ignored, a dash kept as base64url (FI-B10). Group-confirm matching. Kit QR `andvari-kit:2` compose and parse (`card0Hash`, unpadded fields, rejection of padded or uppercase forms). |
| `encodings.json` | `emailNorm` (ASCII-only lowercasing; refusal of non-ASCII, control characters, length and `@` count). The `platform` enum. Proof-of-work bit order. `name` code-point refusals. The `origin` grammar, including refusals of IP literals, single-label hosts and plain http beyond localhost. `emailNorm` refusals after the strip: an interior space, DEL, and the 254-octet limit (VR-6). |
| `signup.json` (server-only, JVM) | Code MAC derivation. Proof-of-work acceptance at 0, 8 and 20 bits, with `pow.date` today, yesterday and older (FI-R4). Uniform responses. Ticket binding. |
| `logappend.json` (server-only, JVM, FC-1) | §3.7's access rules, as rows of stored chain, grant states, caller and posted frame → wire answer: the owner's `restore` of a deleted vault → `200`; a retried byte-identical `delete` → `200`; a retried `leave` whose own effect revoked the caller's grant, a decline included → `200`; a second, different `delete` → `400 precondition`; a member's `leave` in the grace period → `200`; a non-owner's `restore` while deleted → `403 no_grant`; a non-member's post → `403 no_grant`, with no head in the answer; after the purge, every non-genesis append → `403 no_grant`, the owner's `restore` included, while a byte-identical retry of a stored frame → `200`. |
| `accountstate.json` (rewritten, FI-R8) | The §5.4 merges: `contacts` (newest `verifiedAt`, tombstones, the key-change split, `compared` as OR); `vaults` (immutable `genesisHash`, the higher `seq`, a same-`seq` fork, no record passes); `accepted` (the higher `addSeq`; for the same `addSeq` `decline` wins whatever `at` says; between two records with the same decision the newest `at`; and a stale `addSeq` that answers nothing, FS-R6); `recovery` (the newest `at`, ties to `private`). The in-blob `seq` against the served one (FI-B14). The acceptance rows: an `accepted` record against the account's latest `addSeq` gives `accept`, `decline` or `ask` (VR-16). Graded by core and the web twin, and by the extension for the `vaults` rows and for `decision` alone (FN-1). |
| Deleted | §15's list. |

**Flow conformance (T2-B1).** `passwordgate.json` drives the core `PasswordGate` directly, on every target; the CLI uses the same core function. On top of it:
- each native app's UI tests (Android, iPhone, desktop) and the CLI's tests assert that a tapped `/d` link, or one scanned or pasted on "Scan an invite", starts no device link, and that the manifest and association files list `/l` only;
- the same tests assert that a recovery kit scanned or pasted on "Scan an invite" or on "Link from your other device" starts no recovery, and that no screen forwards a payload refused at one entry to another entry (each entry has its own scanner; the person must choose "Forgot your master password?" themselves);
- each app's tests assert that the comparison screen has no control that confirms without typed digits;
- the hostile-server harness serves a device link from an unpinned origin and asserts that **no** prelogin, claim or pickup reaches it before the Trust Gate is accepted, and none at all when the link did not come through "Link from your other device";
- the harness also re-serves a different `nonceE` after the reveal, and a second link reveal, and asserts that both cancel (T2-R-b).

## 18. Parts of the build, frozen interfaces, critical path, reviews

### 18.1 Interfaces frozen before the parts that use them start

The parts are those of §18.2.

| Interface | Frozen content | Done when |
|---|---|---|
| **IF-1 Formats** | §2–§6 and §8–§10 of this document, `encodings.json`, the decision table `passwordgate.json` (T2-B1), and `accountstate.json` (FI-R8) | the specification lands every vector file with its independent oracle, and the IF review signs off |
| **IF-2 Wire** | every route, body and error code above, written into spec 03, **for variant R on the public instance** (W1). Appendix A lists what variant K would change (FI-B11). | the spec review signs off |
| **IF-3 App API** | the list below | **The first deliverable of the core session and app API** (R2): an API-only commit with the Kotlin interfaces and data types in `core/app`, their Swift-facing signatures in `core/src/iosMain` (`IosSessionController`, `AndvariBridge`), and a **fake implementation** the Android, iPhone and desktop apps build against. Merged and reviewed before work on the Android, iPhone and desktop apps starts. |
| **IF-4 Public surface** | the `/l` and `/d` pages, the association files (listing `/l` only, T2-B1), the store-build constants (§0.3, including `PUBLIC_INSTANCE.kind`), the email templates (§11.3) | the public face has published the static site's `/l`, `/d` and association files on a test name under `andvaripass.com`, the templates are reviewed, and the build constants are in `spec/public-instance.json`, part of the specification (FI-L2) |

**IF-3 contents (R2):**

- **First run.** The three choices, and the sign-up-full and mode states.
- **Sign-in and the password rule.** The server choice; the password screen that names the account, instance and origin; the Trust Gate in its typed-address, device-link, link-offer sign-in and `kind`-change wordings (§4.4, §12.3).
- **Sign-up.** Email, code, ticket, age confirmation, proof-of-work progress, `mail_unavailable`.
- **Registration through each link kind.** Setup enrolment and its resume after an interruption (desktop and CLI; the phone apps show only the "open it on a computer" screen, §9.5).
- **Links.** Create (with shares), open, answer, revoke. The dead-end screens (§6.7).
- **Contacts.** The list, remove, the one-tap confirm on password-only devices, and compare codes, typed in both the remote and the in-person flows (§6.11).
- **Vault log actions.** Create, add, role, remove, leave, offer, cancel, accept, delete, restore.
- **Per-vault log states.** Verified, failed, withheld, frozen, connect-first, purged (only after a verified `delete`, §3.8). Copy-out from a frozen vault, and "Remove from this device" for a frozen or withheld one.
- **Card states.** Ok, fork stop, withheld, move prompt, `kind`-change prompt, "Update andvari" for the baked instance, Forget this server.
- **Account deletion with leaves.** The preview and the stale-head retry.
- **Recovery.** The recovery choice and changing it later, with the first-consent warning (FS-R2); the kit (reveal, confirm, rotate); kit recovery only from "Forgot your master password?", the screen naming the account and server, and the new kit forced after the commit (FS-B1).
- **Assisted recovery.** Request, verify, wait, commit (with its forced new kit), and the device-side list of requests (FI-B7).
- **Devices.** Device links in both roles, with their states (§8.2): the entry rule and the pin step (step 0), the "open Link from your other device" screen for a `/d` that arrives elsewhere, and the pin-mismatch sign-out (step 8). Your devices and revoke.
- **Change master password.**
- **Known devices and requireLink** (§8.7): the `deviceSecret` on every device-creating call, and the require-link route (FI-R10).
- **Quota and over-quota states.**
- **Incoming vaults.** Accept and decline (a decline signs a `leave`, below), each bound to the `add` it answers (FS-R6).
- **Contact prompts.** The key-change check and prompt (§6.8 step 6), and "New person from your other device" (§6.9).
- **Recovery prompts.** The veto prompt for an assisted-recovery request (§9.3); the unsigned-change prompt, defaulting to off (§4.5); and the own-mirror prompt on a device without its own mirror (§4.5).
- **Vault actions.** "Move everything to a new vault" (§3.13), with every member unticked by default on a frozen or failed vault (FS-R3), and "Remove from the list" for a member whose account the operator deleted (§3.11).
- **Instance switching.** Switching instance from a link (§6.7).
- **The first-launch wipe** (§12.4).
- **Operator views.** Desktop and `andvari-cli` only; never the web or the phone apps (§9.1).

### 18.2 Parts of the build (disjoint ownership; every file has one owner, R1)

| Part | Owns | Starts after | Size |
|---|---|---|---|
| Specification, vectors and documents | `spec/`, `spec/test-vectors/`, the oracle under `tools/vector-gen`, `docs/` (incl. `self-hosting.md` with the certificate paths of §9.5, N10), `README.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `CHANGELOG.md`. Also **`spec/public-instance.json`**, the one in-repo source of the store-build constants (§0.3), which every app's build reads; the public face supplies its values at the cut-over (FI-L2). | design review | M |
| Crypto and statement verifiers | `core/crypto/*`, **the pure verifiers the server shares** (`VaultLog.verify`, `InstanceCard.verify`, `AccountGenesis.verify`, the link and device-link formats, in a new `core/trust` package), the **crypto platform actuals** in every source set (`CryptoProvider.*`, `LazySodiumCryptoProvider`, `SodiumCryptoProvider`, `PlatformDigest.*`), their tests, and `web/src/crypto/*`: the primitive twins **and the TypeScript twins of the pure verifiers** in `web/src/crypto/trust/*` (vault log, card, genesis), graded by the same vectors (FI-L4) | IF-1 | M |
| Core engine | `core/client/*` except `session/`: vault log state and sync, contacts, link and device-link flows, the password gate (§4.4), account state, `Wire` in `core/model/*`. Also the **client platform actuals** (`SqlBox.*`, `DeviceState.ios`, `FileDeviceState`, `UriParts.*`, `IdnaNfc*`, `IosClocks`) and their tests. **Its first commit** is the new `Wire` DTOs and the §15 deletions in `core/model` and `core/client`, `EnrollLink` included. The server and the apps compile against those files, so that commit is a stated predecessor of the server and of the Android, iPhone and desktop apps (FI-L1). | IF-1, IF-2, the verifiers' APIs | **XL (critical path)** |
| Core session and app API | `core/client/session/*`, `core/app/*`, and the **iOS bridge** in `core/src/iosMain` (`AndvariBridge`, `IosSessionController`, `IosStorage`, `IosQuickUnlockKeys`, `SimulatorQuickUnlockKey.*`) and their tests | IF-1, IF-2. Its IF-3 API-only commit comes first. | **L (critical path)** |
| Server | `server/*`, `deploy/` (`andvari setup`, `andvari admin`, removal of `bringup.sh`), the `Dockerfile`, `tools/recovery-cli` (`assist`, `rotate`) | IF-1, IF-2, **the shared verifiers** (the server runs `VaultLog.verify`, T-R2; N5), and **the core engine's first commit** (the `Wire` DTOs it compiles against, and the `EnrollLink` deletion, FI-L1) | **XL** |
| Web app | everything in `web/` except `web/src/crypto/*` (the verifiers), `web/src/e2e/` (tests and CI) and `web/src/extension-pins.test.ts` (the extension). That includes `web/src/{ui,vault,api,enroll,export,import}`, `main.tsx`, `web/public/` and `policy-clamps.test.ts`; no part changes the clamp values it pins. **The web's vault-log handling, §3.6 adoption and §5.4 account-state merge live in `web/src/vault/*`**, built on the TypeScript verifiers and graded by `wrapad.json` and `accountstate.json` (FI-L4). | IF-2, the verifiers' web twin | M |
| Android app | `app-android/*`: scanner, app links for `andvaripass.com/l` only, **the new applicationId**, first run, links, linking, kit, password change, devices, the §12.4 wipe | IF-3, and the core engine's first commit (FI-L1) | L |
| iPhone app | `app-ios/*` (Swift app and extensions, `project.yml` associated domains), excluding `app-ios/uitest/` scripts | IF-3, and the core engine's first commit (FI-L1) | **L** |
| Desktop app | `app-desktop/*` | IF-3, and the core engine's first commit (FI-L1) | M |
| Extension | `extension/*`, and `web/src/extension-pins.test.ts` (it pins extension source) | **IF-1** and IF-2 | **M** |
| CLI | `tools/andvari-cli` (new): enrol, links, vaults, share, `restore` (spec 07 §3) incl. `--vaults personal`, link-device, **`card sign`** and **`card show`** (§14.1), **`operator enrol`** (§9.5) and **`operator …`** (every §9.2 route, N9) (FI-L5), `--origin/--connect` (R11), demo `seed` and `check`. Also `tools/backup-cli` (shares the `Backup` reader), and the one-line module entry in `settings.gradle.kts`. | the core engine's and the core session's APIs | M |
| **Tests, CI and the hostile-server harness** (new) | `scripts/`, `.github/workflows/`, `tools/uitest-seed` (rewritten or replaced by `andvari-cli`), `web/src/e2e/`, `app-ios/uitest/` scripts. The **hostile-server harness**: an interposing proxy in front of the real server that withholds, replays, forks logs and cards, swaps envelopes and races link answers and device claims. It drives the core's JVM client headless and uses the mail sink and the test clock (§7.1). Android UI tests reach the test server through `adb reverse`, because only `localhost` may be plain http (N10). | the server's test mode; the core's JVM client; the CLI for seeding | **L** |
| Public face and operations | outside this repo: the static site at `andvaripass.com` (website, scriptless `/l` and `/d`, association files listing `/l` only, with the new Android package and the iOS team and bundle ids, privacy, terms, support, donate); the `support@` mailbox and its send-only scope; DNS (SPF, DKIM, DMARC); the move to `vault.andvaripass.com`; the off-site backup drill; the demo timer on the instance host | IF-4 | M |

### 18.3 Critical path

1. Design review.
2. IF-1 and IF-2 frozen, with vectors.
3. The crypto and statement verifiers, and the IF-3 commit of the core session and app API.
4. The core engine and the core session's implementation; the Android, iPhone and desktop apps build against the IF-3 fake meanwhile.
5. Integration of the UIs on the real core.
6. The hostile-server harness and e2e green.
7. Review rounds.
8. The cut-over drill, then the cut-over.
9. Store submissions.

The server is built alongside the core once IF-2 is frozen, the verifiers exist (N5) and the core engine's first commit has landed (FI-L1); the Android, iPhone and desktop apps also wait for that commit. The CLI starts once the core's APIs exist, and must be finished before the cut-over (`restore`). The public face needs only IF-4. Tests, CI and the harness start with the server's test mode.

### 18.4 Reviews

1. Two adversarial reviewers on this document before any code.
2. A review of IF-1 to IF-4 at freeze.
3. Per part of the build, an adversarial round on its first complete slice. The verifiers, the core engine, the core session and app API and the server get two rounds.
4. A whole-diff security scan aimed at trust laundering: any server-supplied value that becomes a pin, a key, an owner or an origin.
5. The **hostile-server harness** (§18.2). Every R20–R23 behaviour gets an assertion.
6. The cut-over drill on a scratch server.
7. Store review notes, with demo accounts on the public instance created and reset on the instance host (§9.5).

**Answered by the owner on 2026-11-14:**
- **Q1, the domain:** `andvaripass.com`. The public instance's display name stays "andvari".
- **Q2, one origin or two:** apart. The website and join pages are at `https://andvaripass.com`; the instance is at `https://vault.andvaripass.com`.
- **The support address:** `support@andvaripass.com`, also the sender (§11.1). The donations target is still open (question 6).

**Working assumptions the owner confirmed on 2026-11-15** (§0.1, §0.6):
- **W1, variant R on the public instance** (§7.7). IF-2 is written for it; Appendix A is the bounded delta for K.
- **W2, question 4 answered "yes"**: the operator may delete an account disabled for at least 30 days, with an email (§9.2).

**Still open:**

3. **Per-account quota numbers** (§7.4 defaults). Demo accounts count against the cap (§9.5).
4. **Operator deletion of disabled accounts** (§9.2): W2, "yes, after 30 days, with an email", is confirmed (§0.6). Still open: whether inactive accounts are ever purged.
5. **The one-tap contacts list on password-only devices** (§8.5, R23): accept it, or require reconnecting with each person?
6. **The donations target.**
7. **Terms and privacy text**, drafted as part of the public face (§18.2) for the owner's approval.
8. **Changes not sent when a server's policy forbids the offline copy**: settled on 2026-11-17 (§0.6, §4.4): the client tries once to send what waits, then wipes as specified, and says afterwards how many could not be sent.
9. **A policy's wipe while the server cannot be reached** (§4.4, "On a device"): settled on 2026-11-19 (§0.6). The rule of 2026-11-17 made the one try at the first unlock after a device learned of a forbidding policy, also with the server out of reach, and then wiped the copy with everything that waited. The owner decided on 2026-11-19 that while the server cannot be reached there is no unlock and no wipe, and that the one try and the wipe come at the first unlock the server answers; the account's keys leave the device under such a policy so that there is no unlock without the server, and a device kept offline keeps a copy that nothing on it can open.

**Known remainders, not solved:**

- **A setup link whose registration is refused at the last moment** (2026-11-17, §7.3). On a computer, when an account comes to be kept on the device while the operator's first registration through the setup link is under way, the registration can stand at the server and its session be refused: the server then holds an operator account with no first card, and the setup link is spent. Signing in needs a pin, no pin can be made without a card, and finishing the setup needs a session, so nothing on any device can finish it. It is a race only. What would finish it (rule of 2026-11-17): a sign-in that the password gate takes at a server that has no card yet, for exactly the account this device made, then the setup's last step; that changes the password gate for one narrow case, reached only by a race on a computer, and is not built.
- **A request for the operator's help typed with an address the account no longer has** (accepted on 2026-11-17, §9.3) stops as "another account is kept": nothing is committed, but the reason it gives is not the exact one.

**Proposed for after the release, not built now** (each adds a route, a setting or a button beyond this plan; 2026-11-19):

- **A sign-out of every other device without a password change**: a new route and a button in each client. Today only a password change (spec 03 §3) and an operator's disable end every other session.
- **A limit on how old a session's chain may grow**: a new policy setting; the price is a sign-in with the password when it is reached. Today each refresh earns the policy's days again, with no limit on the chain's age (spec 03 §2).
- **A sign-out that names its own refresh token to the server**, which then ends that session whether the token was still good or already used, without recording a theft. It would let a sign-out be confirmed in nearly every case where the network is back, in every client, where today a sign-out after a renewal that got no answer cannot be confirmed (spec 03 §2).
- New keys at a password change, which the threat model already names: today a password change wraps the same account key again, so a wrap fetched earlier still opens the account to whoever guesses the old password (spec 05 R33).

**Settled on 2026-11-16:** the Android signing key for the new application id is a new key, the same for every channel (§14.1 step 8, §12.1, §0.6).

---

## Appendix A. Variant K on the public instance: the bounded delta (FI-B11)

IF-2 is frozen for variant R on the public instance (W1, confirmed by the owner on 2026-11-15, §0.6). Should the owner ever choose K instead, this list is the whole change. Private instances already carry every item, so K adds no new mechanism, only switches.

| Area | Change for `ANDVARI_INSTANCE_KIND = public` under K |
|---|---|
| Setup and card | `andvari setup --kind public` offers step 4, the recovery-key ceremony (§9.5). Card 0 may carry a `recoveryFp`. `PUT /instance/card` stops refusing a public card with a `recoveryFp` (that `400 bad_card` case goes). |
| Routes that stop answering `404` | `POST /recovery/requests`, `/verify`, `/veto`, `/poll`, `/commit` (§9.3); `GET /account/recovery-requests`; `GET /recovery-pubkey`; `GET /admin/recovery-requests`; `POST /admin/accounts/{id}/escrow` †; `POST /admin/recovery-requests/{id}/fulfil` †; `PUT /escrow/self`; `PUT /instance/rotation` †. |
| Refusal codes | Register and `PUT /account/recovery` accept `posture: operator`; `400 operator_recovery_unavailable` is then returned only when `recoveryFp = none`. Nothing else changes. |
| Client rule | Store builds drop "`kind = public` means no escrow" (§4.5, §7.7). That needs a client release, which is the point of the rule. Every other §4.5 rule applies: first consent, own mirror, signed rotations, the unsigned-change prompt. |
| Screens on the public instance | The recovery choice at sign-up with the public copy and its warning (§7.7); the choice in Settings; "Ask the operator for help" with its request, verify, wait and commit screens (§9.3); the veto prompt; the own-mirror and unsigned-change prompts (§4.5); in the operator views, recovery requests, escrow download and fulfil. All of them exist for private instances. |
| Copy | The help page "I lost my master password and my kit" offers the operator path instead of "export and start again". |
| Tests | The assisted-recovery e2e (hold, veto, code check, the verify cap) runs on a public test instance too. The R assertions on the public instance (the `404`s, the card refusal, the client rule) are deleted. `recovery.json` is unchanged. |
| Operator's work and residuals | The offline sheet, drills, identity checks with strangers, legal demands (§7.7). R3 and R27 apply to the public instance, with everything in "What remains under K", including a hostile server faking the hold, the veto and the code (FS-R5). |
